PREFACE 

A knowledge of accounting is essential to all who wish to understand our modern economic system. Its usefulness is not restricted to practicing accountants, but extends to responsible men engaged in all fields of business and public activity. The growing complexity of business and economic problems has made an appreciation of accounting more necessary than ever before. 

The study of accounting should enable college and university students to understand better not only our modern economic society but also their relation to that society. 

This edition of Accounting Principles is definitely planned to serve four groups: (1) those who expect to continue the study of accounting and to practice it as public or private accountants; (2) those who expect to enter business activity and who wish a better understanding of accounting as a tool of business; (3) those who are interested in economics and who believe that a sounder understanding is available through a study of accounting; and (4) those who anticipate entering the professions and expect to use accounting in their professional practice. It is not expected that one course in accounting will give a complete training for the practice of accountancy. Rather, it is expected that a study of this book will give students a broad appreciation of the services that accounting is equipped to render to the business and to the economic world.

A relatively small percentage of college and university students go beyond first-year accounting. It is important that no significant part of accounting be omitted from their consideration. 

It is also important that the services of accounting to business and society be understood by them. It is hoped that the use of this book will help make such students more intelligent members of our social order.

CHAPTER I  

Property and Business The use of property is essential to the 

Operations conduct of business activities. A place for the business must be provided in a building that is owned or rented; equipment adapted to the activities of the business must be owned or leased; if goods are sold, they must be purchased and kept in stock prior to sale; if services are rendered, the equipment and supplies used in rendering the services must be available for use. Wherever there is business, property is found. 

Business operations not only require the use of property but also provide, through the sale of commodities or services, funds that may be used to purchase additional property as the business expands. If a business is conducted in such a way that its property increases, the business is said to be successful. In contrast, if its operations result in a decrease of its property, the business is considered to be unsuccessful. 

An automobile-manufacturing business that grew from a oneman shop in the back lot of a Detroit residence to a huge corporation employing hundreds of thousands of people not only in this country but also in other nations of the world, is an example of successful business operations and the accompanying increase in property. A five-and-ten-cent business that grew from a small store in a small town in Pennsylvania to a world-wide enterprise with departmentalized stores in every large city, is another instance of expansion in business and property. Most businesses with large investments in property at the present time began years ago as small enterprises with but little property. 

The property of a business is used in carrying on business activities with the purpose of increasing the amount of the prop erty. Ordinarily this is accomplished by the production of commodities or services that satisfy the wants of others. As these products or services are sold, the business purchases other products or services that it may use in the production of still more products 1

[Ch. 1 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

and services for sale. This cycle of purchasing and selling products and services by business enterprises constitutes the institution known as business.

Property and Property The property owned by a business and Rights used in the operation of that business is called assets. The right or interest of the proprietor in the assets of the business is called his proprietorship, capital, or net worth. 

If there are no claims against the assets, other than that of the proprietor, the relationship of the proprietor to the assets is expressed by the equation:

ASSETS 

PROPRIETORSHIP 

If Henry Norris purchases a retail store for $5,000, his financial condition is expressed by the equation: 

ASSETS $5,000.00 = PROPRIETORSHIP $5,000.00

The accounting value of the assets acquired is determined by the purchase price agreed upon by Henry Norris and the seller. 

The assets are therefore valued at $5,000. 

Many businessmen find that, in order to conduct their businesses satisfactorily, they need property in addition to that provided by their own proprietorship. They can obtain the use of additional property in either of two ways: (1) by borrowing money from others and then using this money to purchase property or (2) by purchasing property on account, that is, procuring the property by giving a promise to pay at some future date. 

Those from whom businessmen borrow or purchase on account are known as creditors. The creditors of a business have a claim on the property of that business until the proprietor pays them in accordance with their agreement. The rights of the creditors in the assets of a business are known as the liabilities of the business.

For example, if Henry Norris borrows $1,000 from a local bank to use in the operation of his retail store, the bank becomes a creditor of Norris. The increase in Norris' assets, represented by the $1,000 in cash received, is accompanied by a corresponding claim on the assets, which the bank will possess until the $1,000 that Norris borrowed is paid. If Norris purchases merchandise from the Cole Mercantile Company and promises to pay for it in

Ch. 1j 

thirty days, this company also becomes a creditor of Norris. If he fails to pay, the company can enforce its claim by legal action. 

The claim of the company on the assets of Norris is a liability of Norris. Virtually every business has some liabilities, for, even though the proprietor may have sufficient assets to operate his business, he usually makes some purchases on account as a matter of convenience.

It is evident that assets are subject to two kinds of claims: those arising from the rights of creditors and those arising from the rights of the proprietor. The sum of these rights is therefore equal to the value of the assets. This fact is expressed by the equation: 

ASSETS = LIABILITIES + PROPRIETORSHIP 

The amount of the proprietorship can be ascertained at any time by subtracting the liabilities from the assets. 

If Henry Norris is using $6,000 worth of assets in operating his store and has liabilities of $500, his proprietorship is $5,500. 

This condition is shown in equation form as follows: 

ASSETS = LIABILITIES + PROPRIETORSHIP 

$6,000.00 $500.00 $5,500.00

The relationship between assets, and liabilities and proprietorship is a fundamental one. All property is subject to property rights. Property and property rights are inseparable from each other. Similarly, the amount of property is exactly equal to the amount of the property rights. It should be noted that the amounts on the two sides of the accounting equation must always balance.

Changes in Property as a Result of Business 

Operations The operations of a business require the frequent buying and selling of commodities and services. The transactions completed in this buying and selling process produce changes in both the nature and the amount of the property used in the operation of the business.

For example, John Hardy has $6,000 in cash that he decides to use in the operation of a grocery store. His assets and proprietorship are shown by the equation: 

CASH $6,000.00 PROPRIETORSHIP $6,000.00

[Ch. 1 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

He pays $3,500 for merchandise and $900 for store counters. 

These purchases decrease his asset cash $4,400 but give him two new assets, merchandise and store equipment. The equation that shows the assets and the rights to the assets is: 

ASSETS 

Cash.... Merchandise..

Store Equipment. 

PROPRIETORSHIP 

$6,000.00 $1,600.00 John Hardy... 3,500.00 900.00 $6,000.00 $6,000.00

The nature of Hardy's property has changed as a result of his purchases, for now he has only $1,600 in cash, but he has merchandise and store equipment valued at $4,400. 

The two transactions given above change the nature of the assets but do not change the total value of the assets. Transactions representing operating expenses (illustrated below) change both the nature and the amount of the assets and the proprietorship. 

Changes in Rights to Business operations produce changes in the Property as a Result of rights to property as well as in the property 

Business Operations itself. When the total assets of a business change in amount, a change of the same amount necessarily results in the total of the rights to the assets. Moreover, the ratio between the rights of the proprietor and the rights of his creditors varies from time to time. 

When Hardy, whose financial condition has just been illustrated, purchases $1,500 worth of merchandise from Carl Young and promises to pay for it later, he increases the total value of his assets $1,500 by the acquisition of the merchandise. At the same time he grants a claim against his assets, for Carl Young now has a right to the amount of $1,500 in these assets. Consequently, a liability of $1,500 is created by this purchase. These facts are shown by the following equation:

ASSETS 

LIABILITIES 

PROPRIETORSHIP 

Cash.. $1,600.00 Carl Young.... $1,500.00 John Hardy .. $6,000.00 

Merchandise.. 5,000.00 

Store Equip... 900.00 $7,500.00= $1,500.00+ $6,000.00

As he would use virtually all his cash if he paid Young for the total amount of the purchase, Hardy decides to pay $500 in

Ch. 1] 

cash on account. The following equation shows this $500 decrease in the assets and the liabilities:

ASSETS LIABILITIES 

PROPRIETORSHIP 

Cash. $1,100.00 Carl Young.... $1,000.00 John Hardy... $6,000.00 

Merchandise.. 5,000.00 

Store Equip... 900.00 $7,000.00= $1,000.00+ $6,000.00

Some of the effects of business operations upon assets and liabilities have been indicated. The primary objective of the proprietor of a business is to increase the assets of his business and his interest in the assets by earning a profit. 

In operating his business, Hardy will be selling merchandise continually. A successful enterpriser keeps the expenses of operating his business at an amount that is less than the difference between the sales price and the cost of the merchandise. For instance, Hardy sells for $5,600 the merchandise that cost him $5,000. If his operating expenses are less than $600, which is the margin on sales, he has earned a profit. If his expenses exceed $600, he has incurred a loss.

If Hardy sells for $5,600 the merchandise that cost him $5,000 and has operating expenses amounting to $400, he has realized a profit of $200. This profit is shown in the equation by an increase of $200 in his assets and an increase of the same amount in his proprietorship. His liabilities are not affected by these transactions.

After these transactions have been completed, Hardy no longer has merchandise valued at $5,000. Instead, he has additional cash of $5,200, the amount he received for the merchandise ($5,600) less his operating expenses ($400). The total value of his property is now $7,200, which represents an increase of $200. 

Since the amount of his liability remains $1,000, his proprietorship is represented by the difference between the assets and the liabilities, or $6,200, which is an increase of $200. The equation is now as follows: 

+ PROPRIETORSHIP 

$1,000.00 John Hardy... $6,200.00

Store Equip... $6,300.00 Carl Young.. 900.00 $7,200.00= $1,000.00+ $6,200.00

[Ch. 1 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

In the preceding example, if Hardy had been unable to keep his operating expenses low enough to be absorbed by the margin between the cost of the merchandise and the sales price, he would have incurred a loss. For instance, if Hardy's operating costs in selling the merchandise had been $800, both his assets and his proprietorship would have been reduced $200. In other words, the expenses of operating the business would have exceeded the $600 margin. Hardy's cash would have been $1,100- $800 + $5,600, or $5,900, or an increase of $4,800. His merchandise, however, would have decreased $5,000. Since his proprietorship and his liabilities would have equaled his total assets, his financial condition, expressed in equation form, would have been as follows: 

PROPRIETORSHIP ASSETS 

Cash. 

Store Equip... 

LIABILITIES 

$5,900.00 Carl Young.... $1,000.00 John Hardy... $5,800.00 900.00 $6,800.00= $1,000.00+ $5,800.00

It can be seen that, if a businessman is to make a profit, he must keep the operating expenses of his business down to an amount that is lower than the margin between the cost of the merchandise and the sales price. 

The operating expenses of a business may not be merely those which must be paid in cash, such as salaries, insurance, and advertising. The value of some of the assets used in the operation of a business decreases as the assets are used or consumed. For example, the furniture and fixtures that are used in the operation of a business will gradually wear out and thus decrease in value. 

Ultimately, these assets will be in such a condition that they can no longer be used in the operation of the business. They must then be disposed of, and new assets must be acquired. 

There are other assets, such as office supplies, that are consumed continually in operating activities. By their use the value of these assets is decreased just as cash is decreased by the payment of salaries. Such decreases must be considered in determining the value of the assets and the amount of the proprietorship. 

Need for Business

Records 

If the proprietor is to conduct his business so that his proprietorship will be increased, he must know the effect of his business transactions on his assets, liabilities, and proprietorship so that he can make wise decisions

Ch. 1] 

in regard to future operations. For example, the retail or wholesale merchant seeks to increase his proprietorship through the purchase and sale of merchandise. A record of past transactions, that will show the favorable or unfavorable effects of those transactions, will help him to make better decisions regarding transactions in the future. He must know the prices he has paid and the prices he has charged in the past in order to determine the goods to be bought and to be sold in the future. To obtain the maximum increase in proprietorship, he must make use of his previous experience as shown on his records. 

If he is considering the desirability of employing additional sales clerks, he should consider the results obtained from the employment of his present clerks and should try to judge from this experience what probable results will be obtained from employing additional clerks. If he is considering the purchase of additional equipment or additional store space, he should give careful attention to the results obtained from the present equipment and space. 

If he thinks the additional clerks and store facilities will increase sales, and hence his proprietorship, he may decide to procure them. 

If the proprietor is contemplating the purchase of additional property by increasing his liabilities, he must consider (1) whether his past experience indicates that this property can be used profitably, that is, whether it can be used so that it will result in an increase in proprietorship, and (2) whether he will be able to pay his creditors in accordance with the terms under which he can obtain the property from them. Creditors will usually accept only cash in payment of their claims. If the proprietor invests too much of his property in land, buildings, equipment, and other assets of a similar nature, he may be unable to obtain the cash needed to pay his creditors at the proper time. He must have information that will show him the amount of his liabilities and the time when the liabilities must be paid. He must also have information that will show him the nature of his assets; otherwise he may be unable to meet his obligations.

In judging the amount of materials and services that he should purchase and the price that he can afford to pay, the manufacturer, who produces commodities for sale, needs information of various kinds with reference to his past experience. In determining what should be done in the future, the railroad executive who is operating freight and passenger trains needs information re-

[Ch. 1 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

garding the volume of freight and of passenger traffic, the expenses of operating various trains, and the relative costs of different operating policies. Decisions should be based, as far as possible, on facts; and business records should, as far as possible, supply those facts.

These illustrations serve to show that the efficient manager of a business is constantly seeking information concerning the effect of his past operations. He then uses this information in planning his future operations so that these operations will produce an increase in his proprietorship. It has been found increasingly useful to plan future operations. Such plans are known as budgets. Business budgets, based in part on records of the past, are useful tools for executives.

In a very small business the proprietor may be able to remember all essential information; but as soon as a business becomes of appreciable size, this is impossible. The proprietor must then make a record of the facts he desires for future use. Experience has shown that it is usually desirable to make this record complete and to show in it the effect of every transaction on the assets, the liabilities, and the proprietorship.

Purpose of Accounting The primary purpose of accounting is to give the proprietor or the manager of a business information concerning not only the nature and the amounts of the assets, the liabilities, and the proprietorship, but also the effect of the operations of the business on these items. 

Stated in another way, the object of accounting is to provide information concerning property and the rights to property, and to show how property and the rights to it have been affected by business operations.

It is the function of accounting to record, to analyze and classify, and to summarize the activities and the effects of these activities for each business enterprise.

Recording reduces to writing the activities of a business. In recording, only the essential activities should be included and their inclusion should be accomplished in the most effective manner. Accounting gives consideration to that manner of recording which involves the least labor. 

Analysis and classification suggest orderly procedure. Accounting seeks to arrange, under predetermined classes, the mass

Ch. 

of events that occur daily in business. In this way the records are more easily interpreted and understood. Furthermore, the records are justified only so far as they are useful to those who have need to use them. Classification, then, should be based on the experience of those executives who, throughout the years, have had occasion to determine action or base policies on such information.

The masses of data, classified as mentioned above, become more useful when summarized. It is not the single business act, but the sum of all the operations of a day, a week, a month, or a year that has the greatest significance. It is the total of many transactions, properly classified, that has the utility desired. So accounting summarizes at intervals the operations and the effects of these operations on property and on rights to property. 

Some summaries must be made frequently; others, only occasionally. For example, it may be desirable to know daily the summary of transactions affecting cash; while it may be useful to know only annually the summary of transactions affecting buildings. 

Accounting Statements The principal accounting summaries are two: (1) the first lists the kinds and amounts of property, together with the ownership rights in this property; (2) the second reports on the effects of the operations of the owners or their agents on the owners' equity or right in this property. The first of these summaries is known as the balance sheet; it shows the assets of the business, together with the rights of the creditors and of the proprietor in the assets. The second summary is known as the profit and loss statement; it lists the income of the period, together with the costs of operations for the period resulting in an increase or a decrease in proprietorship. 

The balance sheet gives a picture of financial condition at a given moment; the profit and loss statement covers the period between two balance sheets.

From an accounting point of view, the life of any business enterprise is a succession of balance sheets, usually prepared annually and tied together by profit and loss statements, each covering one year. If the life of a business enterprise were represented by a line starting at point X and moving toward the right, accounting statements would appear as in the illustration at the top of page 10.

There are persons other than the proprietor who are interested in the operations of the business and who are entitled to information about these operations. If the proprietor wishes to borrow money from a bank, the officers of the bank may require him to give information that will enable them to judge his ability to repay the loan. This information will probably include the amounts and the nature of his assets and liabilities, the amounts of the profits earned in previous years, and the way in which these profits were earned. The officers of the bank will need to know the amounts and the nature of the assets and the liabilities so that they can judge how rapidly the assets of the business will be turned into cash and whether the proprietor will be able to take care of all liabilities as they mature. They will also wish to know how much the proprietorship has increased in previous years, for obviously they will be more willing to lend money to a business that has been earning profits regularly than to one that has been incurring losses. Finally, they will wish a detailed explanation of how the profits were earned. If the profits were earned in the regular operation of the business, a much better condition might be indicated than if they were unusual gains resulting from transactions that might not occur again. 

Creditors may also request similar information before they sell merchandise on account to a business. The Government may require a statement showing the income of the business so that the income tax can be ascertained and assessed. 

Obviously, in controlling his business, the proprietor needs all the information that might be desired by anyone else. Hence accounting records that meet the needs of the proprietor will ordinarily meet the needs of all others. The proprietor, however, frequently needs information about many details in which outsiders are not interested. Statistics showing the amounts of different expenses, the cost of selling certain kinds of merchandise, the

Ch. 1] 

ratio of returned goods to goods sold, and many similar types of information are important to the proprietor but are ordinarily not needed by others. In the following chapters attention will be given primarily to the needs of the proprietor, but the needs of others will not be ignored.

Transactions and 

Business transactions are the raw materials 

Proprietorship of accounting out of which are prepared the statements described in preceding paragraphs. Transactions are also evidence of business activity and life. When business is dull or slow, the number of transactions has decreased; when business is keen and active, the number of transactions has increased. Without transactions there would be no accounting; there would be no business.

The business transaction sets the accounting value. This value is the price agreed upon between the buyer and the seller in the transaction; it measures the amount shown on the accounting record. For example, if a building is purchased by a businessman for $50,000, that amount is used in the accounting records. The seller may have held the building at $60,000 up to the time of sale, the buyer may have planned to pay $40,000 for it, the building may have been assessed at $35,000, and another buyer may have offered the purchaser $75,000 for it the day after he bought it. These values have no effect on the accounting records. The transaction price, $50,000, sets the value of the building on the balance sheet of the purchaser: If the purchaser should decide that it is better for him to sell the building at $75,000 to the new buyer than to use it himself, then there is a new transaction that sets a new accounting value for the new buyer. The building may appear on the balance sheet of the new buyer at $75,000 because that was the price he paid for the asset. 

It is only by recording the prices of the actual transactions of the owner that accounting records can have satisfactory stability. They then become a true record of his transactions and the effect of his transactions on his proprietorship. 

Records for Noncom- Records are often needed by those not mercial Enterprises engaged in business operations in the technical sense. For example, records are used by professional men and by those who have retired from active participation in business. The physician, the lawyer, and the accountant must

[Ch. 1 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

maintain records to show at least (1) the amounts owed them by their clients so that they can take the necessary steps to procure collection and (2) the amount of income earned so that they can make their income-tax returns to the Government. 

They may keep a complete record of their operations for use in making future plans. Governmental units, such as cities and states, need information concerning past operations in planning their future activities. The same is true of lodges, clubs, and churches. Every individual or organization that uses property in the conduct of operations should maintain a record of this property and the effect of operations on the property. Emphasis will be placed primarily on the records needed by those carrying on commercial operations for profit, but much of this discussion will be applicable to the needs of all those requiring the services of accounting records.

Specialized Accounting The accounting that has been referred to Fields in previous pages is sometimes called "general" accounting. It has to do with the recording of transactions for a business or other economic unit and the periodic preparation of statements from these records. It provides accounting information for management, for creditors, for the government, and for any others who have use for such information. 

In addition to "general" accounting, a number of specialized accounting fields have developed, especially during the past fifty years. Among these specialized fields attention may be given briefly to the following:

Auditing and investigations represent a field of accounting activity, which reviews independently the "general" accounting.

Auditing is usually done by an independent accountant who examines records and reports, and issues a statement of opinion regarding their accuracy, together with a report containing confidential advice to the management. 

Cost accounting emphasizes accounting for costs, particularly manufacturing costs. It stresses costs of products and processes rather than the proprietorship of the enterprise as a whole. 

Income-tax accounting has been of great importance since the adoption of the Sixteenth Amendment to the Constitution in 1913.

It involves a knowledge of the latest decisions of income-tax authorities and seeks to determine the correct amount of tax liability based on income.

Ch. 1] 

Accounting systems stress the construction of accounting records for any particular enterprise or group of similar enterprises. 

It seeks to make more efficient the mechanisms of recording and reporting in accounting for a specific business. 

Budgetary accounting presents in account form the transactions planned for the coming period and summarizes these transactions in accounting statements. It deals with contemplated rather than actual transactions.

Governmental accounting specializes in the transactions of political units, such as states and municipalities. It seeks to provide useful accounting information on the business aspect of public administration.

Relation of Economics to Accounting 

Economics and accounting treat of similar subject matter. Both are concerned with property and with human activities relating to the production, distribution, and consumption of this property. It is the point of view that differentiates one from the other. Economics treats of wealth in its relation to society, or the mass of owners. Accounting considers the interest of each owner or group of owners in this same wealth and the way in which each owner or group manages property. Economics considers the value of this property as a whole as belonging to society; accounting attempts to measure the interest of each individual in the same property. 

The economic organization of our present system of society is based quite largely on the private ownership of property. The property used in business operations is owned by individuals or groups of individuals who carry on business activities for their own gain.

Because of the individual point of view that characterizes accounting, the amount that one person owes and that he considers as a liability is considered to be an asset of the same amount in the records of the person to whom the debt is owed. Such amounts may be designated as payables and receivables respectively. All the payables in society as a whole must exactly equal the receivables, for whatever is payable by one is receivable in like amount by another. From the point of view of economics and society, receivables are not wealth for they are offset by a like amount of payables. From the point of view of accounting, however, the receivable of one business is an asset of that business,

[Ch. 1 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

or property to be realized in cash, and therefore constitutes wealth in the calculation of the proprietorship of that business. 

Economics treats of society as a whole, of wealth in general, of the wants of people, and the satisfaction of those wants by the same people. Accounting sets up individual units, the property of each unit, the requirements of the units, and the productive accomplishments of them. Economics is general; accounting is specific. 

Relation of Law, Engi neering, and Public

Administration to Accounting 

While accounting is most closely related to economics, it has many points in common with law, with engineering, and with public administration. Whenever any of these fields have business relationships or operate in the field of business transactions, accounting is involved.

In corporations, whether business or nonbusiness, law and accounting are closely related. Corporation law and corporation accounting are essential studies of this dominant organization in business activity. It is difficult to have a satisfactory understanding of the corporation and its place in modern economic society without an appreciation of corporation accounting. Accounting and law both treat of property and property rights. 

Engineering involves costs of construction and costs of operations. These costs arise in business transactions. Engineering is therefore related to general accounting and in particular to cost accounting. Engineering projects are carried out by many and varied business transactions, which need to be accounted for currently. 

Public administration, like business administration, is concerned with transactions and their effect on properties and property rights. The properties of cities and the operations of city managers must be accounted for if the quality of the city administration is to be known. Accounting can measure public administration as well as business administration. 

The Profession of Accountancy 

Just as a man may devote his life to law or to medicine, so he may devote it to accounting. When he makes his life work the improvement of accounting service, he is truly a professional man. Such identification of human interest with accounting is known as the profession of accountancy.

Ch. 1] 

If the accountant serves as chief accountant or controller of a going business, he is sometimes said to be engaged in private accounting. His services are in this case rendered entirely to one business unit except insofar as he may belong to associations of accountants or controllers, which seek to improve the practice of accountancy. A number of national organizations of private accountants seek to raise the level of accounting service, through periodic meetings and publications. 

When an accountant sets up his own office and accepts clients, he is said to be engaged in public accounting. Because of the importance of the work performed by the public accountant, and because of the confidential relation he bears to his clients, it has long been regarded desirable to have some means by which the business public can judge his ability. To accomplish this, laws providing for the examination and the certification of public accountants have been passed in all the states of the Union. In each state there is a board of examiners, which commonly gives a public examination twice a year to those who comply with the requirements mentioned below. Those who make a satisfactory grade on these examinations, and who have met specified requirements in relation to experience, are granted a certificate that gives them the right to the title Certified Public Accountant. The accountant usually indicates this title by writing after his name the letters C. P. A. The state laws prohibit any one from calling himself a certified public accountant unless he has passed the examination and has been in public accounting practice for the period of time prescribed by the state.

The qualifications required for the C. P. A. certificate differ in the various states. In most states the applicant must be a highschool graduate or have equivalent education, and must have had three years' experience in accounting work. In New York, the leading C. P. A. state, the applicant must be a graduate of a collegiate school of business in addition to having the experience requirement. The nature of the experience differs among the states, some requiring that it all be acquired in the office of a public accountant, others accepting accounting work in a private business in partial fulfillment. The requirements vary to such an extent that only their general nature can be stated. Reference must be made to the laws of each particular state for details.

The City of Beverly began the fiscal year with cash, $700,000; buildings, $500,000; streets, $2,700,000; and land, $1,100,000. 

It owed on bonds outstanding, $2,500,000.

During the year it completed the following transactions: (a) Collected from taxes, $405,000 in cash; from other sources, $225,000. (b) Paid salaries of city employees and fire, park, and other operating expenses, $635,000. (c) Sold bonds for $900,000 in cash. (This amount is listed not only as an increase in cash but also as a liability because the city will have to pay back the value of the bonds to the bondholders at the maturity date.) (d) Built a new city hall at a cost of $500,000. (e) Bought land for park purposes, $200,000. (f) Donated $100,000 for the purchase of a university site in an adjoining area.

CHAPTER II  

Purpose of the Balance For the intelligent conduct of business Sheet activity, the proprietor must know the financial condition of his enterprise from time to time and the changes in the financial condition that take place over a period of time. It would be virtually impossible for him to obtain such information from a direct inspection of the accounting records. The accounting records show the effect of every transaction performed by a business on the assets, the liabilities, and the proprietorship. Since even small firms may perform hundreds of transactions each day, the accounting records contain an immense amount of detail. It would be extremely difficult for the proprietor to judge, from an inspection of the records, the financial condition of his enterprise and to analyze the changes in the assets, the liabilities, and the proprietorship. Only by seeing the effect of the changes that take place over a certain period of time can the proprietor judge whether his financial condition is improving and, if it is, to what extent. 

The proprietor's needs are served satisfactorily in most cases if he receives periodically, usually monthly, statements showing the results of the operations of his business. These statements were briefly described in the previous chapter. We shall now study the balance sheet, the statement showing financial position. 

The balance sheet shows the amounts and the nature of the proprietor's assets, liabilities, and proprietorship. By comparing the present balance sheet with the one prepared at the beginning of the period, changes in financial condition may be determined. 

Form of the Balance Sheet

ASSETS = 

A balance sheet is merely an elaboration of the equation: 

LIABILITIES + PROPRIETORSHIP 

The relation between this equation and the balance sheet can be shown by a simple illustration. If Robert Moore, a retail 23

[Ch. 2 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

merchant, has assets worth $6,500 and liabilities amounting to $2,500, his financial condition may be expressed by the equation: 

ASSETS 

$6,500.00

LIABILITIES + PROPRIETORSHIP 

$2,500.00 $4,000.00

In preparing a balance sheet, it is customary to omit the equation sign and to show the assets at the left of double vertical lines, and the rights of the creditors and the proprietor at the right of the lines. The balance sheet of Robert Moore is, then, as follows: 

Assets.. 

$6,500.00 Liabilities..

Proprietorship. 

$2,500.00 4,000.00

This statement does not satisfy the needs of Moore or others entitled to information about his business, for it does not show the nature of his assets. It does not show whether he has sufficient cash to meet his needs and his obligations or sufficient merchandise to satisfy the wants of his customers. Nor does it list the value of the store and delivery equipment he uses in rendering service to his customers. It does not indicate whether he owns the building in which his business is conducted or whether he has too much or too little of his capital invested in his various assets. All of this is pertinent information, for, if he has too much property invested in one form of asset and too little invested in other forms, his business may not be so profitable as it should be. For example, if 

Moore's investment includes land and buildings worth $4,000, it is very likely that he would not have sufficient funds with which to operate his business profitably. His balance sheet should show the amount he has invested in each kind of asset; it should also show the nature of his obligations.

An examination shows that the assets of Moore consist of the following: cash, $450; merchandise, $1,700; store equipment, $850; office equipment, $300; building, $2,000; land, $1,000; store supplies, $150; and office supplies, $50. He owes Barton & Co. $1,000 for merchandise that he purchased, and he owes $1,500 on a note secured by a mortgage on his land and building. His financial condition is expressed in balance sheet form as illustrated at the top of page 25.

Definition of Balance Certain terms are used on balance sheets 

Sheet Items to describe the assets, liabilities, and proprietorship. Since these terms will be used frequently, they will be discussed briefly here.

Cash. Cash includes currency, checks, bank drafts, and money orders. Any medium of exchange that a bank will accept at face value on deposit may usually be shown on the balance sheet as cash. The cash that a business uses in its operations may be partly in its cash drawer and partly on deposit at the bank. 

Notes Receivable. A promissory note is a written promise to pay a certain sum in money at a definite time to the order of a specified person or to the bearer. A note receivable is a promissory note given by some one owing the proprietor. This note may be transferred by the proprietor to some other person, or it may be

Ch. 27 

sold to the bank for cash. It represents the type of asset most readily convertible into cash.

Accounts Receivable. An account receivable is the claim of a business against a certain person or other concern. Usually an account receivable arises from the sale of merchandise on account to a customer. Customers who have established their credit standing are ordinarily permitted to pay at some future time for the goods they buy. The granting of credit for goods sold results, not in an increase in cash, but in an asset called accounts receivable on the seller's accounting records. If there are only a few such debtors, each name may be listed on the balance sheet. It is more customary, however, to show the total of the receivables as a single item. On the balance sheet of Robert Moore, the account 

Norbert Fitch is an account receivable, which arose as a result of the sale of store equipment for which Fitch promised to pay in the future.

Merchandise. Merchandise consists of the goods purchased or produced for sale. Food offered for sale in a grocery store and clothing produced for sale in a clothing factory are examples of merchandise. The merchandise on hand at any specific time is referred to as the merchandise inventory. The inventory is usually determined by a physical count of the merchandise on hand. 

Delivery Equipment. Delivery equipment includes trucks and other equipment used in delivering goods to customers. The assets included under this heading are not intended for resale. It is expected that they will be worn out in the operation of the business, although they may be sold when they no longer serve the purpose for which they were purchased. Because they are not bought for the purpose of sale at a profit, they cannot be classified as merchandise.

Store Equipment. Store equipment includes such permanent assets as counters, showcases, window decorations, and any other items of furniture used directly in selling the goods. Like delivery equipment, this equipment may be sold when it no longer serves the purpose of the business.

Office Equipment. Office equipment includes such assets as desks, typewriters, adding machines, and other items not used directly in selling the goods but needed for the general operation of the business.

[Ch. 2 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

Building. A building owned by a business may be a structure such as a factory, a warehouse, a store, or an office. When a building is not owned by the proprietor, it is rented or leased from the owner. In that case the value of the building does not appear on the balance sheet of the business because the building is not property of the enterprise.

Land. The value of the land on which the building is located is usually shown as a separate item on the balance sheet. Although a building may no longer be valuable because of its age or its condition, the land still remains. Because of this difference, it is desirable to show the land value separately. 

Store Supplies. Store supplies include paper, twine, and similar items used in selling. They are consumed in the operation of the business and are considered as assets until they are consumed. The amount of such supplies on hand must be ascertained at the time each balance sheet is prepared. 

Office Supplies. Office supplies include stationery, stamps, and similar items not used in selling, but needed for the general operation of the business. Like store supplies, the amount on hand must be ascertained at the time that each balance sheet is prepared.

Other Assets. There are other types of assets used in business operation. Those most commonly used include investments; machinery; tools; prepaid expenses, like insurance that is paid in advance, and income receivable, like interest that has accumulated on a note receivable, but is not yet due. An explanation of such assets will be given in later chapters. 

Notes Payable. A note payable is a written promise given by the proprietor to some one to whom he owes money. He may give it to a creditor from whom he has purchased merchandise, or to a bank from which he borrows money.

Accounts Payable. An account payable is a financial obligation of a business. Usually an account payable arises from a purchase on account. A purchase in which the buyer gives his promise to pay at some future time for the goods he receives results in a liability, or account payable, on his accounting records. When there are only a few such creditors, each name may be listed on the balance sheet. It is more customary, however, to show the total of the accounts payable as a single item.

Ch. 2] 

Mortgage Payable. A mortgage payable represents a debt owed by a business, for which the creditor possesses a preferred claim through a mortgage on some particular asset. If the amount owed is not paid by the debtor, this preferred claim gives the creditor the right to bring court action that may result in the sale of the asset to satisfy the claim. 

Other Liabilities. There are other types of liabilities incurred by business enterprises. The most common include bonds payable; accrued expenses, like interest that has accumulated on a note payable, but is not yet due; and prepaid income, like interest collected in advance on a note receivable. An explanation of such liabilities will be given in later chapters. 

Proprietorship. Proprietorship is the interest of the owner in the assets of the business. If a business has no liabilities, the proprietorship is equal to the total of the assets; otherwise, it is the difference between the assets and the liabilities. 

Proprietorship, capital, and net worth are synonymous terms. 

All of these terms are used by businessmen and accountants. The first two are, perhaps, more accurate terms than the third, since the term "net worth" may seem to imply that accounting values are all based on present market values. As was stated in Chapter I, accounting values are determined by the prices agreed upon in the recorded transactions. These may be above or below the present market prices. Bankers sometimes use the term "capital" to refer to the total assets. It is usually easy to ascertain from the context the sense in which the term is used. 

Sometimes the proprietorship is vested in two or more persons. Then the business is known as a partnership or a corporation according to the methods of ownership. The capital interests of partners in a partnership are shown separately on the balance sheet. The sum of the interests of the various partners represents the net worth of the partnership. The interest of each stockholder in a corporation is not shown on the balance sheet. The interest of the stockholders as a group is shown in two amounts, the original investment and the accumulated earnings or losses since the beginning of operations by the company. The sum of these two amounts constitutes the proprietorship of the corporation. 

The manner of reporting the different types of ownership interest on the balance sheet is illustrated below. These illustrations give evidence of the personal emphasis in the sole proprietor-

[Ch. 2 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

ship and the partnership, and of the impersonal emphasis in the corporation.

SOLE PROPRIETORSHIP J. L. Pauly, Capital.. 

$ 6,564.32

PARTNERSHIP Edward Conrad, Capital.. 

David Meyer, Capital.. 

$ 12,346.15 13,148.20

Total Capital.

In the sole proprietorship and the partnership the interests of the owners are designated by the names of the owners. In the corporation the stockholders' interests are represented by the sum of the capital stock and the surplus items. The nature of the partnership and the corporation will be considered in later chapters. For the sake of simplicity it is assumed for the present that the proprietorship is vested in one person, known as the sole proprietor.

The changes that have occurred in a sole proprietorship since the previous balance sheet was prepared are sometimes reported on the current balance sheet. This practice gives a more complete report of financial condition by showing the additions through profits and the subtraction through losses or withdrawals. It is important for the proprietor to know whether his withdrawals from the business have exceeded his earnings. If such excess withdrawals occurred sufficiently often, his proprietorship would eventually disappear. This more complete statement of proprietorship is shown below.

J. L. Pauly, Capital, January 2, 1939. 

Earnings in 1939.. 

Less Withdrawals in 1939..

Total Capital, December 31, 1939.. 

$6,000.00 $1,764.32 

1,200.00 564.32 $6,564.32

Classification of Assets The assets of a mercantile business may be and Liabilities divided into three main groups:

Ch. 2] 

(1) Current Assets. Assets in the form of cash or assets that may reasonably be expected to be converted into cash in the near future by the regular operations of the business are called current assets. These assets are listed in the order in which they will probably be converted into cash. The balance sheet of Earl 

Miller, which is illustrated on page 34, shows that he has cash amounting to $600; claims against customers amounting to $3,160, of which amount $90 is estimated to be uncollectible; and merchandise on hand valued at $4,000. His current assets are therefore valued at $7,670. (2) Fixed Assets. Assets that are of a permanent or fixed nature and that will not be sold or converted into cash so long as they serve the needs of the business are called fixed assets. In some cases the most permanent of these are listed first in the fixed assets section of the balance sheet; at other times the least fixed are reported first. In this book the least fixed assets, or those with the shortest life, will be shown first. Fixed assets that must be replaced when they are no longer useful to the business carry two values on the balance sheet. From the original cost of the asset is subtracted the estimated depreciation of that asset since the time of purchase. Each fixed asset is estimated to have so many service units in it. After six months of use, obviously the service units have decreased. When expressed in dollars based on the original cost of a fixed asset, this disappearance of service units is known as depreciation. On Earl Miller's balance sheet, assuming that all fixed assets were new on June 30, the annual rates of depreciation were 20% on delivery equipment, 12% on store and office equipment, and 6% on the building. As the fixed assets have been used only six months, the depreciation on each is equal to one half of the estimated annual depreciation. The estimated cost value of service units remaining in the fixed assets is $10,608. (3) Deferred Charges to Expense. Assets that are purchased for use in the business and that will be consumed in the near future are called deferred charges to expense. For the sake of brevity, they are frequently referred to as deferred charges. The classification of deferred charges is determined largely by the analysis of expenses on the statement of profit and loss. Earl Miller has paid $520 for insurance premiums applicable to future periods. He has on hand store supplies worth $180 and office supplies worth $122.

[Ch. 2 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

The liabilities of a mercantile firm may be divided into two major groups: (1) Current Liabilities. Liabilities that will be due within a short time after they are contracted are called current liabilities. 

For example, a merchant purchases merchandise on account with the agreement that he will pay for it within thirty, sixty, or ninety days. This transaction results in a current liability. Money borrowed on a note from a local bank and wages due employees are two additional examples. Earl Miller has current liabilities of $1,800. (2) Fixed Liabilities. Liabilities that will not be due for a comparatively long time after they are contracted are called fixed liabilities. Most small firms, as well as many large firms, engaged in trading operations incur but few fixed liabilities. Earl Miller has a fixed liability in the form of a mortgage payable of $2,500. 

It is not possible to distinguish definitely between current and fixed liabilities. A liability that is to be paid within one year after it is incurred is usually considered to be a current liability. If a liability does not have to be paid within one year after it is contracted, it is usually considered to be a fixed liability. A fixed liability that is due in the current year and that is to be liquidated rather than renewed should be moved into the current liabilities group. If the business plans to continue the fixed obligation by a renewal, and if it has adequate assurance that such a plan can be carried out, the liability may be left in the fixed liabilities group. 

In the interest of complete information, the date of the maturity of the specific obligation should be shown on the balance sheet. 

Order of Presentation of Balance Sheet Items 

The order of presentation of assets and liabilities on the balance sheet varies. The sequence is often from fixed items to current items. The tendency seems, however, to be to arrange the items in order from the most current to the most fixed. Current assets are followed by fixed assets and deferred charges to expense in the assets section of the balance sheet, and current liabilities are followed by fixed liabilities in the liabilities section. Within each of these classifications, the order of items is from the most current to the least current.

The arrangement in which the current assets are listed first in the assets section, and the current liabilities are given first in the

Ch. 2] 33333 

liabilities section, is desirable since it shows clearly the liquidity of the assets and their availability in meeting obligations. The creditors of a business look first to the ability of the business to meet its obligations; and when the items on the balance sheet are arranged in order from the current to the fixed, this information can be determined easily. Furthermore, this order of presentation shows the items in relation to their use and indicates changes in the business. Current assets are closely related to current operations and are therefore subject to significant changes. Similarly, current liabilities are closely related to current operations and are subject to significant changes. Deferred charges usually represent a very small percentage of the total assets and therefore may reasonably be shown last in the assets classification. 

Heading of the Balance A balance sheet is prepared to show the Sheet financial condition of a specific firm on a specific date. The name of the firm for which the balance sheet is prepared and the day for which the report shows the financial condition are customarily used in the heading or title. The date used is that of the business day that has just closed. A balance sheet prepared at the close of the calendar year 1939 would be dated December 31, 1939.

The account form is used more frequently in the preparation of balance sheets for large organizations because the many assets and liabilities of a large business cannot be arranged in the report form in a reasonable space. The report form is frequently used in the preparation of balance sheets for small businesses because the amount of information that must be given on the balance sheet of a small business can be conveniently arranged in this form, and

[Ch. 2 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

also because the report form is usually considered to be more easily understood by those who are not trained in accounting. 

Interpretation of the One who studies a balance sheet can ob- 

Balance Sheet tain much useful information by considering the ratio of the different classes of assets and liabilities to one another.. One of the most useful of these ratios is the ratio of current assets to current liabilities. Current liabilities usually require payment in cash, most of which is realized from current assets, such as receivables and merchandise. A margin of safety should be provided for any shrinkage that might take place in the conversion of such current assets into cash. It is therefore desirable that the current assets should always be greater than the current liabilities. In the balance sheet of Earl Miller on page 34 the ratio of current assets to current liabilities is 

7,670 ÷ 1,800, or more than 4 to 1. The margin of safety is $7,670 $1,800, or $5,870. This condition is very favorable. 

Banks have insisted for many years that the value of the current assets should be twice the value of the current liabilities. 

This is not a safe rule to follow arbitrarily, since the ratio that should exist between the current assets and the current liabilities varies according to the nature of the business, the market, and temporary financial conditions. The importance that bankers attach to this ratio emphasizes the necessity for the current assets to exceed the current liabilities by a considerable margin if the firm is to be considered in a sound financial condition. 

In the illustration of the balance sheet of Earl Miller and that of Henry Adams, given on pages 34 and 35, the assets and the liabilities are arranged so that comparisons can be made most easily. For example, current assets may be compared with current liabilities, fixed assets, or total assets. Other comparisons may be made if they are desired. Furthermore, the assets are arranged in the order in which they will be turned into cash; and the liabilities, in the order in which they will become due. This method assists the proprietor and others in determining whether the sums needed to pay obligations will be available as they are required. 

In a later chapter the method of analyzing and interpreting the items on the balance sheet will be discussed in considerable detail. The present purpose is only to emphasize the importance of such an interpretation and to give an illustration of the method by which that interpretation may be made.

The banker contends that the balance sheet does not show a satisfactory condition, because the business must always carry in stock about $10,000 worth of merchandise in order to take care of its customers, and because it has accounts receivable of about $10,000. Under these circumstances the banker believes that the business may not be able to pay all its accounts payable. Considering only the preceding facts, do you believe that the company is entitled to a loan?

CHAPTER III  

Purpose of the State- The primary object of the proprietor is to ment of Profit and Loss operate his business in such a manner that he will increase his proprietorship. He desires his accounting records to provide information that will aid him in accomplishing this end. By comparing the amount of his proprietorship as shown by balance sheets prepared on successive dates, he can ascertain whether his proprietorship is increasing or decreasing, as well as the amount of the increase or the decrease; but he cannot determine the cause of the increase or the decrease. 

The balance sheet of Earl Miller, illustrated on page 34, shows his proprietorship to be $14,800. Six months before, on 

May 31, his balance sheet showed his proprietorship to be $14,000. 

His profit for the six months was $1,200 but he withdrew $400. 

The net increase in proprietorship was therefore $800 during the six months. If he had withdrawn $1,500 and had made the same net profit, his proprietorship would have been decreased $300. 

The amount of profit measures the increase in proprietorship unless there have been withdrawals or additional investments during the period.

The proprietor needs to know at frequent intervals the amount and the causes of his profit or loss in order that he may plan his future operations so as to increase his profit or to eliminate his loss. In a mercantile business, profit or loss results from the buying and selling of commodities. The proprietor needs a report showing the amount of his sales, the cost of procuring and selling the goods, and the difference, which is the profit or the loss. The statement that gives this information is known as the statement of profit and loss. It is sometimes called by the more descriptive title operating statement. It is usually prepared at the same time as the balance sheet and shows the results of the operations for a definite period of time. The period it covers is known as a fiscal period. 44

Ch. 3] 

The simplest form of the statement of profit and loss is one that shows the results 

Form of the Statement of Profit and Loss of the purchase and the sale of one unit of property. For example, if Ralph King purchases a house and lot for $5,000 and immediately sells them for $6,000, he can show the effect of the two transactions by the following statement: 

Sale Price of House and Lot.

Cost of House and Lot. 

Profit. $6,000.00 5,000.00 $1,000.00

If he paid a real-estate agent a commission of $240 for selling the house and lot, his profit would be shown in the following manner: 

Sale Price of House and Lot.

Cost of House and Lot. 

Gross Profit. Commission. 

Net Profit. $6,000.00 5,000.00 1,000.00 240.00 $ 760.00

If King continues to buy and sell such property, he can determine his profit on each transaction in this manner if he desires. 

It is not practicable, however, for a businessman to use this method in determining the profit on each sale because ordinarily he makes a large number of sales, many of which are for small amounts. 

Moreover, information about the exact profit on each sale is usually not needed. The needs of the businessman are served satisfactorily if he knows the net results of his operations at the end of the fiscal period. The statement of profit and loss gives him a summary of his operations during each period in terms of profit and loss.

If Ray Ferron, during the month of December, purchases merchandise for $2,400 and sells it for $3,000, he makes a gross profit of $600. If he consumes commodities and services that cost $450, his net profit is $150. Commodities and services, such as store and office supplies, store space, heat, and light, when consumed in the operation of a business, are known as expenses. 

Ferron's statement of profit and loss for the month of December would be as follows:

Sales..

Cost of Goods Sold. 

Gross Profit...

Expenses. 

Net Profit.. 

$3,000.00 2,400.00 600.00 450.00 $ 150.00

The Accounting Period It is customary to determine in advance the length of the accounting period to be covered by the statement of profit and loss. The period commonly chosen is a month because businessmen need information about the results of their operations at frequent intervals. Other periods are a quarter year, a half year, and a year. A business that makes monthly reports may also make quarterly, semiannual, and annual reports. Businesses almost invariably make annual reports because the year represents a recurring cycle of business activities. 

To be understood rightly, items and amounts appearing on the statement of profit and loss must be considered in the light of the period covered by the statement. 

Some transactions come within the limits of the accounting period and have effects pertaining to that period only; other transactions are carried over from previous periods or may continue into future periods. The amounts shown on the statement of profit and loss must conform to the length of the accounting period. Thus, on a statement of profit and loss for the month of 

May, sales, purchases, cost of goods sold, rent, and all other items must apply to that month alone. None may apply to April, which has passed, or to June, which is to come. 

Ordinarily the number of units sold in a given accounting period does not correspond to the number of units purchased in that period. A portion of the purchases of the previous period is included in the sales of the current period. Likewise, a portion of the purchases of the current period may still be on hand at the end of the period, and may therefore not be included in the sales of the current period. It is necessary, therefore, in determining the cost of sales, to take into consideration the lag between purchases and sales.

Definition of Items on the Statement of Profit 

The items shown on the statement of profit and loss of Earl Miller, page 53, are those which must be included in the simple form of that statement. A discussion of each follows. and Loss

Ch. 3] 

Gross Sales. In a mercantile business, the gross sales are the total amount customers have paid or have agreed to pay for the merchandise sold them. Various other types of businesses make sales of numerous commodities and services, which are designated by different terms. For example, the sales of railroad services are called freight revenue and passenger revenue; the income of professional men is known as fees; the income of investment trusts is called interest income and dividend income. In any case, the first item listed on the statement of profit and loss represents the gross return from operations.

Sales Returns and Allowances. When goods or services are unsatisfactory, the seller may permit their return by the customer or may make an allowance on the sales price. The total of such returns and allowances constitutes a deduction from gross sales to obtain net sales. 

Cost of Goods Sold. The cost of goods sold is the purchase price paid by the business for the goods it has sold, that is, for the gross sales less all returns and allowances. It is the purchase price as distinguished from the sales price. Since the lots of goods sold ordinarily do not correspond to the various lots of goods purchased, it is necessary to calculate the amount of the inventory in order to determine the cost of the goods sold. In the preceding illustration it was assumed that Ferron sold, in December, all the merchandise he had purchased during that month and that he had no merchandise on hand at the beginning of the next month, January. In other words, his purchases were equal to the cost of the goods sold. This is not usually the case. A merchant ordinarily carries a stock of goods on hand at all times. If there is an inventory at the beginning of the fiscal period and one at the end of the fiscal period, both inventories must be taken into consideration when the cost of goods sold is determined. 

The cost of merchandise acquired by a merchant is made up of two different items: the invoice price payable to the vendor and the freight and drayage charges for the delivery of the goods. 

Buyers of merchandise must therefore consider two items in determining the cost of their purchases, which have a quoted price and a delivered cost. The statement of profit and loss adds the amount of the freight charges to the invoice cost of purchases in order to get the cost of goods purchased and delivered.

[Ch. 3 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

For example, if Earl Miller began his fiscal period with an inventory of $8,000, purchased merchandise amounting to $41,600, and paid freight and drayage charges of $2,400, the total cost of the goods he had available for sale would be the sum of these three items, or $52,000. If the merchandise he still had on hand 

December 31 was $4,000, the cost of goods sold would be the difference between $52,000 and $4,000, or $48,000. 

This information should be shown on the statement of profit and loss for the six months ending December 31 in the following manner: 

Cost of Goods Sold: 

Merchandise Inventory, June 30, 19 

Purchases.. 

$ 8,000.00 $41,600.00 

2,400.00 44,000.00 52,000.00 4,000.00 $48,000.00

Freight In. 

Merchandise Available for Sale... 

Less Merchandise Inventory, Dec. 31, 19 

Cost of Goods Sold.... 

The value of the inventory is ascertained by making a physical count of the stock and by valuing each item at the cost price or the cost to replace, whichever is the lower. For example, one hundred units of merchandise that had cost $5.50 a unit are on hand at the end of a fiscal period. The same merchandise can now be purchased for $4.75 a unit. The inventory should show this item at $475, the present market price, which is lower than the original cost. On the other hand, if one hundred units of another item that cost $5.50 a unit are on hand and the market price has gone up to $6 a unit, that item should be valued at $550, the original cost price, which is lower than the present market price. 

Gross Profit on Sales. When the cost of merchandise sold is subtracted from the total sales, the difference represents the profit that would be made if no expenses were incurred in conducting the business. Expenses are always incurred, however, in the operation of a business, and they must be considered in determining the profit. Consequently, the difference between the amount received from sales and the cost of the merchandise sold is termed gross profit on sales. It is called gross profit because the expenses of operating the business must be deducted to obtain the final or net profit.

Ch. 49 

The difference between the cost price and the selling price was referred to in Chapter I, page 5, as margin. Margin is the amount in dollars and cents, or in percentage of sales, that the merchandiser receives for rendering service to his customers. 

The proprietor of a merchandising enterprise must keep his operating costs within this margin if he is to gain a net profit as a result of his operations. The extent of the margin definitely limits the scope of his activities unless he is willing to suffer a loss from operations.

Expenses. Commodities and services of various kinds are consumed in the operation of a business. The services of sales clerks are used in selling merchandise to customers, and the services of other employees are used in delivering to customers the merchandise sold. Paper and twine are consumed in wrapping the merchandise to prepare it for delivery. Coal is consumed in heating the building in which the business is conducted. Stationery and stamps are consumed in carrying on the correspondence between the business and its creditors and customers. Other commodities and services too numerous to mention are consumed in the operation of a merchandise enterprise. 

All commodities and services purchased are assets before they are consumed. When consumed, they become expenses and must be deducted from the gross profit on sales. It is usually more convenient to treat those transactions involving services that will be consumed before the end of the period as deductions from proprietorship rather than as assets to be consumed. Thus, monthly rent that is payable on the first of the month is treated as an expense because it will have become an expense by the end of the accounting period. At the time it is incurred, it represents an asset equal in value to its cost, because it represents a right to use space facilities. During the month it becomes less valuable through use, and at the end of the month it represents wholly a deduction from proprietorship.

All expenses of a business may be shown as one item on the statement of profit and loss, but this method does not provide adequate information concerning their nature. A proprietor desires to know the amount of each commodity or service consumed so that he can ascertain whether the amount is too large and, if it is, take measures to reduce it. He wishes the statement of profit and loss to be made in such a form that he can see the

50. [Ch. 3 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

amount of each expense. Large firms need a very detailed analysis of expenses for control purposes. In subsequent chapters considerable attention will be given to the method of providing this analysis. 

The expenses of greatest significance in a typical retail store are salaries of sales clerks, store supplies used, delivery expense, advertising, rent or the expense of operating the building if the building is owned, office supplies used, and miscellaneous expenses. 

Under the heading "Miscellaneous Expense" may be included such items as are not of sufficient importance to make it desirable to show them as separate items on the statement of profit and loss. 

In general, the best classification of expenses is one that keeps at a minimum the amount representing miscellaneous expense. 

Whenever the total of miscellaneous expense is a considerable amount, this total should be analyzed and new expense classifications should be set up. 

The extent to which the classification of expenses is carried depends on the degree to which information on the statement of profit and loss is used to measure business activities. The expenses of a large business will probably be classified in a large number of groups, but those of a small business can usually be classified under a few major heads. In a small retail business of the kind that has been used for illustrative purposes, it is usually satisfactory to classify the expenses in two main groups: (1) Expenses incurred directly in connection with the sale of merchandise. These are known as selling expenses. Salaries of sales clerks, store supplies used, delivery expense, and advertising belong to this group. (2) Expenses incurred in the general operation of the business. 

These are known as general expenses. Building expense, office supplies used, and miscellaneous expense are placed in this group. 

This is the general miscellaneous group. 

It is customary to show this classification of expenses on the statement of profit and loss in the manner shown at the top of page 51. 

The foregoing description has dealt with the average small retailing or wholesaling concern. It illustrates an accounting procedure for that type of enterprise. The items appearing on a statement of profit and loss are determined by the nature of the

Heading of the State- 

The statement of profit and loss is prepared ment of Profit and Loss to show the result of the operations of a specific business during a particular period of time. Whatever the length of the period, the period is known as a fiscal period. 

There is an increasing tendency for businessmen to seek information concerning the results of their operations at more frequent intervals. Frequent reports on operations make possible more effective control of business policies and activities. 

The name of the proprietor and the period of time for which a statement of profit and loss is made should be clearly stated. 

If the period covered is one year, the heading of the statement will indicate that sales, purchases, and other items are for a year. 

The items appearing in a statement of profit and loss have no significance except when analyzed in connection with the period. covered. Sales, purchases, cost of sales, and operating expenses represent continuing acts in the history of a business enterprise. 

Sales of $10,000, for instance, may have little importance until one knows that this amount represents sales for one month rather than for one year. Rent expense of $1,200 might be very reason-

52 [Ch. 3 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

able for a statement of profit and loss for one year, but very unreasonable for a period of one month.

Interpretation of the

Statement of Profit and Loss

The proprietor should watch carefully the ratio of each group of his expenses to his sales. If the ratio of his selling expenses to his sales shows an increase, it may be due to the fact that the sales volume is being obtained at an unsatisfactory cost. Most businesses can increase their sales if they are willing to incur sufficient expense. If an increase in the ratio of general expenses to sales is indicated, it may be because the rent expense is too large or the miscellaneous expense is too great. In any case the proprietor must watch these ratios carefully in order to make a satisfactory profit. 12,000 3,600 For example, the gross profit on the sales of Earl Miller is 60,000 or 20 per cent for the period covered by the statement on page 53. If this percentage of margin has declined from 25 per cent in the previous period to 20 per cent in this period, it means either that prices on commodities sold have dropped or that prices paid suppliers have advanced. Regardless of which is the cause, the condition is to the disadvantage of Miller, the proprietor. The percentage of salaries of sales clerks to sales is 0.000 or 6 per cent, that of advertising to sales 1,000 or 2 per cent. If in the previous period the ratios had been 8 per cent and 1 per cent, it might be evidence of the effectiveness of increased advertising appropriation. 

The net profit is 1000 or 2 per cent of sales. If this has dropped from 5 per cent in a previous period, it behooves the proprietor to make a careful inquiry into all the factors causing the decrease. .These factors are shown on the two statements of profit and loss, and a study of them should provide information for better policies in the periods to come. 60,000

Comparisons similar to these should be made at the end of each fiscal period in order that the proprietor may determine whether his statement of profit and loss indicates favorable or
