If there is a net loss instead of a net profit for the period, the statement is the same in form as that in Illustration No. 3, except that the amount of the gross profit on sales is subtracted from the total of the expenses and the final amount is shown as "Net Loss" instead of as "Net Profit."

[Ch. 3 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES Relation of the State- 

The statement of profit and loss of Earl ment of Profit and Loss Miller shown in Illustration No. 3 covers to the Balance Sheet the period of six months since June 30, 19--, when the balance sheet was prepared. This statement of profit and loss accompanies the balance sheet shown in Illustration No. 1, page 34. Both statements include the merchandise inventory as of December 31, 19--, and the net profit for the six months. The balance sheet subtracts the withdrawals of the proprietor during the six months from the net profit, leaving the net increase in proprietorship since the last balance sheet. In one sense, the statement of profit and loss expands on the idea of proprietorship increase shown in the balance sheet, by summarizing the business operations of the period that result in that increase.

The statement of profit and loss that accompanies a balance sheet measures the effect of the operations of the accounting period just preceding the date of the balance sheet. The balance sheet is cumulative; that is, it shows the financial position resulting from the activities summarized on all previous statements of profit and loss. The statement of profit and loss is limited to a particular accounting period; it is one of many such statements that, taken together, would give the result of the total volume of activities since the enterprise began.

1. What relationship has the statement of profit and loss to the balance sheet?

CHAPTER IV  

Recording Transactions It has been shown that every transaction performed by a business produces a change in one or more elements of the equation: 

ASSETS LIABILITIES + PROPRIETORSHIP 

As the number of transactions completed by an ordinary business is large, many changes take place in the assets, the liabilities, and the proprietorship during the fiscal period. If an accurate balance sheet and statement of profit and loss are to be prepared at the end of the fiscal period, it is necessary that the results of all changes be ascertained.

At the end of the period the proprietor can obtain the amounts of some of the items to be reported on the balance sheet, such as cash, by making a count. This method does not, however, provide a means of proof. For instance, unless the proprietor knows the amount of cash received and the amount paid during the period, he does not have proof that the amount on hand is what it should be. A record showing the amount received and the amount paid should give the same balance as the amount of cash found by counting the cash at the end of the period. Counting alone is therefore insufficient. Accounting must provide the records to prove that the count is correct and that the result is what it should be. The proprietor must also have a satisfactory method of arriving at the value of some other assets, such as delivery equipment, furniture and fixtures, and store supplies. In order to determine satisfactorily the value of such assets, he must know not only the original cost of the assets but also the increase or decrease in the value of these assets since their purchase. Also, when he prepares his statement of profit and loss, he will find it difficult to remember the total of his sales or of his expenses. 

Since the proprietor of a business cannot remember all facts concerning his transactions and their effects on assets, liabilities, and proprietorship, a record of each transaction must be made. 59

[Ch. 4 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

Otherwise, accurate information will not be available for the preparation of the balance sheet and the statement of profit and loss. The purpose of this chapter is to discuss and illustrate a method of recording changes in assets, liabilities, and proprietorship. Proprietorship changes involve the income and expense items appearing on the statement of profit and loss. 

Accounts 

The effect of each transaction might be shown by the preparation of a new balance sheet and statement of profit and loss after the completion of the transaction. Since hundreds of transactions are performed during each fiscal period, and in many businesses during each day, this plan would result in an unreasonable amount of detailed work. 

Furthermore, new statements are not desired after each transaction, since the manager does not have time to observe the effect on his assets and proprietorship of each of a great number of transactions. Information showing him the effects of groups of similar transactions is sufficient for his purpose. 

It is therefore more convenient and equally satisfactory to maintain a separate record with each item on the balance sheet and to show in this record the increases and the decreases in the item that occur during the period. At the end of the period, the increases are added to the amount of the item at the beginning of the period, and the decreases are subtracted from this sum. 

The result is the amount desired for the balance sheet. Similarly a separate record is kept with each item on the statement of profit and loss so that the total may be determined at the end of the fiscal period.

The form of record kept with each item is known as an account. A group of accounts is known as a ledger. Not more than one account is ordinarily kept on one page of a ledger. Construction of Accounts following illustration:

The relationship between the balance sheet and the ledger can be observed from the William Gates invests $5,000 in a retail store. On his balance sheet the transaction would appear as follows: Cash... 

5,000 00 William Gates, Capital... 

5,000 00

Ch. 4] 

The investment of Gates may also be shown in accounts in the following manner:

CASH 

$5,000

WILLIAM GATES, CAPITAL 

$5,000

The names of the two accounts are Cash and William Gates, Capital. The title of each account is written over the horizontal lines above the figures and is descriptive of the data in the account. 

Each account consists of two sides: the left and the right. It is customary to enter assets and additions to assets on the left side of the asset accounts, and proprietorship and additions to proprietorship on the right side of proprietorship accounts. The student will therefore find it helpful to note the relation between accounts and the account form of the balance sheet. Assets and additions to assets are entered on the left side of both asset accounts and the account form of the balance sheet; and proprietorship and additions to proprietorship, on the right side. This method makes it possible to bring the accounts together at any time to form a balance sheet in account form. 

Gates next purchases store equipment for $1,500 in cash and office equipment for $500 in cash. These transactions result in decreases of $1,500 and $500 in cash and in the acquisition of the assets, store equipment, $1,500, and office equipment, $500. 

The ledger of Gates then appears as follows:

CASH $5,000 $1,500 500 

STORE EQUIPMENT 

$1,500

OFFICE EQUIPMENT 

$500 

WILLIAM GATES, CAPITAL 

$5,000

[Ch. 4 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

The amount $1,500 is entered on the left side of the account Store Equipment because this purchase represents the acquisition of an asset, store equipment. The same amount is entered on the right side of the cash account because it is a subtraction from an asset, cash. The acquisition of office equipment in exchange for cash is treated in a similar manner. All amounts representing subtractions from an asset are entered on the right side of the asset account so that they can be deducted in one amount whenever the net value of the asset is to be determined. The difference between the sums of the entries on the two sides is known as the balance of the account and is the value of the asset. 

Gates also purchases merchandise for $2,500 in cash. Since purchases enter into the cost of goods sold on the statement of profit and loss as a subtraction from sales income, they represent a deduction from proprietorship. A deduction from Gates's capital is entered on the left side of an account. Because this account provides a record of the purchases of the business, it is known as the purchases account. After this transaction has been recorded, Gates's ledger will appear as follows:

The Theory of Debit It will be observed that a separate account and Credit is constructed for each item on the balance sheet and the statement of profit and loss. If additional information is to be shown on either statement, additional accounts must be constructed to supply it. The number of accounts to be opened in the ledger depends on the nature of the transactions and the extent of detail desired on the accounting statements. 

Each account is divided into three parts: a space for the name of the account and two separate sections below the name. The left section of the account is called the debit side, and the right section is called the credit side. When items are entered on the left side of the account, they are called debits and the account is said to be debited. When items are entered on the right side of the account, they are called credits and the account is said to be credited. The difference between the total debits and the total credits of an account is called the balance of the account, regardless of whether that difference is a debit or a credit.

The cost of an asset is entered on the left, or debit, side of the account at the time the asset is acquired; and all increases in the value of the asset are shown as additional debits. Decreases in the asset are the opposite of increases and are therefore entered as credits on the right side of the account. Amounts owed to a creditor are entered on the right, or credit, side of his account. 

When a payment is made to a creditor, the amount is entered on the left side of his account as a debit. The balance owed is thus decreased. The original investment of the proprietor is entered on the right, or credit, side of his capital account. Increases in proprietorship are entered on the credit side; decreases, on the debit side.

A business transaction affects not less than two accounts. If only two accounts are affected, one must be debited and the other must be credited for a like amount. This equality of debit and credit is inherent in the equation presented in Chapter I 

(ASSETS = LIABILITIES + PROPRIETORSHIP) and is the basis of what is known as the double-entry system of recording. 

In the double-entry system the sum of the debit entries in the ledger equals the sum of the credit entries.

The theory of debit and credit, in its application to the various kinds of accounts presented, is illustrated by the following diagram:

Ch. 4] 

 ANY ASSET ACCOUNT DEBIT 

Increases

ANY LIABILITY ACCOUNT CREDIT DEBIT 

Decreases Decreases 

CREDIT 

Increases

ANY PROPRIETORSHIP ACCOUNT DEBIT 

Decreases 

CREDIT 

Increases

If a transaction affects two asset accounts, it must represent an increase in one asset and a decrease in the other. The receipt of cash in payment of a customer's account represents an increase in the asset cash and a decrease in the asset account receivable. If a transaction affects one asset and one liability account, both accounts must be increased or decreased in like amount.

The items in the statement of profit and loss give the details that result in a net increase or decrease in proprietorship. Statement of profit and loss accounts are therefore proprietorship accounts. Of these, there are two types: those that increase and those that decrease proprietorship. The chief increasing account is 

Sales. From the sales price, as has been said before, all costs and expenses must be subtracted. Expense accounts are therefore those that decrease proprietorship. This is illustrated by the expanded accounting equation, together with the debit and credit schedule.

ASSETS = 

LIABILITIES + PROPRIETORSHIP 

1EXPENSE 

+ INCOME 

The schedule given above relates the debit and credit theory to the expanded accounting equation. It provides for all the groups of items in the balance sheet and the statement of profit and loss and indicates the debit and credit treatment of each group. Every item that may appear on either statement may be classified under one of the above groups, and its relation to debit and credit may be determined thereby.

The positive side of an account is determined by the position of its classifying parent in the accounting equation. The negative

[Ch 4 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

side is always the opposite of the positive side. Thus, Cash, an asset account, has additions on the debit side and subtractions on the credit side; whereas Notes Payable, a liability account, has additions on the credit side and subtractions on the debit side. 

Income and expense accounts commonly have entries on only one side. Thus, Sales, an income account, has only additions on the credit side; and Sales Salaries, an expense account, has only additions on the debit side.

The account serves as a mathematical device for showing increases and decreases in values. When items are to be added, they are placed on the same side of an account; when items are to be subtracted from an item or items previously entered in the account, they are placed on the opposite side. When the balance of the account is to be obtained, the two columns of figures are added and the smaller sum is deducted from the larger. Obviously, the sum of the amounts entered on the left side of an asset account should not be less than the amounts on the right. Similarly, the sum of the amounts entered on the left side of a liability account should not exceed the sum of the amounts on the right. 

It is customary to arrange accounts in the ledger in the same order in which they are listed on the accounting statements. 

Current asset accounts precede fixed asset accounts; all asset accounts come before liability accounts; proprietorship accounts are last in order. If a loose-leaf ledger is used, any new account needed as the result of any transaction can be readily inserted into its correct place. When accounts are to be opened for a series of transactions, it is advisable to plan the order of the accounts to conform to the transactions to be recorded. An estimate of the transactions that will probably be performed can be made from a study of the transactions of a previous period or of a similar business.

The Trial Balance 

Since the debit resulting from each transaction is equal to the credit resulting from that transaction, it follows that, if a correct record is made in the accounts, the total of the debits in all the accounts will equal the total of the credits in all the accounts. A test of the equality of the debits and the credits is made at intervals, usually at the end of each month. Such a test is known as a trial balance. 

The trial balance serves to summarize the information shown in detail in the ledger. This summary is always prepared at the end of a fiscal period and provides most of the information needed to prepare the balance sheet and the statement of profit and loss. 

A trial balance may be taken by listing the total of the debits and the total of the credits of each account, adding these totals of the accounts, and then comparing the sum of the debit totals with the sum of the credit totals. A preferable method, however, is to use the difference between the total of the debits and the total of the credits of each account; that is, to obtain the debit or the credit balance of the account and then to list the debit and the credit balances of all the accounts on the trial balance. If the sum of the debit balances equals the sum of the credit balances,

Ch. 4] 

it is evident that the debits and the credits in the ledger are equal. 

A trial balance of the ledger of William Gates, prepared according to the latter method, is illustrated below in Illustration No. 4. Cash

Proof Provided by the The trial balance does not give a complete 

Trial Balance proof of the bookkeeper's work. It shows only that the debits and the credits are equal. This is valuable information, however, since errors frequently affect the equality of the debits and the credits, and are indicated by a trial balance that is out of balance. The trial balance does not, of course, prove that the proper account has been debited or credited or that the debits and the credits are for the proper amounts. It is therefore important that the accuracy of the figures be determined before they are entered in the bookkeeping record and that care be taken to record the entries in the proper accounts. 

The Trial Balance and The chief source of data shown in the bal- Accounting Statements ance sheet and statement of profit and loss is the trial balance, which summarizes the ledger. The ledger, the book of accounts, is the most important record in the accounting

[Ch. 4 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

system. It seems natural that accounts and accounting should be so intimately related.

In the case of William Gates the ledger is the only source of information for the statements. Since all of the goods purchased in October were sold, and since all of the expenses are for the month of October alone, the trial balance does not need to be adjusted before the statements are prepared. 

The balance sheet of William Gates, as of October 31, 19——, and his statement of profit and loss for the month of October may be prepared directly from the figures in the trial balance. It is considered advisable, however, to make use of a columnar device called a work sheet to insure the accuracy of the calculations before the formal statements are prepared. Whenever a work sheet is used in accounting, it is only a means to an end and is justified only to the extent that it facilitates the work of preparing the statements. 

A six-column work sheet for William Gates as of October 31, 19--, is shown in illustration No. 5 on page 75. This work sheet consists of three pairs of columns: the first pair containing the trial balance, and the last two pairs containing the figures for the statement of profit and loss and the balance sheet. For his convenience in preparing these reports, the accountant extends all the items in the Trial Balance columns into the two pairs of money columns under the headings "Statement of Profit and Loss" and "Balance Sheet."

The amount of the first item, Cash, is extended into the debit column under the heading "Balance Sheet" because it will appear on the balance sheet as an asset. Accounts Receivable, Store 

Equipment, and Office Equipment are likewise assets and are extended into the same column after Cash. Since the account payable, Jones Company, represents a liability, that amount is extended to the credit column under the heading "Balance Sheet." 

The balance of the William Gates, capital account is the proprietorship at the beginning of the period; it is extended into the credit column under the heading "Balance Sheet." The balance of the William Gates, personal account represents a deduction from proprietorship. This amount might have been subtracted from the original proprietorship instead of being debited to a separate account. Then only the net proprietorship would be extended into the credit column. ILLUSTRATION No. 5, SIX-COLUMN WORK SHEET 

account and that of the personal account are ordinarily extended into the Balance Sheet columns. The balance of the personal account is extended into the debit column to show that it is actually a deduction from the amount of the proprietorship that has been recorded in the credit column. All the items in the Trial 

Balance columns that will be used in the preparation of the balance sheet have now been entered in the debit and credit columns of the Balance Sheet pair.

All the other items on the trial balance represent income and expenses, and are therefore extended into the Statement of Profit and Loss columns. Since the balance of the sales account represents income, it is entered in the credit column. The amounts of purchases, sales salaries, advertising, miscellaneous selling expense, rent, and miscellaneous general expense represent expenses and are therefore extended into the debit column. 

After all the amounts have been extended into the Statement of Profit and Loss and the Balance Sheet columns, these columns are totaled. The difference between the total of the debit column and the total of the credit column under "Profit and Loss Statement" is the amount of the net profit or loss for the period. The

26 [Ch. 4 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

net profit of Gates is found to be $610. If the work has been completed correctly, this amount is also the difference between the total of the debit column and the total of the credit column of the Balance Sheet pair of columns, because the increase in the proprietorship during the period plus the withdrawals should equal the profit for the period. The amount of the profit, $610, is added to the total of the debit, or Expense, column to show that the expenses plus the net profit are equal to the income. The same amount is then added to the total of the credit column of the Balance Sheet pair of columns. The sum obtained by this addition is equal to the total of the balance sheet debit column. This procedure proves that the net profit for the period is equal to the sum of the increase in the proprietorship and the withdrawals.

The totals of the four columns under the headings "Statement of 

Profit and Loss" and "Balance Sheet" are brought down to the same line, and the columns are ruled with double lines. The work sheet has now been completed and can be used as the basis for the preparation of the statements.

CHAPTER V ADJUSTMENTS AND THE ACCOUNTING STATEMENTS Mixed Accounts 

In the previous chapter the use of the account to record increases and decreases in balance sheet and statement of profit and loss items due to transactions was explained. All transactions affecting statement of profit and loss accounts were such that they fell within one accounting period. All the purchases of the period were sold within the period so that there was no inventory of merchandise to account for. All expense transactions fell within the period and none extended beyond the period. For example, there were no inventories of store supplies or office supplies; there was no calculation of expired insurance; there were no wages or salaries due employees at the end of the period. As a result of the nature of these transactions all items in the trial balance taken at the end of the period were balance sheet items or statement of profit and loss items. 

In actual business it is neither convenient nor possible to make transactions coincide with the accounting period. Store supplies are purchased for future use; if any are on hand at the end of the period, it is possible to adjust the record in the store supplies account to show how much was used and how much is on hand. Prior to adjustment, accounts of this type are called mixed accounts. They contain amounts neither wholly balance sheet nor wholly statement of profit and loss, but mixed. The store supplies account balance mentioned above is part asset and part expense. It is necessary to separate the asset and the expense portions so that the amounts on the balance sheet and the statement of profit and loss will be correct. It is the purpose of this chapter to illustrate the accounting for adjustments and the preparation of statements from a trial balance that must be adjusted before the statements are prepared.

Standard Form of the 

Account The accounts in the ledger of William Gates, given in Chapter IV, are constructed in the simplest form possible. This is termed the "T" form of account. This form merely serves to show the purpose for which 83

[Ch. 5 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

accounts are used. It does not provide for a convenient record of all the information that it is advisable to show in the account. A more useful form of ruling for the account is shown in Illustration 

No. 6. 

19Oct 24 Cash 

Fred Smith 

150000 Oct 2 Purchases. 

PAGE 8 

1500 1 2 3 ·4 1 2 3 

ILLUSTRATION No. 6, STANDARD RULING FOR THE ACCOUNT 

It should be observed that the standard form of account preserves the original three parts of the "T" account. The name appears at the top, and provision is made within the account for entries on the debit and credit sides. The page of the ledger on which the account appears is indicated at the upper right-hand corner of the account. The columns on the left and right sides provide for the classification of information.

The columns shown in this illustration are used as follows: 

Columns 1 and 2 are the date columns, the first being used for the month and the second for the day of the month. After the month has been written once, it need not be repeated during the same month. The year is written just above the month. 

Column 3 is the explanation column, in which any information desired may be written. When a transaction is entered directly in ledger accounts, it is customary to write in the explanation column of each account the title of the other account in the transaction. In Chapter VI a preliminary record in which entries are made before they are recorded in the ledger is described. 

When this record is made, the explanation column is commonly left blank. The explanation column of a liability account is then used to indicate the terms of the liability; that is, the date payment is due and any discounts that may be allowed. As will be explained later, this column is used in other accounts to indicate similar information.

Column 4 is the folio column. When a preliminary record of transactions is made before recording in the ledger, the page of

the book containing the preliminary record on which the transaction was first entered is written in the folio column of the account so that cross reference will be possible.

Column 5 is the amount, or money, column, the wider space being used for dollars and the narrower space for cents. 

Illustration of Accounts The entries for the transactions in this in Standard Form chapter are recorded in accounts in standard form. Each account is usually kept on a separate page of the ledger. In order that space may be conserved, however, only partial pages are reproduced in the illustrations in this chapter. 

An illustration of a cash account with one debit entry and two credit entries is given below.

ILLUSTRATION No. 7, AccOUNT WITH SEVERAL ENTRIES 

The credit amount column of the cash account has been totaled, and the total has been entered in small pencil figures directly below the last entry. The difference between the debit and the total credits is $1,649.50. This amount is the balance of the account. It is entered in small pencil figures in the explanation column on the debit side, since the debit exceeds the total credits. 

Accounts are commonly footed and balanced in this manner when the balances are to be used on the balance sheet or other reports. 

It is not necessary to foot accounts when the balance is evident without footing. 

Transferring an

Account When the page allotted to an account has become full, the account must be transferred to another page. This may be done by balancing the account and transferring only the balance to the new page, or by transferring the totals of the debits and the credits. The latter method is preferable, because it is advantageous to know the total debits and the total credits of the account. 

When the balance is to be transferred to a new page, the difference between the total debits and the total credits (the bal-

[Ch. 5 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

ance of the account) is entered on the side of the account having the smaller total. The amounts on both sides are then added, the totals are written directly opposite each other, and double lines are drawn across all columns except the explanation columns. 

The balance of the account is then transferred to the new page by recording the amount on the side of the account that contained the larger total on the original page. The method of transferring the balance of an account is shown in Illustration No. 8. Feb

When the totals of the account are to be transferred to a new page, the amounts on both sides are added, the totals are written directly opposite each other, double lines are drawn across all columns except the explanation columns, and the totals are transferred to the new page by recording them on the same sides of the account as those on which they appeared on the original page. This method is shown in Illustration No. 9 at the top of page 87. Recording Changes in In Chapter III it was pointed out that in 

Proprietorship a mercantile business profit or loss results from the buying and the selling of commodities. The merchant purchases merchandise that he expects to sell at a price greater than its cost. The difference between the selling price and the

ILLUSTRATION No. 9, FORWARDING THE TOTALS OF AN ACCOUNT 

cost is known as gross profit. The proprietor also purchases commodities and services to be used in the operation of his business. 

When these are consumed, they become expenses. If the gross profit is greater than the expenses, a net profit is made. If the opposite is true, a loss results. The successful merchant is the one who keeps his expenses within the limits set by the gross profit. 

The profit from each sale could be calculated and then added directly to proprietorship. This method was followed in the equations in Chapter I. Similarly, expenses could be deducted directly from the proprietorship. These methods are not used, however, for they would require an excessive amount of work. 

Furthermore, they would not provide the data for the statement of profit and loss. The sales would not be shown in one account but rather in two, Merchandise and Capital; and the purchases, the inventory, and the expenses would not be entered in separate. accounts.

For the reasons given, it is customary to set up separate accounts for merchandise inventory, purchases, sales, and each expense. The inventory account is debited for the inventory at the beginning of the period because that inventory represents an asset, merchandise. The purchases account is debited for each purchase because purchases enter into the cost of goods sold, the first amount to be deducted from the gross income from sales.

[Ch. 5 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

The sales account is credited for each sale because it represents the gross income from which the cost of goods sold and the expenses are subtracted to find the net profit or loss on the statement of profit and loss.

The amounts given in payment of expenses are debited to appropriate expense accounts because they represent decreases in proprietorship. Also, the amounts of assets that will be wholly consumed before the end of the accounting period may be debited to expense accounts because, at the time that the statement of profit and loss will be made, they will represent decreases in proprietorship. The amounts of the assets that will be only partially consumed at the end of the accounting period, may, on the other hand, be debited to asset accounts. At the end of the accounting period the consumed portion of such an asset will be determined by mathematical calculation or by an inventory, and this amount will be debited to an expense account. 

An illustration of an asset that will be wholly consumed before the end of the accounting period is the rent paid on the first of the month for the period of the month. At the end of the month rent will have become an expense, although it was an asset at the beginning of the month. The payment of rent is therefore recorded in the light of what rent will be at the end of the period-an expense. A three-year insurance premium paid on the first of the month will have only partially expired at the end of the month. 

The payment of insurance is therefore recorded in the light of what the insurance will chiefly be at the end of the month-an asset. At the end of the month the expired portion will be determined mathematically (1/36 of the premium) and will be debited to an expense account.

Expenses appearing on the statement of profit and loss come from two principal sources: (1) Services or commodities that are wholly consumed during the accounting period. (2) Services or commodities that are obtained in or prior to the accounting period but that are only partially consumed during the period.

All transactions of the first type are recorded by debits to expense accounts. Those belonging to the second type are recorded by debits to asset accounts; but the portions that are consumed during the period are charged to expense accounts at the end of the period.

To illustrate the use of the sales, merchandise inventory, purchases, and expense accounts, the transactions of E. C. Martin will be recorded in a ledger in which these accounts are found.

The Profit and Loss The income and the expenses of Martin 

Summary Account have been recorded in a number of different accounts, and the transactions are summarized in the trial balance shown above. As will be shown in the following chapter, the information in these accounts will be brought together in an account called Profit and Loss Summary, which is placed in the ledger on the page before the sales account. The profit and loss summary account will then contain essentially the same information that is reported on the statement of profit and loss. 

Several items on the trial balance, together with one not available on the trial balance, that is, the merchandise inventory at the end of the period, are combined on the statement of profit and loss to obtain the cost of goods sold. The trial balance of Martin shows the merchandise inventory at the beginning of the period. 

That merchandise inventory has, during the period, been combined

[Ch. 5 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

with the merchandise purchased. From the total of this merchandise, sales have been made during October. As sales have been recorded in one account during the fiscal period, no attempt has been made to determine the cost of the goods sold in each transaction. In order to determine the cost of the goods sold for the entire period, a physical inventory of merchandise on hand is taken at the close of business on October 31. When Martin takes such an inventory, he finds the accounting value of the merchandise on hand to be $4,500.

This amount is an increase of $1,500 over the inventory at the beginning of the fiscal period. It would be possible to subtract this $1,500 from the total of the purchases for October to determine the cost of the goods sold. The amount obtained would be the correct amount, but the method of combining figures would not correspond to the operations of the period. The actual procedure was that purchases, as they were received, were added to the old inventory, and from both of these sales were made without discrimination. Hence, on the statement of profit and loss, purchases, increased by freight in, are added to the old inventory, and from the sum of these the new inventory is subtracted to obtain the cost of goods sold. 

The treatment of these items in the profit and loss summary account follows their treatment on the statement of profit and loss.

The Ten-Column Work Since some of the amounts appearing in Sheet the trial balance of E. C. Martin are unadjusted, that is, not adjusted to show the use of the commodity during the period, it is necessary to expand the work sheet to include adjustments. Hence, two pairs of columns are added: the first pair for the actual adjustments and the second pair for an adjusted trial balance. The adjusted trial balance combines the information in the trial balance of the ledger and in the adjustments and provides the correct amount for each item appearing in the accounting statements.

In Chapter III it was observed that, in the preparation of the statement of profit and loss, the bookkeeper uses both the beginning and the ending inventories of merchandise and the exact amounts of all expenses. The trial balance of Martin, however, does not contain all this information. It shows the mer-

chandise inventory of September 30, but not that of October 31; it shows the total of the store and office supplies purchased, but these amounts are not necessarily the same as the amounts of the supplies used or consumed during the period; it lists the amount paid for insurance, but this amount does not correspond to the amount of insurance that has expired during the month. The second group of money columns under the heading "Adjustments" is therefore provided on the work sheet for those items that are required to bring the mixed accounts up to date. Amounts that represent debits are entered in the Debit column; and those that represent credits, in the Credit column. 

By an actual count of the merchandise on hand, it is found that the merchandise inventory on October 31 is worth $4,500. 

This amount represents an asset and should therefore be recorded as a debit to the merchandise inventory account. It is entered in the Debit Adjustment column on a line with the title "Merchandise Inventory." Since the new merchandise inventory represents also a deduction from the cost of goods sold, the amount of $4,500 is entered in the Credit Adjustment column on a line with "Profit and Loss Summary." Since the profit and loss summary account is not a part of the trial balance but is a summary account through which the merchandise inventory is adjusted, the title is written on the first blank line below the trial balance. This adjustment is designated Adjustment (a) on the work sheet, Illustration 

No. 10, page 98.

The trial balance of Martin shows that the merchandise inventory at the beginning of the fiscal period was valued at $3,000. 

This inventory is no longer available because the merchandise purchased during the month was added to it and then all but $4,500 worth of this total merchandise (the ending inventory on 

October 31) was used in making sales. The bookkeeper indicates that the beginning inventory is no longer an asset in its original form by writing $3,000 in the Credit Adjustment column on the line with the title "Merchandise Inventory." This credit offsets the debit of $3,000 in the trial balance. The merchandise inventory at the beginning of a fiscal period is reported on the statement of profit and loss as a part of the cost of goods sold. As explained on page 93, all profits and losses are summarized in the profit and loss summary account after the financial statements have been prepared. In order to show that the beginning mer-

[Ch. 5 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

chandise inventory represents one of the costs to be summarized in the profit and loss summary account, the bookkeeper of Martin enters the amount $3,000 in the Debit Adjustment column on the line with the title "Profit and Loss Summary." This is designated as Adjustment (b) on the work sheet. 

By an actual count the store supplies on hand are found to be worth $160. Since the store supplies account has a debit balance of $300, it is evident that supplies worth $140 have been used. In order to show this change in value on the work sheet, the bookkeeper enters the amount $140 in the Credit Adjustment column opposite the title "Store Supplies” and also in the Debit Adjustment column opposite "Store Supplies Used." As the store supplies used account does not appear in the trial balance, the account title is written directly below that for the profit and loss summary account. If other accounts not appearing in the trial balance are used on the work sheet, the titles will be added as they are needed. 

This adjustment is designated as Adjustment (c). In a similar way the value of the office supplies on hand was found to be $80; the entries designated (d) are therefore made on the work sheet. 

Since the insurance bought on October 3 gives protection for a full year, only the premium for one month, or 1/12 of a year, has been used up. Therefore 1/12 of the full premium, or $10, is considered to be an expense for the month; and the balance of the premium, or $110, is considered still to be an asset. Prepaid 

Insurance is therefore credited for $10 and Expired Insurance is debited by the entry designated (e) in the Adjustments columns. 

Since salaries are paid weekly and since the month of October ends on a Wednesday, the sales clerks and office workers have earned half a week's salary since the last salary payment and up to the end of the month. This salary that has accrued but is not yet due is a proper addition to the expenses of October and constitutes a current liability to the employees on October 31. The adjustment entries designated (f) increase the expenses and set up the liability.

The Trial Balance and the Adjustments columns now contain all the information required to prepare the statement of profit and loss and the balance sheet. To facilitate transfer of this information to the proper statement of profit and loss and balance sheet columns, it has been found helpful to combine the first four columns into a new pair of columns headed Adjusted Trial Balance.

Before the adjustments are combined with the trial balance, it is advisable to determine that the Adjustments columns are in balance. They are therefore footed and, if the two columns are not in balance, the entries are checked until the error is discovered and corrected.

The items in the Trial Balance and Adjustments columns are then combined. Cash is carried over to the Debit Adjusted Trial 

Balance column without change because this amount needs no adjustment and has received no adjustment. The beginning merchandise inventory, which is shown in the Debit Trial Balance column, is canceled by the amount entered in the Credit Adjustment column. The amount in the Debit Adjustment column represents the present asset merchandise inventory. This amount is brought into the Debit Adjusted Trial Balance column, whence it will be transferred to the Debit Balance Sheet column after the adjusted trial balance has been found to be in balance. The net value of the store supplies, that is, the original debit of $300 minus the credit adjustment of $140, is transferred to the Adjusted Trial Balance columns as a debit, which will later be transferred to the Debit Balance Sheet column. This procedure is followed until the Adjusted Trial Balance columns contain every item in the original trial balance and all the adjustments. 

After the Adjusted Trial Balance columns have been found to be in balance, the amounts in these columns are transferred to the proper columns under "Statement of Profit and Loss" and "Balance Sheet." The asset items are transferred to the Debit 

Balance Sheet column; the liability and proprietorship items to the 

Credit Balance Sheet column. The balance of the E. C. Martin, personal account represents a deduction from proprietorship. 

This amount might be subtracted from the original proprietorship and then only the net proprietorship extended into the Credit Balance Sheet column. As will be explained later, both the capital account and the personal account are used in certain entries, known as closing entries, that are based on the work sheet. For this reason both the balance of the capital account and that of the personal account are extended into the Balance Sheet columns. 

All the other items on the work sheet represent income and expenses, and are therefore extended into the Statement of Profit and Loss columns. Since the balance of the sales account represents income, it is entered in the Credit, or Income, column. The

ILLUSTRATION No. 10, TEN-COLUMN WORK SHEET

amounts of purchases, freight in, sales salaries, rent, miscellaneous expenses, store supplies used, etc., represent expenses and are therefore extended into the Debit, or Expense, column. The two amounts in the Adjustments columns on a line with the title "Profit and Loss Summary" represent the beginning and the ending merchandise inventories. Since both of these items are used in the statement of profit and loss, they are extended into the Statement of Profit and Loss columns. The debit amount represents a part of the cost of goods sold and is therefore entered in the Expense column. The credit amount represents a deduction from the cost of goods sold. Since, on the work sheet, it cannot be subtracted conveniently from the expenses, it is entered in the Income column. This procedure is permissible because increasing incomes gives the same final result as decreasing expense. 

After all the amounts have been extended into the Statement of Profit and Loss and the Balance Sheet columns, these columns are totaled. The difference between the total of the Expense column and the total of the Income column should be the amount of the net profit for the period. The net profit of Martin is $115. 

If the work has been completed correctly, this amount is also the difference between the total of the Assets column and the total of the Liabilities and Proprietorship column, because the increase in the proprietorship during the period plus the withdrawals should equal the profit for the period. The amount of the profit, $115, is added to the total of the Expense column to show that the expenses plus the net profit are equal to the income. The same amount is then added to the total of the Liabilities and Proprietorship column. The sum obtained by this addition is equal to the total value of the Debit Balance Sheet column. This procedure proves that the net profit for the period is equal to the sum of the increase in the proprietorship and the withdrawals. The totals of the four columns under the headings "Statement of Profit and Loss" and "Balance Sheet" are brought down to the same line, and the columns are ruled with double lines. The work sheet has now been completed and can be used as the basis for the preparation of the statements.

Classification of

Accounts 

The principal classes of accounts to be found in the ledger have been presented in this and the preceding chapter. The number of accounts in the ledger is determined by the number of items on the balance sheet and the statement of profit and loss. The order of accounts in the ledger should agree with the order of items on the accounting statements. The major classes of accounts are:

[Ch. 5 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 1. Asset accounts 2. Liability accounts 3. Proprietorship accounts 4. Summarizing account 

5. Income accounts 

6. Expense accounts

The first three classes provide for the recording of transactions affecting items on the balance sheet; the last two classes, for the recording of transactions affecting items on the statement of profit and loss. The summarizing account provides for a summarization of the income and the expense accounts. The first three groups are permanent, lasting as long as the business. The last two groups are temporary because they are closed at the end of each accounting period, and their existence is therefore limited to the length of the accounting period. The summarizing account has no time significance, for it is used to summarize the temporary proprietorship accounts at the end of each accounting period. 

1. The standard ruling for an account, shown on page 84, provides a number of columns. Explain the use of each column. 

2. The "T" form of account is easier to construct than the standard form. Why is it not better to use the "T" form? 

3. James Hyatt made an error in calculating his inventory at the end of the month and recorded its value as $3,500 when he should have recorded it as $3,000. (a) What effect did this error have on his profit for the month? (b) What effect did it have on the statement of profit and loss? 

4. If Hyatt did not discover the error but used the correct inventory at the end of the following month, what was the effect of the mistake (a) on his profit for the second month? (b) on the statement of profit and loss for that month? (c) on the balance sheet? 

5. Hyatt's accounts show the following information at the end of a fiscal period: sales, $5,000; beginning inventory, $2,000; purchases, $3,000. His inventory at the end of the period is valued at $1,000. (a) What is the cost of goods sold? the gross profit? (b) What additional information will be needed if Hyatt

wishes to know the net profit? (c) Where will he obtain this information? 

6. On May 1, J. H. King prepays $300 rent for the next three months. What effect will this payment have on his profit for May? 

7. When he makes withdrawals, does the proprietor anticipate the realization of a profit? Explain. 

8. C. J. Cole, a retail merchant, allows himself a salary of $300 a month. (a) How should this salary be recorded? (b) How does it affect his income? (c) Does the recording of the salary provide 

Cole with useful information? 

9. Some writers call expenses "expired assets." term by the use of examples. 

Explain this 10. The gross profit of W. S. Jones is greater than his expenses. 

Is this ratio typical of businesses? Explain. 

11. Which would be more accurate: (a) to report one half of the cost of store supplies purchased during a month as an expense on the statement of profit and loss prepared at the end of the half month, or (b) to take a physical inventory and to report as an expense the sum of the amount on hand at the beginning of the period and the amount purchased minus the ending inventory? 

LABORATORY PROBLEMS 

1. On April 2 John Withrow had the following assets invested in a retail shoe business: cash, $5,500; merchandise, $14,100. He owed $2,000 to the Bernard Shoe Company for merchandise purchased. During the first week of April he completed the following transactions: 

April 2. Paid the rent for three months, $130. 

2. Purchased merchandise for cash, $1,300. 

2. Purchased store supplies for $30 and office supplies for $25 in cash. 

2. Cash sales for the day amounted to $210. 

3. Paid the Bernard Shoe Company the $2,000 owed it. 

3. Cash sales for the day amounted to $175. 

4. Purchased merchandise for cash, $550. 

4. Paid $35 for advertising in a newspaper. 

4. Cash sales for the day amounted to $150. 

5. Purchased office stationery for $6. 

5. Withdrew $20 in cash for personal use.

[Ch. 5 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

5. Cash sales for the day amounted to $170. 

6. Paid $30 for advertising supplies. 

6. Cash sales for the day amounted to $180. 

7. Paid office salaries of $28 and sales salaries of $125. 

7. Withdrew $35 in cash for personal use. 

7. Cash sales for the day amounted to $235.

The inventories at the end of the week were: merchandise, $15,190; store supplies, $15; advertising supplies, $10; office supplies, $18. The value of the prepaid rent was $120. 

INSTRUCTIONS: (1) Open the necessary accounts. Record the assets, the liability, and the proprietorship of Withrow on April 2 and the foregoing transactions in the proper accounts. Allow eighteen lines for Cash, ten for Sales, and five for each of the other accounts. (2) Prepare a trial balance, a work sheet, a balance sheet as of April 7, and a statement of profit and loss for the week ending April 7.

CHAPTER VI BOOKKEEPING PROCEDURES 

Need for an Additional Previous chapters have explained how the Record transactions performed by a business may be analyzed and how their effect on the financial condition of the business is recorded in accounts. Although the transactions of a business can be recorded directly in accounts at the time of their occurrence, it has been found by experience that such a practice is usually not advisable. There are several reasons why this is true.

In the first place, the information that can be recorded in the account is meager. Since the account is a means by which information is summarized and classified, it does not give details. There are, however, some details of sufficient importance to make a record of them desirable. If the account were expanded to show all the details that might be desired, it would serve less well as a device for classifying and summarizing, which are its two chief functions.

Again, if transactions are recorded directly in accounts, errors made in the record cannot be corrected easily. As the debit and the credit resulting from a transaction are recorded in different accounts, a wrong amount entered in an account, or a debit or a credit placed on the wrong side of an account, is difficult to locate. 

For example, if Bob Payne purchases furniture and fixtures for $450 on account from W. B. Lewis, the asset Furniture and Fixtures is debited and the account with W. B. Lewis is credited for $450. If the furniture and fixtures account is debited for only $50, instead of $450, it will later be discovered that the debits and the credits are not equal. The error, however, will be quite difficult to locate.

If Leslie Ashby receives $175 as a payment on account from Ray Hadley, the cash account is debited and the account with Ray Hadley is credited. If the account with Roy Hadley is credited instead of the account with Ray Hadley, the latter cus- 108

Ch. 6] 109 

BOOKKEEPING PROCEDURES 

tomer will object when a statement is submitted to him, for he will not have been credited for the payment that he made. From the information shown in the account, it will be difficult to judge the validity of his objection and also to ascertain which account was actually credited. 

Finally, it is difficult and tedious to enter transactions one by one in accounts. In a large business this method of recording transactions is impossible, for only one person at a time can conveniently work on a ledger and many employees are required to make a record of all the transactions of such a business. 

For these reasons it is customary to make a preliminary record of transactions before they are entered in accounts. Such a record is called a record of original entry. It shows the debits and the credits resulting from transactions and, in addition, gives the descriptive data necessary to provide a permanent explanation of the transactions. From time to time the debits and the credits entered in the record are transferred to the accounts in the ledgers, which in turn constitute the basis of the accounting reports.

The General Journal 

Various kinds of records of original entry may be used, depending on the size of the business and the nature of the operations. The simplest type of record is that in which the transactions of a business are recorded in chronological order. This record is called the general journal or more briefly, the journal. It contains a daily record of current transactions. A standard form of ruling for the journal is shown in Illustration No. 11. 

GENERAL JOURNAL 

19-- DATE 

ACCOUNT TITLES AND EXPLANATION Feb 20 Purchases. 

MM Myers

Purchased merchandise on account L.P.

DEBIT 

AMOUNT CREDIT AMOUNT 13 65044 65044 

ILLUSTRATION No. 11, STANDARD RULING FOR THE JOURNAL

[Ch. 6 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

An entry is recorded in the general journal in the following manner: 

The year is written directly under the printed heading "Date." If this heading is not printed, the year is written in this space. 

The month of the first transaction is written in the first column at the extreme left. The name of the month is repeated only at the top of the new page or at the beginning of a new month.

The day of each transaction is written on the first line used by each transaction. It is repeated for each transaction regardless of the number of transactions completed on the same day. 

The names of the accounts to be debited and credited and the explanation of the transactions are written in the Account Titles and Explanation column. The account to be debited is written first, and the account to be credited is written on the following line and is indented about one-half inch. The explanation of the transaction is written on the next line and is indented about one inch. 

The debit amount is written in the Debit Amount column opposite the name of the account debited. The credit amount is written in the Credit Amount column opposite the name of the account credited. 

No entry is made in the column headed "Ledger Page" at the time that the entry is made in the journal. The debits and credits will later be transferred to the proper accounts in the ledger. At the time this transfer is made, the page on which the account appears in the ledger will be written in the Ledger Page column. 

This process is explained on page 112. 

The second transaction may be recorded in the journal on the line immediately below the first transaction. Most bookkeepers, however, prefer to leave a blank line between transactions. 

Illustration of the Journal 

On February 1, D. H. Hill invests $2,000 in cash and $5,000 in merchandise in a retail business. This investment may be recorded in the journal in the manner shown at the top of page 111.

The titles of the accounts to which the debits and the credits are to be transferred, and the amounts, are recorded in the proper columns. The explanation is then given. The journal entry thus provides a complete record of the transaction. This record is made conveniently and is useful if reference must be made to the transaction at any time in the future.

After these transactions have been recorded, the journal appears as in Illustration No. 12, with the exception of the 

Ledger Page column items, which are explained below. Posting The process by which the debits and credits recorded in the journal are transferred to accounts is called posting. It consists in transferring each amount in the Debit Amount column of the journal to the debit side of an account, and transferring each amount in the Credit Amount column of the journal to the credit side of an account. The account to which each item is to be posted may be determined from the title of the account stated in the journal. It is customary to post all debit items before beginning the posting of credit items so that there will be less likelihood of posting items to the wrong side of accounts.

When an item has been posted, the number of the page in the ledger containing the account to which the item has been transferred is placed in the Ledger Page column of the journal, as shown in Illustration No. 12. At the same time the number of the page in the journal from which the item has been posted is placed in the Folio column of the account. This process is called paging. It facilitates reference from the journal to the ledger, and vice versa. 

When the entries in the journal in Illustration No. 12 have been posted to the appropriate accounts, the ledger of Hill appears

Adjusting and Closing At the end of a fiscal period, after the work 

Accounts sheet, the balance sheet, and the statement of profit and loss have been prepared, entries may be made in the ledger so that the accounts will show the same information as the statements. The ledger of D. H. Hill, illustrated on pages 115 and 116, shows the merchandise inventory of February 1, the sales, the purchases, and the payments for office supplies, office salaries, rent, and miscellaneous expense. It does not, however, show the changes in the proprietorship that took place during the period of operation. At the end of the period the ledger must show the changes in the proprietorship just as they are shown in the statements. To accomplish this, adjusting entries and closing entries are made at the end of the fiscal period.

ILLUSTRATION No. 15, WORK SHEET OF D. H. HILL

Ch. 6] 119 

BOOKKEEPING PROCEDURES 

It is customary to close the ledger accounts once a year. 

Statements of profit and loss and balance sheets may be prepared more frequently. Such statements are usually called interim statements because they fall between annual statements. The accounting procedure described in the following pages comes less frequently than the preparation of statements. 

Adjusting Entries. The adjusting entries are those entries needed to bring the accounts up to date. They record the new merchandise inventory in place of the old, and also the decrease in the assets and the increase in the expenses that were not recorded currently.

Closing Entries. The closing entries are those entries needed to transfer the balances of the temporary accounts to a summary account so that the net profit or the net loss will finally be shown in the capital account. These entries do not record any new information in the ledger. Their purpose is to rearrange the information so that it will be more useful. 

Closing entries involve the use of the profit and loss summary account described in the previous chapter. All of the temporary proprietorship accounts are closed into the profit and loss summary account so that its balance shows the net profit or net loss for the period.

Adjusting the Accounts The adjusting entries of D. H. Hill are of D. H. Hill based on the information contained in the Adjustments columns of the work sheet. The first entries affect the merchandise inventory account. 

The new merchandise inventory which was found to be $4,741, is recorded by debiting the merchandise inventory account and crediting the profit and loss summary account. The merchandise inventory account is debited because this inventory represents an addition to the asset account. The profit and loss summary account is credited, for the ending inventory represents a deduction from the cost of goods sold and is therefore the opposite of an expense.

The merchandise inventory at the beginning of the fiscal period has become a part of the cost of goods sold. The merchandise inventory at the end of the period is an asset and should now be shown as an asset on the books. In order that the beginning

[Ch. 6 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

inventory will be removed from the merchandise inventory account, the profit and loss summary account is debited and the merchandise inventory account is credited for the value of the beginning inventory. The profit and loss summary account is debited because the beginning inventory is now considered as one of the costs of merchandise sold. The merchandise inventory account is credited to show that this amount is deducted from the asset merchandise inventory.

The inventory taken of the office supplies shows that the office supplies at the end of the fiscal period are worth $45; therefore office supplies worth $25 have been used. The expense resulting from the use of the supplies is recorded by debiting the office supplies used account for $25. The decrease in the value of the asset office supplies is recorded by a credit of the same amount to the office supplies account.

The journal entries required by the adjustment of Hill's merchandise inventory and office supplies accounts are made in the following manner:

It is now desirable to transfer the net result of business operations from the profit and loss summary account to the proprietor's personal account. The latter account will then show the net increase or decrease in the proprietorship. Balancing and Ruling the Accounts

Since the amounts entered in temporary proprietorship accounts represent increases or decreases in the proprietorship during a particular fiscal period, they must not be mixed with amounts applicable to the following period. Such a confusion of data would make difficult the preparation of the statement of profit and loss for the latter period. 

Therefore, after the accounts have been adjusted and closed, the amounts recorded on each side of each temporary proprietorship account are totaled, and double lines are drawn under the totals and across all columns except the Explanation columns. When accounts are closed in this manner, it is understood that all amounts

Ch. 6] 125 

BOOKKEEPING PROCEDURES 

above the double lines are to be ignored in accounting for future fiscal periods.

For the convenience of the bookkeeper in using asset, liability, and proprietorship accounts, these accounts may be balanced. 

To balance an account, the bookkeeper (1) adds the balance of the account to the smaller side; (2) records the totals of the two sides, which are now equal, on the same line; (3) rules the account as if it had been closed; and (4) brings down the balance of the account on the side that was originally the larger. 

After the accounts have been balanced and ruled, the ledger of D. H. Hill appears as in Illustration No. 16.

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES The Post-Closing Trial 

Balance The ledger of Hill has now been adjusted and closed. The balances of the accounts remaining open represent assets, a liability, and the proprietorship. All income and expense accounts, which served as temporary proprietorship accounts, have been closed. The amounts in these accounts will not be used in future reports; they may be used, however, in obtaining information regarding past increases or decreases in proprietorship. A post-closing trial balance is then prepared to make certain that the open accounts in the ledger are in equilibrium and are ready for the entries of the new fiscal period. 

This post-closing trial balance is shown in Illustration No. 17.

The Bookkeeping Cycle This and the preceding chapter have presented the sequence of bookkeeping procedures, beginning with transactions and concluding with the post-closing trial balance. This sequence of procedures is frequently referred to as the bookkeeping cycle or the accounting cycle. 

It will be made more evident in subsequent chapters, which deal with further descriptions of accounting technique. These formalized procedures are almost universally followed in accounting practice. 

It is advisable at this time to review the steps in the bookkeeping cycle. An appreciation of these steps is preliminary to an understanding of accounting principles. For convenience these steps are presented in eight parts as follows:

Ch. 6] 129 

BOOKKEEPING PROCEDURES 1. Journalizing 

2. Posting 

3. Taking a trial balance 

4. Determining the necessary adjustments 

5. Preparing the work sheet: 

6. Preparing the accounting statements 

7. Closing the books 

8. Taking a post-closing trial balance : recording transactions in the journal : transferring the entries in the journal to the ledger : summarizing the ledger accounts : calculating accounting information not recorded currently classifying information on columnar paper preparing the balance sheet and the statement of profit and loss from the work sheet : recording the adjusting and closing entries in the journal and posting them to the ledger : proving the accuracy of closing the books The items listed above constitute a cycle of accounting activities that are normally repeated each fiscal period. The first six steps may be repeated, without the last two steps, if it is desired to have interim statements without closing the books. Frequently, such statements are prepared monthly, whereas the books are closed annually. If statements are prepared monthly but the books are closed annually, the profit or loss shown on the statement of profit and loss at the end of the year will be the profit or loss for the entire year. If the profit or loss for the last month of the year is desired, the profit or loss for the previous eleven months, as shown by the interim statement, must be subtracted from the profit or loss for the entire year. In this way there are monthly cumulative statements and also the statements for the last individual month.

All of the eight steps are necessary to determine the net result of an accounting period and to prepare the books for the ensuing period. Subsequent chapters will deal with extensions and modifications of these procedures. But the matters treated in later chapters should be considered as supplementary procedures applied to the fundamental mechanisms already described.

CHAPTER VII CREDIT TRANSACTIONS Accounting for Credit 

The price agreed upon between the buyer and the seller in a business transaction represents the amount of cash to be paid by the buyer to the seller. 

If payment is to be made at the time the goods or services are delivered by the seller, the transaction is known as a cash transaction; if payment is to be made after the delivery of the goods, the transaction is known as a credit transaction. A credit transaction always looks forward to an additional transaction in which the credit granted is canceled by a cash payment.

The granting of credit by a seller greatly increases the scope of his operations and often facilitates the relations between him and the buyer. Purchasers are not required to have cash on hand at the time goods are delivered. A retail buyer may sell the goods he has bought and pay for them later out of the cash collected from his own sales. By delaying the time when payment is ex pected, the manufacturer or wholesaler finances the retailer. A very great deal of such financial assistance is found in our marketing system. Delayed payments prevail in many business transactions.

The consumer may find it more convenient to have a bill presented to him at the first of the month for purchases made during the month. If he receives a monthly salary, his cash income and cash outgo are thus made to coincide. The prices of some articles require the payment of such large sums of money that the purchaser would be unable to buy the articles if it were not for credit. For example, the purchase of a radio, an automobile, or a house would be impossible for most consumers unless credit could be arranged.

The accounting for credit transactions will be introduced in this chapter. This will take into consideration (1) credit granted by retailers to consumers, and credit received by retailers from their sources of supply, (2) credit evidenced by negotiable instru- 137

[Ch. 7 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES CCOUNTING 

ments involving interest, and (3) discounts taken by the purchaser and granted by the seller for payment before the end of the credit period. The subjects of interest and cash discounts are significant features of our credit system. 

Accounts with Customers

In preceding chapters the transactions presented have been principally cash transactions. In most businesses, however, it is customary to make sales on account. The customer gives his implied, oral, or written promise to pay at some future time for the goods he receives. 

In the retail trade it is usual for those who buy on account to pay on the first of the month for all purchases made during the previous month. In other lines of business, payments are made in accordance with the terms of sale, which are usually fixed by the custom of the trade. The customary terms may be changed by an agreement between the seller and the purchaser. 

The promise of a customer to pay is an asset of the seller, for it represents the right of the latter to obtain cash on a future date.

Implied and oral promises are called accounts receivable. Written promises made in the form of notes are called notes receivable. 

Accounts Receivable 

When sales are made on account, an accurate record of both the sales made to customers and the payments received from them must be kept. 

This record is obtained by keeping an account with each customer. 

For example, E. R. Bryan, a retail merchant, makes the following sales on account to Harold Boyd: 

February 1.. 10. 20. .$50.00 85.00 97.00 

Each of these sales gives Bryan a claim against the property of Boyd until Boyd pays the amount due. Each transaction is recorded by debiting the account of Harold Boyd and crediting sales. After the entries have been posted to the ledger, the ac counts appear as follows: 

19Feb. 10 20 192 248 

HAROLD BOYD 

50 00 85 00 97 00

19-- Feb. 102 10 20 248 50 00 85 00 97/00

CASH

Notes Receivable 

Few notes are received in payment of sales in a typical retail business. 

A note is usually received by a retail firm only when a customer cannot pay his account on the due date and gives his note to obtain an extension of time. Notes are also received by retail firms that sell their, products on long-term credit. For example, retail dealers in household furniture may receive notes from customers in payment for merchandise. These notes are usually paid by monthly installments. Wholesale firms and manufacturers receive notes more frequently than retailers, but their accounts receivable are usually much larger than their notes receivable. 

Harold Boyd is the maker and the payer of the note in Illustration No. 18. E. R. Bryan is the payee, that is, the one to whom the money is to be paid. The note is an asset of Bryan, for it gives him a claim against the property of Boyd. 

It is customary to record all notes receivable in one account since the proprietor has in his possession notes representing the total of this account. The amount due from each customer on a

Notes are commonly received from customers to extend the credit period originally granted. When such notes are given, the date of payment in cash is further delayed. Accounts with Creditors In most businesses the proprietor makes frequent purchases of merchandise on account. By doing this, he obtains property in the form of merchandise, but he does not make payment until a future time. He may purchase merchandise in this manner because it is the custom of the trade or because it is more convenient. He may sometimes purchase on account because this method provides him with additional property that can be used in operating the business. 

During the period of credit he has a larger amount of property, particularly cash and merchandise, for use in the business than he would have if he had made payment in cash at the time of the purchase. Liabilities of this nature, arising from the purchase of merchandise or other property on account, are known as accounts payable.

The proprietor may give notes to creditors in payment for merchandise or other property, or he may give notes to banks to obtain funds with which to purchase merchandise or other property. Notes are frequently issued to banks to obtain funds with which to pay accounts due creditors, or they may be issued directly to creditors. In such cases the liabilities created are known as notes payable.

Both accounts payable and notes payable are usually paid in cash. When a note falls due, a new note is sometimes given in exchange for it. This transaction does not change the nature of the liability; it only postpones the date of payment. Proprietors avoid this practice if possible, for it tends to injure their credit standing.

When a retailer purchases merchandise on account, he usually agrees to pay for it within a stated number of days after the date of purchase. Payments are made throughout the month as the different amounts become due. Ordinarily the retailer does not

Ch. 7] 143 

CREDIT TRANSACTIONS 

pay for all purchases made during a month on the first of the following month, in the manner in which the consumer pays him. 

Accounts Payable 

Most businesses make purchases on account from a number of creditors. The proprietor must have an accurate record of the purchases made from every creditor and the payments made to him, in order to be able to ascertain his liabilities accurately. This record is made by keeping an account with each creditor. For example, E. R. 

Bryan makes the following purchases on account from A. E. Funk: 

February 1.. 2 5. .$400.00 150.00 572.00 650.00 

Each transaction results in not only an increase in the purchases but also in a liability to Funk. After these transactions have been journalized and the entries have been posted to the ledger, the accounts appear as follows: 19Feb. 1 2233 1258

Purchases is debited in each case because each purchase on account has the same effect as a purchase for cash. If, on March 1, Bryan pays $772 on account to Funk, Funk's account will be debited for $772 and Cash will be credited for the same amount. These accounts appear as follows:

Notes Payable 

All notes payable are usually recorded in one account. If many notes are issued, a supplementary record that gives the details of the notes may be kept. The form of this record will be discussed in a later chapter. 

Here only the ledger record of notes payable will be discussed. 

When notes are issued to creditors in payment of accounts, the liability accounts payable are decreased and the liability notes payable are increased. These facts are recorded by debiting the accounts of the creditors to whom the notes were issued and by crediting the notes payable account. 

For example, when Bryan pays $772 in cash to Funk, he asks for an extension of the credit period on the balance of his account. 

Funk agrees to extend the credit period thirty days if Bryan will give him a note in settlement of the account. Bryan accordingly gives him a 30-day note. This transaction results in a decrease in the liability accounts payable and an increase in the liability notes payable. It is therefore recorded by a debit to the account of A. E. Funk and a credit to Notes Payable. These entries appear in the accounts as follows:

The method of recording transactions involving the issuance of notes to banks is discussed and illustrated later in this chapter. 

Ruling Customers' and Relations with customers and creditors 

Creditors' Accounts may continue for an indefinite period of time. The customers' and creditors' accounts provide a continuous record of these relations. Consequently numerous debits and credits are made to these accounts. If all the items in the account of a customer or a creditor were included at each time the balance was to be calculated, the preparation of the trial balance would soon become quite laborious. 

Since the customer making payment frequently pays an amount equal to one or more of the amounts that he owes, each credit to a customer's account usually equals one or more debits to that account. It is customary, therefore, to cancel the debits and the credits that offset one another by drawing a line on the debit side of the account at the point where the debits above the line equal the credits above the corresponding line on the opposite side. Since each debit to a creditor's account usually, equals one

[Ch. 7 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

or more credits to that account, the same form of ruling is used. 

When the balance of the account of a customer or a creditor is to be determined, only the items below these lines are considered. 

This practice reduces the mechanical work involved in finding the balances of the account. 

Harold Boyd
