ILLUSTRATION No. 20, CUSTOMER'S ACCOUNT CLOSED

Ch. 147 

CREDIT TRANSACTIONS Method of Showing Re ceivables and Payables on the Balance Sheet

The balance of each customer's account may be reported on the balance sheet as an asset. However, the number of customers' accounts is usually so large that the balances of these accounts are not listed on the balance sheet individually, but are reported as one total opposite the title "Accounts Receivable." When this plan is followed, a list of the balances of the customers' accounts may be attached to the balance sheet for the information of those who are interested in the amounts owed by individual customers. 

This list is known as a schedule of accounts receivable. 

The balance of each creditor's account may be reported on the balance sheet as a liability. If the number of creditors is large, they may be reported as one total opposite the title "Accounts Payable." As is done in the case of receivables, a list of the balances of the creditors' accounts may be attached to the balance sheet. This list is known as a schedule of accounts payable. 

Notes receivable and notes payable commonly precede accounts receivable and accounts payable on the balance sheet. 

Notes are considered more liquid than accounts. For example, a note receivable may be discounted at the wish of the holder and is thereby converted into cash. Accounts receivable, however, usually await the act of the customers before they become cash. 

Nature of Interest

In the credit granted, thus far, there has been no accounting for the cost of this credit. The allowance of credit is one of the services, among many, rendered by the seller to the buyer, and its cost is included in the sales price of the commodity. Credit, itself, is a commodity that is bought and sold. The price paid for credit is known as interest. For example, John Rust may borrow $1,500 in cash from

The First National Bank on June 1 and agree that he will pay for this amount on September 1 with interest at 6 per cent for the three-month period. Accordingly he pays $1,522.50 on September 

1, of which $22.50 is interest, the price he pays for the use of $1,500 from June 1 to September 1.

When a business borrows money, it ordinarily gives the lender a written promise to pay, or a note. The note may be interest-bearing or non-interest-bearing. If it is interest-bearing, it states that interest is to be paid at a fixed percentage. If it is non-interestbearing, no interest is charged until after the due date. Interest

[Ch. 7 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

may be charged at the legal rate for any time that the note remains unpaid after it is due.

The majority of notes issued by firms are issued to banks in procurement of loans. Such a note is interest-bearing if the interest is to be collected at maturity, or, in other words, when the note is due. The bank may require, however, that the interest on the note be paid at the time the loan is contracted. In this case no rate of interest is stated on the face of the note because no interest is to be collected at maturity. A more detailed explanation of interest on notes issued to banks is given later in this chapter. 

Most firms sell on account, and some of them receive notes in payment of either merchandise or accounts. An account receivable usually does not bear interest during the credit period, but it may bear interest from the termination of this period until the day when payment is made. This is true also of accounts payable. 

A note receivable, like a note payable, may be interest-bearing or non-interest-bearing. If a non-interest-bearing note is not paid at maturity, interest is charged after the date of maturity. 

The interest charged a firm by its creditors is an expense and is called interest cost. The interest that a firm charges its customers is an income and is called interest earned. It is evident that interest cost to one firm is interest earned to the firm to which it is paid. 

Recording Interest

Cost 

Like all other expenses, interest cost represents a deduction from proprietorship and might be carried directly to the debit side of the capital account of the proprietor. In harmony with the method of handling other expense items, it is preferable to record interest cost first in a separate account. The title of the account in which the expense due to interest is recorded is Interest Cost. 

For example: On July 1 C. K. Rust gives a creditor, R. F.

Burdick, a note for $800, due in 30 days and bearing interest at the rate of 6 per cent. This transaction is recorded by a debit of $800 to the account of Burdick and a credit of the same amount to Notes Payable. The entry is as follows: 

July 1 

R. F. Burdick.

Notes Payable. 

Issued a 30-day note, interest 6% from date, in payment of the account with R. F. Burdick. 80000 800 00

Interest Cost..

Burdick. 800 00 400 804 00 The net result of these entries is a debit of $800 to the account of the creditor; a debit of $4 to the expense account Interest 

Cost; and a credit of $804 to Cash. 

Recording Interest Earned 

Interest earned is compensation for credit granted to others. It is an addition to the income of the firm that receives it. This fact is shown by crediting the interest to the interest earned account. 

For example: On July 1 John Ritter receives from a debtor, Wm. Linder, a note for $1,000, due in 30 days and bearing interest at the rate of 6 per cent. This transaction is recorded by a debit to Notes Receivable and a credit to the account of Linder. The entry appears in Ritter's journal as follows:

Wm. Linder.. 

Received a 30-day note, interest at 

6%, to apply on account. 1,000/00 TH 1,000 00 

On July 31 Ritter receives $1,005 from Linder in payment of the note and interest. This transaction is recorded by a debit of $1,005 to Cash, a credit of $1,000 to Notes Receivable, and a credit of $5 to Interest Earned. The entry appears in Ritter's journal as follows:

The net result of these entries is a debit of $1,005 to Cash, a credit of $1,000 to the debtor's account, and a credit of $5 to 

Interest Earned.

Discount on Notes 

Banks may collect interest in advance on the loans they make to their customers. 

A bank deducts the amount of interest from the face of the note given it by the customer and gives him credit for the remainder. 

This procedure is called discounting a note. The deduction made by the bank when it discounts a note is called discount. Discount is merely interest paid in advance; hence it is charged to the interest cost account. 

For example: On September 1 John Ritter discounts his own thirty-day note for $1,000 at the First National Bank. The bank charges him 6 per cent interest, which amounts to $5. It therefore gives him credit for $995 for his $1,000 note. Ritter's transaction with his bank is recorded in his books by debiting Cash for $995, debiting Interest Cost for $5, and crediting Notes Payable for $1,000. The entry is as follows: 

Sept. 1 Cash... 

Interest Cost..

Notes Payable... 

Discounted a 30-day note at 6% at the bank. 995 00 500 1,000/00

On October 1 Ritter pays the bank $1,000 in settlement of the note. This transaction is recorded by a debit of $1,000 to 

Notes Payable and a credit of $1,000 to Cash. The entry is as follows: 

Oct. 1 Notes Payable. 

Cash... 

Paid the note discounted at the First 

National Bank on September 1. 

1,000 00 

1,000 00 fo |fo|| The net result of these entries is a debit of $5 to the expense account Interest Cost and a credit of $5 to the cash account. 

Sometimes a business discounts the notes of customers at its bank. If a customer's note is non-interest-bearing, the bank will deduct, from the face of the note, interest from the date of dis-

Ch. 151 

CREDIT TRANSACTIONS 

count to the date of maturity. It will then give the owner the difference. If the note is interest-bearing, the bank will add, to the face of the note, the interest from the date of issue to the date of maturity in order to find the maturity value of the note and will then deduct from this maturity value the discount from the date of discount to the date of maturity. It will then give the owner the difference, that is, the proceeds. 

For example: On November 9 John Ritter receives from a customer, J. W. Stone, the latter's 90-day, non-interest-bearing note of $1,125 in payment of a sale on October 10. This transaction is recorded by a debit of $1,125 to Notes Receivable and a credit of $1,125 to the account of J. W. Stone. This entry appears in the journal as follows: 

Nov. 9

Notes Receivable.. 

J. W. Stone... 

Received a 90-day, non-interestbearing note from J. W. Stone in payment of the sale on October 10. 1,125 00 1,125,00 

At the end of 30 days Ritter needs additional cash and therefore discounts this note receivable at his bank. The bank must wait 60 days from the date of discount to obtain the face of the note, $1,125; consequently, it deducts discount, or interest, for 

60 days from the face of the note. If the rate of discount is 6 per cent, the amount of discount is $11.25. Ritter therefore receives credit for $1,113.75 for the discounted note. This transaction is recorded by a debit of $1,113.75 to Cash, a debit of $11.25 to

Interest Cost, and a credit of $1,125 to Notes Receivable. The entry is as follows: 

Dec. 9 Cash.... 

Interest Cost..

Notes Receivable. 

Discounted the 90-day, non-interestbearing note, dated November 9, that was received from Stone. 1,113 75 1125 1,125 00 The problem is slightly different if the note bears interest. 

For example: On November 10 Ritter receives from Harvey

At the end of 90 days this note will be worth $1,218, the face plus interest. If the note is discounted at the end of 30 days, the bank will take into consideration the fact that it will receive $1,218 when the note is due. It will therefore base the discount on that amount. If the rate of discount is 6 per cent, the discount on $1,218 for 60 days will be $12.18. When this amount ($12.18) is subtracted from the value of the note at maturity ($1,218), the proceeds, or the amount that the bank will pay Ritter for the note, will be $1,205.82. It is common in transactions such as the one illustrated above to consider only the excess of the net proceeds over the face value of the note as interest earned. The item of $12.18 is not, in reality, interest cost, but rather an adjustment of the overstatement of interest earned. If the note had been held until maturity, the interest earned would have amounted to $18. Discounting the note, however, resulted in a net gain of only $5.82. This amount is therefore recorded as interest earned. The computation of the proceeds of the note would be made exactly as shown in the tabulation above. When a business discounts a note receiv- Discounted able, it is responsible to the bank for the payment of the note if the note is not paid at maturity by the maker. Discounting a note therefore results in what is known as a contingent liability. If, on April 11 John Ritter wishes to record the contingent liability incurred when he discounts a $2,000 90-day, non-interest-bearing note dated March 12 which he received from E. L. Hansell, he will record the transaction as follows: 

April 11 Cash... 

Interest Cost..

Discounted at 6% a 90-day, noninterest-bearing note dated March 

12 and received from E. L. Hansell. 1,980 00 20 00 2,000,00

It will be observed that a new account known as Notes Receivable Discounted is used. The note remains in the notes receivable account as an asset, and Notes Receivable Discounted appears in the ledger as a contingent liability. The contingent liability should be reported on the balance sheet as a deduction from the asset.

---

[Ch. 7 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

The use of the notes receivable discounted account gives the same final result as that which would have been obtained had 

Notes Receivable been credited when each note was discounted.

Using the former account has the theoretical advantage of showing on the books the contingent liability until the note is paid. If the face of each of the notes discounted is large in proportion to the total current assets, and if the management considers there is a possibility that several of the notes will not be paid, it is advisable to use the notes receivable discounted account in order that the records may at all times show the contingent liability. 

If, on the other hand, a large number of small notes are discounted, the majority of which will probably be paid, it is not worth while to undertake the extra work required to show the notes receivable discounted in a separate account. When reports are prepared, the total amount of the notes receivable discounted can be readily determined from an examination of the detailed record of notes, which is explained in Chapter XIV. 

Businesses that make a practice of discounting customers' notes usually discount a considerable number of comparatively small notes, most of which they believe to be good. Furthermore, they prepare statements at infrequent intervals, usually annually. 

The notes receivable discounted account is seldom used since maintaining it is more difficult than obtaining, at the end of each fiscal period, the same information from a separate record of notes. 

Dishonored Notes 

If a note that has been discounted is not paid when it is due, the bank will notify the one who discounted it. The latter, as the indorser of the note, will then have to pay for it. If the J. W. Stone note that was discounted on December 9 (page 151) is dishonored, Ritter will have to pay for it and will make the following entry: 

Feb. 9 J. W. Stone... Cash.. 1,125 00 1,125 00 

Paid the dishonored note of J. W. 

Stone, which was dated November 9 and was due on February 8.

If the note had been interest-bearing, the cash payment would have included interest from the date of the note to maturity, and the debit to J. W. Stone would have included the interest.

Ch. 

155 

CREDIT TRANSACTIONS 

If Ritter were required to pay a fee at the time the notice of dishonor was given, the fee would also be charged to J. W. Stone. 

If a note that has been recorded as a contingent liability in the notes receivable discounted account is dishonored, the entry will be different from that given above. The one who discounted the note must show that he has discharged the contingent liability recorded when the note was discounted. For example, if the E. L. Hansell note of March 12, which was discounted on 

April 11 (page 153) is dishonored, the entry required at the time the note is paid will be: 

June 10 E. L. Hansell.. Cash.

The effect of these entries is to charge back to E. L. Hansell the amount paid for the dishonored note and to cancel the contingent liability, which no longer exists. 

Method of Ascertaining The rate of interest for a year is stated as Interest a percentage of the face of the note. Thus, if the rate is 6 per cent; the face of the note, $1,000; and the time, one year, the interest is six one-hundredths (.06) of $1,000, or $60 a year.

The time involved in ordinary commercial loans is usually less than one year; consequently the greater portion of practical interest problems require ascertaining the interest for a fraction of a year. To facilitate these calculations, it is customary to regard a year as 360 days. In figuring interest on notes, businessmen usually consider a month to be 30 days, or one-twelfth of a year. When banks discount notes, however, they usually count the exact number of days until maturity, although 360 days are considered one year. For example, a note that is discounted on 

July 1 and will be due on September 1 is discounted for 62 days, or sixty-two three hundred and sixtieths (62/360) of a year.

[Ch. 7 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

To illustrate, assume that goods amounting to $1,200 were sold to Edwin D. Alford on June 1. Alford gave in payment his non-interest-bearing note for $1,200, payable in three months. 

The holder discounted the note on July 1. The bank deducted discount for 62 days, the time from the date of discount to September 1, the due date of the note. If the rate of discount was 6 per cent, the discount for 62 days on $1,200 amounted to $12.40.

There are various ways of calculating interest. One method is illustrated below: 

Interest at 6 per cent a year on a note running 60 days, or % of a year, is equal to 1 per cent interest on the principal. The following calculations may be made in determining the interest on $1,200 for 62 days at 6 per cent: 

Interest on $1,200 for 60 days at 6% = $12.00 

Interest on $1,200 for 6 days at 6% 

Interest on $1,200 for 2 days at 6% 1.20 .40 

Interest on $1,200 for 62 days at 6% = $12.00+$0.40 = $12.40

The preceding calculations show that, by moving the decimal point in the principal two places to the left, 1 per cent of that amount, or the interest for 60 days at 6 per cent, is found. When the decimal point is moved three places to the left, the interest for 6 days is determined. The interest for 2 days is found by taking one-third of the interest for 6 days. The interest for 62 days at 6 per cent is then calculated by adding the interest for 

60 days and that for 2 days.

It is often necessary to ascertain the interest for a certain period at a rate greater or smaller than 6 per cent. In such a case the interest for the period at 6 per cent is determined first. The proper amount to be added to or subtracted from this amount is then computed, and the interest at the given rate is thus ascertained. 

For instance, if, in the example given above, the interest rate were 8 per cent, the interest on $1,200 for 62 days at 6 per cent would be determined first. The interest at an additional 2 per cent would then be found by taking one-third of the interest at 6 per cent. The two amounts would then be added. The calculation of the interest in this case is shown by the following table: 

Interest on $1,200 for 62 days at 6% = $12.40 

Interest on $1,200 for 62 days at 2% 4.13 

Interest on $1,200 for 62 days at 8% $12.40+$4.13 = $16.58

Ch. 

157 

CREDIT TRANSACTIONS 

If the rate of interest in the preceding example were only 5 per cent, one-sixth of the interest at 6 per cent would be determined and this amount would be subtracted from the interest at 6 per cent. The calculation of the interest in this case is shown by the following table: 

2.07 

Interest on $1,200 for 62 days at 6% = $12.40 

Interest on $1,200 for 62 days at 1% 

Interest on $1,200 for 62 days at 5% $12.40-$2.07 $10.33 

Credit Periods and Cash In extending credit to customers, business- Discounts men make the credit terms definite in order to promote better understanding of the arrangement. The time allowed for payment is therefore made a part of the sale transaction. The time of payment may be the first of the following month, 30 days hence, 60 days or whatever time has become the prevailing practice. If goods are sold by the purchaser in a short time, the credit period tends to be short; in slower-moving goods, it tends to be longer.

The expression n/30 means that payment is to be made in thirty days, n/60 that payment is to be made in sixty days. To encourage payment before the expiration of the credit period, creditors have developed the practice of offering a special inducement in the shape of a cash discount for early payment. Thus, the expression 2/10, n/60 means that while the credit period established by custom of the trade is sixty days, the debtor may deduct 2 per cent of the amount of the bill if payment is made in ten days. This deduction is known as a cash discount. 

The expression "1/10, n/30" is to be read: "1 per cent discount in ten days, net thirty days." It means that the buyer will be given a discount, known as a cash discount, if he pays the amount within ten days of the date of the invoice. If the invoice is not paid within this period, the gross amount will be due in thirty days. Cash discounts granted to customers are known as sales discounts; those received from creditors, as purchases discounts. 

The terms are given on each invoice so that the seller and the buyer will know whether the invoice is subject to a discount. 

If the customer makes payment within the discount period, sales discount becomes an expense of the seller. For example, if goods amounting to $600 are sold to R. K. House by John Ritter

[Ch. 7 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

on June 14, terms 2/10, n/60, and if House on June 22 sends his check less the discount in payment, the entry on Ritter's books is: 

June 22 Cash.. 

Sales Discount. 

R. K. House. 

Received check from R. K. House for invoice of June 14 less 2% discount. 58800 1200 600 00 

On the other hand, if Ritter purchases goods on June 13 from G. L. Fax amounting to $1,000, terms 2/10, n/60, and makes payment within 10 days, he may deduct 2%, or $20, from his check. Assuming he sends his check for $980 on June 23, the entry on Ritter's books is:

June 23 G. L. Fax……. 

Purchases Discount. 

Cash. 

Paid Fax for invoice of June 15 less 2%. 

1,000 00 20/00 980 00 In this case the payment, 50 days before the account comes due, produces an income of $20 for the one making payment because it saves him that amount of money. 

Treatment of Cash 

Discounts 

Accountants are not unanimous in their opinions regarding the correct treatment of cash discounts. Some accountants believe that the cost of the merchandise purchased is the invoice price less cash discount. 

They hold that discounts not taken must be accounted for, and that those taken are not to be recorded. For example, on April 15

E. S. Olive purchases $1,000 worth of merchandise on terms of 

2/10, n/60 from H. S. Reed. The entry, which is shown here in journal form, is as follows: 

April 15 Purchases.. 

and af 

H. S. Reed.

Purchased merchandise on terms of 

2/10, n/60. 980 00 980 00 +|+||

Ch. 7] 159 

CREDIT TRANSACTIONS 

If this invoice is not paid within the discount period, the following entry, which is shown here in journal form, will be made when the invoice is paid: 

June 13 H. S. Reed.

Purchases Discount Expense. 

Cash.

Paid the invoice of H. S. Reed. The discount was not taken since the invoice was not paid within the discount period. 980 00 20 00 1,000 00 The method results in recording as an expense the purchases discounts not taken, and in not recording those that have been taken. The purchases account is debited for only the amount of the invoice less the cash discount. 

The more common treatment is that used in this text and illustrated on previous pages in this chapter. Under this method the invoice price and the credit terms are accepted as a business condition not requiring adjustment. If an invoice is paid within the discount period, and the amount paid is therefore less than the total of the invoice, the purchases discount is recorded as a part of the entry. This discount represents a financial earning because the debtor was able to pay before the end of the period allowed him.

The saving made in taking advantage of cash discounts is considerably more than the interest cost on current capital. 

Terms of 2/10, n/60 mean a saving of $24 on a $1,200 invoice, compared with an interest cost of $10 on a like amount borrowed at 6 per cent for a 50-day period. The benefits to the business offering the discount are the receipt of payment before the end of the credit period, a saving due to the fact that statements need not be mailed and the impossibility of a loss due to a bad debt. 

The discount represents income to the business that is financially able to take advantage of it. Hence a cash discount is beneficial to both buyer and seller.

Method of Reporting 

Interest and Cash Dis counts on the Statement of Profit and Loss

Interest earned and purchases discount are usually reported on the statement of profit and loss as other income; interest cost and sales discount, as other expense.

[Ch. 7 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

Items listed on the statement under the headings "Other Income" and "Other Expense" affect the net profit or the net loss of the enterprise. They are considered apart from items representing the ordinary operations of the business and are therefore commonly placed at the end of the statement. A common method of reporting cash discounts on the statement of profit and loss is illustrated below.

1. The balance sheet of Harmon Wold, a retail grocer, on December 31, 19--, showed notes receivable of $5,000 and accounts receivable of $5,000. On the same date the balance sheet of Peter 

Kincaid, a competitor, showed notes receivable of $1,500 and accounts receivable of $8,000. Which balance sheet indicated the more desirable condition? 

2. If Wold and Kincaid were dealers in farm machinery, would your answer be the same?

[Ch. 7 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

3. C. J. Cockley, a retail merchant, does not keep an account with each customer but files a copy of each sales ticket under the customer's name. When a payment is made, the corresponding sales ticket is removed from the files, stamped "Paid," and filed in a "Paid" file. Do you think this method is satisfactory? Why? 

4. Cockley does not keep an account with each creditor but files all invoices received in an "Unpaid" file. When a payment is made, the invoice is removed, stamped "Paid," and filed in a "Paid" file. What is your opinion of this method? 

5. Robert Adams, a retail merchant, contends that if all notes receivable are recorded in one account, the same plan should be followed in recording accounts receivable. Do you agree? 

6. M. D. Harder, a dealer in real estate, sells a lot to W. P. 

Rilling for $5,000 and receives in payment $2,000 in cash and a note due two years from the date of the sale. How should this note be shown on the balance sheet of Harder, prepared one month after the date of the sale? 

7. E. H. Kramer needs additional funds with which to finance current purchases. He therefore borrows from the bank, giving it a 60-day note for $1,000, which is discounted at the rate of 6 per cent. He also gives a creditor a 60-day, 6 per cent note for $1,000 to apply on account. (a) In what way will the entries to record these two transactions differ? (b) Give the journal entry for each. 

8. In sixty days Kramer pays both of the notes mentioned in 

Problem No. 7. (a) In what ways will the entries to record the transactions differ? (b) Give the journal entry for each. 

9. On October 7 E. H. Kramer receives from one of his customers a 60-day, non-interest-bearing note for $500, dated October 6. 

How should this transaction be recorded in the journal? 

10. On October 21 Kramer discounts the note received on October 7 (Problem No. 9) at 6 per cent. Give the journal entry required to record this transaction. 

11. On October 22 Kramer also discounts a 90-day, 6 per cent note for $1,000, dated September 30. The rate of discount is 7 per cent. Give the journal entry required to record this transaction.

14. E. H. Cole receives two 60-day, 6 per cent notes for $1,000. 

At the end of 30 days he discounts one at 6 per cent. At the end of 60 days payment is refused on both. How do you recommend that the bookkeeper of Cole record these facts? He is uncertain how to record these facts because he previously recorded interest earned on the note receivable that was discounted but not on the other note. Should he now record interest earned on the other note, or should he write off the earning that was recorded but will never be realized? 

15. O. B. Bell wishes to borrow $10,000 for 90 days. The bank will discount his note at 7 per cent. He can also borrow $10,000 from an acquaintance if he will give him a 72 per cent interestbearing note. (a) Which is the better proposition? (b) What is the difference in interest cost?

Sixty days later the note is collected with interest at 

8 per cent from the date of maturity. Give the journal entries required to record these transactions.

Need for Additional 

Journals CHAPTER VIII SPECIAL JOURNALS 

All the transactions of a business may be recorded in the ordinary two-column journal, which has been used in previous chapters. Transactions are usually classified, however, according to their nature, and those that recur frequently are recorded in special journals. This method has two advantages:

First, the amount of space required for the record of each transaction is reduced. When transactions are recorded in chronological order in the general journal, a rather complete explanation of each entry must be written. The record of an ordinary transaction requires several lines in the general journal. When transactions are classified, however, and all those of one kind are recorded in a separate book, it is possible to avoid repeating much information that is the same in all cases. The record can therefore be made much briefer, and a great amount of labor eliminated in journalizing and posting. 

Second, it facilitates the division of labor necessary in a large organization. If the transactions are recorded in different books of original entry, one clerk may record nothing but sales; another, nothing but purchases; another, nothing but cash receipts; and still another, nothing but cash payments. Such recording requires less experienced clerks than are needed when only one journal is kept. The chief accountant needs a complete and thorough understanding of accounting, but his assistants who keep the special journals need only a minimum of accounting training. 

Sales on Account 

During the month of June, 19––, R. E.

Rowley, a retail merchant, makes the following sales on account: 

June 5. W. E. Tewson, Garden City, terms on account.. $ 149.25 

12. M. C. Madsen, Winnetka, terms 30 days... 

23. W. E. Tewson, Garden City, terms on account.. 

28. M. E. Dolan, Racine, terms 30 days.... 1,729.50 305.00 624.50 168

If these sales are recorded in the general journal, the entries will be as follows: 

June 5

W. E. Tewson, Garden City. 

149 25 

Sales... 

149 25

Sold merchandise, terms on account. 28

624 50 Sales.. 624 50 

Sold merchandise, terms 30 days.

If only sales on account are entered on one page, the entries can be made much more efficiently. The credit to the sales account need not be indicated for each sale, but the sales account can be credited for the total of all sales. In other words, a compound entry with numerous debits to the accounts of individual customers and one credit to the sales account can be made. The explanation of each transaction can be brief, for the appearance of the entry indicates that the transaction is a sale on account. 

Furthermore, the information to be recorded about all sales on account is uniform. Special columns that indicate the nature of the information can therefore be used. The use of these special columns further tends to reduce the length of the explanations. 

The Sales Journal 

A common form of the sales journal is shown on the following page. Since nothing but sales on account is recorded in this book, the sales account is not credited for each amount, but is credited for the total of the sales. When the entries are posted, the account of a customer is debited for the amount of each sale in the same manner as if the entry had been made in the general journal. The sales account is credited in one amount for $2,808.25. After this entry has been posted, the balance of the sales account is the same as it would have been if each sale on account had been credited to the account separately. The equality of the debits and the credits is maintained because there is one credit to the sales account that is equal to the sum of the four debits to the customer's accounts.

ILLUSTRATION No. 22, SALES JOURNAL 

Form of the Sales Different forms of the sales journal are Journal used because different information concerning sales is desired. The form shown in Illustration No. 22 is simple and commonly used. The use of the columns in this sales journal is, in the main, apparent. The column headed "Sale No." gives the numbers of the itemized lists of the merchandise sold to the customers. These lists are known as sales invoices or sales tickets. When goods are sold, a sales invoice showing, among other things, the kind, the amount, and the price of the goods sold is sent to the customer. A copy of this invoice is retained, and from it the information to be recorded in the sales journal is obtained. 

These invoices are numbered consecutively and, after the entries have been recorded in the sales journal, are filed so as to be available for future reference. The number entered in the Sale No. column serves to identify the entry with the sales invoice to which it pertains, in case a future comparison is desired. The purpose of the column headed "L. P." is to show the pages of the ledger to which the various amounts have been posted. 

Sometimes the individual items of goods sold, as well as the price of each item, are listed in the sales journal. This procedure requires much additional work and serves no particular purpose because both the purchaser and the seller have a copy of the invoice or the sales ticket. Since the seller enters the number of the invoice or the sales ticket in the sales journal, he can find the invoice or the ticket at any time and can obtain from it any detailed information that he desires.

The record of sales is known by various names, such as the sales journal, the sales register, the sales book, and the sales record.

Ch. 8] 171 

SPECIAL JOURNALS 

It is, in reality, a journal since transactions are recorded in it so that their effect on the accounts will be shown. The sales journal will be used as a record of sales of merchandise only. For the present, only sales on account will be entered in the sales journal. 

The use of the sales journal not only decreases the work of recording the transactions but also decreases by almost one half the work of posting the entries for the sales transactions. The more numerous the transactions with sales, the more advantageous is the use of the sales journal. 

Posting from the Sales Journal

Posting from the sales journal consists in (1) transferring the separate items shown in the Amount column to the debit side of the customers' accounts indicated in the Account Debited column and (2) transferring the total of the sales, which is obtained by totaling the items in the Amount column, to the credit side of the sales account. The debits to the accounts of the customers should be posted daily so that the status of a customer's account can be determined at any time. In the case of a wholesale business, from which customers purchase large quantities of merchandise on account from time to time, this daily posting is especially important. Whether the one responsible for granting credit to customers will permit a further granting of credit to a customer will depend to a considerable degree upon the amount now owed by the customer and the promptness of past payments. The one granting credit will expect to obtain this information from the customer's account; but if all the charges to the account have not been posted, he may be deceived by what the account shows. The total of the sales is usually posted to the sales account monthly.

The ledger page column in the sales journal and the folio column in the ledger are used in the same way as when transactions are posted from the general journal, except that the letter S precedes the page number in the folio column of the ledger to show that the posting was made from the sales journal. 

The method of ruling the sales journal after it has been posted is shown in Illustration No. 22, page 170.

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

in recording the transaction in the journal and in posting from the special journal to the ledger.

ILLUSTRATION No. 23, PURCHASES JOURNAL 

The Purchases Journal A common form of the purchases journal is shown in Illustration No. 23. Since nothing but purchases on account is entered in this book, the purchases account need not be debited for each purchase, but can be debited for the total. When the entries are posted, the account of a creditor is credited for the amount of each purchase in the same manner as if the entry had been made in the general journal. The total of all the purchases, $5,092.48, is carried to the debit side of the purchases account. This account then has the same balance as it would have had if each purchase had been debited to it separately. It is apparent that the equality of the debits and the credits is maintained since the one debit to the purchases account is equal to the sum of the four credits to the creditors' accounts.

Form of the Purchases 

Journal

The purpose of the purchases journal is to record the purchases made and the obligations due the creditors from whom the purchases were made. The

Ch. 8] 173 

SPECIAL JOURNALS 

record of purchases is known by various names, such as the purchases journal, the purchases book, the purchases register, the purchases record, and the invoice record. Regardless of its name, it is, in reality, a journal since transactions are recorded in it so that their effect on accounts will be shown. 

The purchases journal is here considered a record of purchases of merchandise only. In some cases all purchases made by a business, whether of merchandise, fixed assets, supplies, or services, are recorded in the same journal. Such a record is usually called an accounts payable register or a voucher register. Its construction and use will be discussed in a later chapter. For the present, only purchases of merchandise on account will be recorded in the purchases journal. 

The form of the purchases journal depends to a considerable degree upon the size and the nature of the business and the amount of information the owner desires concerning his purchases. The information usually desired concerning purchases can be obtained from a form such as that shown in Illustration No. 23. 

The use of the columns is, in the main, apparent. The column headed "Pur. No." gives the numbers of the itemized lists of merchandise purchased, which are known as purchases invoices. When goods are purchased, the purchaser receives from the seller a written invoice showing, among other things, the kind, amount, and price of the goods purchased. From this invoice the information to be recorded in the purchases journal is obtained. These invoices are numbered consecutively and, after the entries have been recorded in the purchases journal, are filed so as to be available for future reference. The number entered in the Pur. No. column serves to identify the entry with the purchase invoice to which it pertains, in case a future comparison is desired. Such a comparison would be made if a creditor objected to the amount paid him or to the terms upon which he was paid. The information entered in the columns headed "Date of Invoice" and "Terms" indicate when an invoice will be due. The purpose of the column headed "L. P." is to show the pages of the ledger on which appear the accounts named in the Account Credited column. 

Posting from the Purchases Journal shown in the Amount

Posting from the purchases journal consists in (1) transferring the separate items column to the credit side of the creditors'

[Ch. 8 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

accounts indicated in the Account Credited column and (2) transferring the total of the purchases, which is obtained by adding the items in the Amount column, to the debit side of the purchases account. Posting to the creditors' accounts should be made daily so that the status of a creditor's account can be determined at any time by reference to the ledger. This posting is frequently made at the same time that the entry is recorded in the purchases journal. The total of purchases is usually posted to the purchases account monthly.

As the posting is completed, the number of the ledger page is entered in the ledger page column of the purchases journal in the same manner as when the posting is made from the general journal. 

The number of the page of the purchases journal is placed in the folio column of each account. In the ledger the page number is usually preceded by the letter P to show that the entry came from the purchases journal.

The method of ruling the purchases journal after it has been posted is shown in Illustration No. 23, page 172.

Cash Receipts 

During the month of June, 19——, R. E. 

Rowley completes the following transactions involving cash receipts:

June 1. Invested $3,000 in cash in a retail drygoods business. 

11. Received $100 on account from W. E. Tewson.

28. Received $1,221.33 from cash sales to date. 

30. Received $354.25 from W. E. Tewson in full of account.

If these cash receipts are entered in a special cash receipts journal, the debit to the cash account need not be indicated for each cash receipt, but the cash account can be debited for the total of the receipts. In other words, a compound entry with one debit to Cash and numerous credits to other accounts can be made. 

Special columns will also facilitate the writing of explanations. 

The Cash Receipts 

Journal A common form of the cash receipts journal is shown in Illustration No. 24. Since nothing but cash receipts is recorded in this book, the cash account is not debited for each amount, but is debited for the total

Ch. 8] 175 

SPECIAL JOURNALS 

of the receipts. When the cash receipts journal is posted, each item entered in the Amount column is posted to the credit side of the account named in the Account Credited column. The total cash received, $7,339.73, which is the total of the Amount column, is transferred to the debit side of the cash account. The cash account will then show the same result as it would have shown had each cash receipt been debited to it separately in the general journal.

ILLUSTRATION No. 24, CASH RECEIPTS JOURNAL 

The credits to the accounts of customers are usually posted daily so that the status of a customer's account can be ascertained at any time. The remaining credits may also be posted daily, or only monthly if that method is preferred. The cash account is debited for the total cash receipts at the time a trial balance is to be taken, usually at the end of each month. 

The ledger page column of the cash receipts journal is used in the same manner as that of the other books of original entry. 

The numbers in the ledger page column of the ledger, however, are preceded by the letter C to show that the amounts have been posted from the cash receipts journal. The equality of the debits and the credits is maintained in the ledger since there is one debit to the cash account that is equal to the total of the credits posted separately to the other accounts.

If these cash payments are entered in a special cash payments journal, the credit to the cash account need not be indicated for each cash payment, but the cash account can be credited for the total of the payments. In other words, a compound entry with one credit to Cash and numerous debits to other accounts can be made.

The Cash Payments A common form of the cash payments Journal journal is shown in Illustration No. 25.

When the cash payments journal is posted, the separate items shown in the Amount column are posted to the accounts indicated in the Account Debited column. The total cash paid, $2,322.85, which is the total of the Amount column, is credited to the cash account. The cash account will then show the same result as it would have shown had each payment been credited to it separately. 

The debits to the accounts of creditors are usually posted daily so that the status of a creditor's account can be ascertained at any time. The remaining debits may also be posted daily, or only monthly if that method is preferred. The cash account is credited for the total cash payments at the time a trial balance is to be taken, usually at the end of each month. The source of the entries is indicated in the ledger by the letter C placed before the page numbers in the folio column. ILLUSTRATION No. 25, CASH PAYMENTS JOURNAL 

the nature of the entry clearly indicates whether the entry was posted from the cash receipts journal or the cash payments journal. 

The Cashbook 

The cash receipts journal and the cash payments journal sometimes are joined to form a cashbook. In this case a page for the recording of cash receipts is immediately followed by a page for the recording of cash payments, these pages alternating throughout the book. 

The cash receipts are recorded on the left-hand pages of the cashbook, and the cash payments are recorded on the right-hand pages. The left-hand pages are called the receipts side of the cashbook, and the right-hand pages are called the payments side. 

The purpose of the cashbook is to record all cash received and paid. Every transaction that involves a receipt of cash is recorded on the receipts side of the cashbook, and every transaction that involves the payment of cash is recorded on the payments side. 

The difference between the totals of the two sides should, at any time, equal the cash on hand.

The use of the cash book is not nearly so prevalent as it used to be. The business practice of depositing all cash receipts in the bank and of making payment by checks, involves the use of several cash records in place of the single cashbook. In small businesses, the cash balance may be kept on the stub of the check book, as described in a later chapter. In large businesses a number of records are substituted for the single cash book.

[Ch. 8 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES Form of the Cashbook 

The form of the cashbook differs from the forms of the separate journals for cash receipts and cash payments only in the use of additional columns for balancing purposes. The cash receipts and the cash payments journals illustrated in this chapter, arranged in the form of a cashbook, are shown in Illustration No. 26. When the total cash receipts and the total cash payments are to be posted to the cash account (usually at the time a trial balance is to be taken), it is customary to show the amount of cash on hand by balancing the cashbook in the same manner as the cash account is balanced. 

The method of doing this is shown in the illustration.

If, at the end of a fiscal period, the cash over or short account has a debit balance, the balance will be considered one of the general or administrative expenses. If it has a credit balance, the balance will be added to the net profit reported on the statement of profit and loss. 

Posting from the Cash- The analysis of cash receipts and cash book payments given in the preceding paragraphs explains the basis of posting from the cashbook. The cash receipts journal and the cash payments journal are joined to form the cashbook. The two sides of the cashbook are posted in exactly the same manner as the cash receipts and the cash payments journals are posted.

Transferring the Cash- When the page for either side of the cash book Totals book becomes filled, the totals of both sides are obtained and transferred to new pages. The method of transferring these totals is indicated in Illustration No. 26. This illustration also shows the method of recording the transactions of a second period after the cashbook has been balanced at the end of the first period. 

Recording Cash Sales 

Cash sales may be recorded in the cash receipts journal, or on the receipts side of the cashbook, in the manner shown by the third entry in Illustration No. 26. This entry results in a debit to Cash and a credit to 

Sales. This method of recording cash sales is usually followed in a retail store, in which numerous cash sales are made. In most cases the cash sales for each day are entered as a total at the end of the day.

As cash sales are rare in a typical wholesale business, each cash sale may be entered in the sales journal in the same way that it would be recorded if it were a sale on account. This entry results in a debit to the account of the customer and a credit to Sales. The cash received in payment is entered in the cash receipts journal, or on the cash receipts side of the cashbook, in the same manner that any receipt of cash in payment of an account is recorded. This entry results in a debit to Cash and a credit to the customer's account. The credit in the customer's account off-

Ch. 8] 181 

SPECIAL JOURNALS 

sets the debit that was posted to that account from the sales journal. The final result is a debit to Cash and a credit to Sales. 

The customer's account, however, shows both the debit and the credit. This information is valuable if the debtor wishes a statement at intervals showing all cash and credit purchases and all payments made to the seller. It is also useful to the proprietor when he wishes to know the total volume of business transacted with the customer.

Recording cash sales in the sales journal is not contrary to the purpose of the sales journal, which is to record sales on account. 

In this case a cash sale is treated as a sale on account for which payment is received on the same day. 

Recording Cash Purchases

Cash purchases may be recorded in the cash payments journal, or on the payments side of the cashbook, in the manner shown by the sixth entry in Illustration No. 26, page 179. This entry results in a debit to 

Purchases and a credit to Cash.

In a large business the invoices for purchases are not handled by the employees who are responsible for cash payments; consequently, the record of purchases and the record of cash payments are kept by different employees. Under these conditions it is more convenient to record cash purchases in the purchases journal in the same manner as purchases on account, and then to record the payments for these in the cash payments record. The entry in the purchases journal results in a debit to Purchases and a credit to the account with the vendor. The entry in the cash payments record results in a debit to the account with the vendor and a credit to Cash. Since the entries in the account of the vendor balance each other, the net result of the entries in the two records is a debit to purchases and a credit to cash. As cash purchases are made infrequently by businesses, the work involved in recording them in both the purchases and the cash payments records is not burdensome.

Recording cash purchases in the purchases journal is not contrary to the purpose of the purchases journal, which is to record purchases on account. In this case a cash purchase is treated as a purchase on account for which cash is paid on the same day.

[Ch. 8 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES Effect of Special Jour nals on the General 

Journal 

When special journals are used, the general journal is relieved of from 80 to 90 per cent of the transactions that would otherwise be recorded in it. The general journal contains, however, important entries. These entries can be conveniently classified as (1) opening entries, (2) current entries, (3) correcting entries, and (4) adjusting and closing entries. 

Opening Entries. Opening entries are those that record the financial condition of a business at the time of organization. The financial condition of a business is shown by the assets, the liabilities, and the difference between the two, or the net worth. 

The opening entry or entries must therefore provide a record of the assets, the liabilities, and the proprietorship of the business at the time of organization.

The proprietor's investment may consist entirely of cash. 

In this case the opening entry may be made in the cashbook. The entry consists in a debit to Cash and a credit to the proprietor's capital account. In some cases, however, the proprietor invests other property, such as land, a building, and merchandise. It is then necessary to make the opening entry in the general journal, for it is not possible to record the investment of all these assets in the cash record. 

For example, on October 1, J. K. Jansen invests the following assets in a retail hardware business: cash, $3,500; a note for $518 signed by C. G. Hardwick; merchandise, $810; and store equipment, $325. He owes the Georges Company $850 and desires that the business assume this liability. The investment may be recorded by the following compound entry:

GENERAL JOURNAL DATE

The financial condition of Jansen will be shown correctly in his accounts when this entry has been posted from the general journal. This method may be objectionable, however, because it does not provide a record of the cash investment in the cash receipts journal, or on the receipts side of the cashbook. It is apparent that, if the cash investment of Jansen is entered in both the cash record and the general journal, the amount should not be posted from both books, for this procedure would result in a double entry in the cash account and in Jansen's capital account. One method of overcoming this difficulty is to eliminate the posting of the cash item from the entry in the general journal and of the investment from the entry in the cash record. In order that the entry will not be posted, a check mark is placed in the folio column on a line with the cash item in both the general journal and the cash record at the time the entry is made. This method of recording the entire investment is:

In this case there will be two credits to the proprietor's capital account, one from the general journal and one from the cash record. 

The

[Ch. 8 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

advantage of entering the cash investment in the general journal is that the total investment is shown in one place. Both of these methods give the same result in the accounts and on the reports. 

When an opening entry is to be made from facts not in balance sheet form, it is desirable that a balance sheet be prepared. The journal entry is then merely a transcript of the balance sheet, showing the asset as debits and the liabilities and the proprietorship as credits.

Current Entries. Since the general journal is used to record all current transactions that do not go into special journals, it contains a variety of transactions.

The purchases and sales journals are used only to record purchases and sales of merchandise. In every business it is necessary to purchase property for use in the conduct of the business. 

Likewise, property that must be discarded or that is no longer needed by the business is sold. If these purchases and sales are made for cash, they may be entered in the cash record; but if they are made on account, they are recorded in the general journal. 

For example, on June 1 Robert Wheeler purchased on account $875 worth of store equipment from Cletus Ferron. Even though special journals have been introduced for transactions with merchandise sales, merchandise purchases, and cash, this transaction must be recorded in the general journal. 

Also, on June 30 Robert Wheeler received a 60-day note for $750.25 from A. G. Addison in settlement of his account. Since this transaction is not a sale, a purchase, or a cash receipt or payment, it was recorded in the general journal. The general journal entries for these two transactions are:

GENERAL JOURNAL DATE ACCOUNT TITLES AND EXPLANATION

Ch. 8] 185 

SPECIAL JOURNALS 

The two entries that had to be recorded in Wheeler's general journal are typical of the large number of entries that cannot be entered in special journals. Assets other than merchandise are not only bought on account, but they may also be sold on account. 

Notes are issued as well as received. Merchandise is returned for credit and allowances are granted and received because of unsatisfactory merchandise. These transactions and all others that cannot be recorded in special journals are recorded in the general journal. If any one of these transactions is repeated with considerable frequency, a special journal may be established for transactions of that kind.

Correcting Entries. Errors are occasionally made in recording transactions. Errors in books of original entry or in the ledger should not be erased. Erasures may lead one examining the books to believe that entries have been altered by the bookkeeper to defraud the owner, since an erasure does not indicate the reason for the change.

When the wrong amount is recorded in a book of original entry, the error may be corrected by drawing a line through the incorrect amount and writing the correct amount above. If the entry has been posted, but the accounts affected have not been closed, the error may be corrected in this way. In this case care must be taken to make the correction in both the book of original entry and the ledger.

If the wrong account was indicated in the book of original entry and the entry was thus posted incorrectly, or if an amount was merely posted to the wrong account, the correction is ordinarily made by a journal entry. The amount entered in the wrong account might be canceled, and the entry might be made in the correct account; but this method would not indicate the reason for cancellation. It is therefore better to make the journal entry so that the full explanation will be given. For example, an expenditure for the services of clerks in a store, which should have been charged to Sales Salaries, was recorded as a debit to Office Salaries. The owner, in looking over his trial balance at the end of March, noticed that the amount of the sales salaries was smaller and that of office salaries larger than they were during the previous month. The bookkeeper, in seeking the cause, traced the items in the sales salaries and office salaries accounts back to the book of original entry. By reference to the explanations in the book of

[Ch. 8 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

original entry, he found the item that had been improperly charged. 

He then made a correcting entry in the journal in the following manner: 

Mar. 31 Sales Salaries.. 

Office Salaries.. 

130 00 130/00 11+1+ 

To correct the error made in the cashbook on March 24, caused by charging $130 to Office Salaries in stead of to Sales Salaries. 

A complete explanation should be given in such an entry so that the reason for the entry can be ascertained at any time. A note may also be added to the explanation of the incorrect entry in the accounts explaining the error and giving the number of the page on which the entry has been corrected, so that any one examining the record will be certain that the error has been corrected.

All errors that are discovered after the end of the fiscal period, and which affect books of original entry that have been ruled, such as the sales or the purchases journals, or which affect accounts that have been closed' or ruled, are corrected by journal entries. In such cases the correction may be made more quickly by a journal entry than by changing amounts. The journal entry is also understood more readily than a change in an amount affecting the total of a book or an account. When the entry affects only asset or liability accounts, the debit and the credit needed to correct the error are made to those accounts. When an error affects the amount of the net income of a previous fiscal period, a debit or a credit is made to the capital account instead of to the income or the expense account, since all income and expense accounts have been closed and the net profit or the net loss for the period has been transferred to the capital account. 

For example, a payment of $500 for advertising has been charged as an expense applicable to a particular month, although it is for space in a newspaper for a period of six months. If the error is discovered after the books have been closed, the bookkeeper will show the asset prepaid advertising and the increased proprietorship. The following entry is therefore made to correct the error:

Ch. 8] 187 

Apr. 20 Prepaid Advertising, John Adams, Capital. 

To correct the error of March 31, when advertising for six months was charged as an expense of that month. 416 67 416 67 The method of correcting errors will, to some extent, depend on the nature of the errors and the circumstances surrounding them. While the bookkeeper has considerable latitude in the methods used, he must make the corrections and the explanations so clear that they can be readily understood by any one examining the records. This is necessary not only to protect himself from possible charges of fraud, but also to make it possible for the owner or the auditor to determine whether good accounting principles have been followed.

Adjusting and Closing Entries. In Chapter VI it was stated that the entries required to adjust and close the accounts at the end of a fiscal period are recorded in the general journal. The method of making these entries was illustrated there; hence it need not be discussed or illustrated in this chapter.

How could this error have been prevented?

CHAPTER IX SPECIAL LEDGERS 

Need for Subdividing A large number of accounts is necessary to the Ledger record the operations of a typical business. 

In most businesses the number is so large that it is impossible to maintain all the accounts in one ledger. For example, a large mercantile establishment that sells on account to numerous customers and purchases on account from many vendors may need to maintain several thousand accounts. If these accounts were placed in one ledger, one person could not keep the accounts, and more than one person would find it inconvenient to maintain the accounts. Moreover, it would be inconvenient to consider each of these accounts when the trial balance and the financial statements were to be prepared. To overcome these difficulties, it is customary to divide the accounts into groups and to maintain a ledger for each group.

Basis for Subdividing The usual basis for subdividing the ledger the Ledger is to take out of the original ledger and to place in a special ledger each class of accounts that is large in number. For example, the accounts with customers are usually the first accounts placed in a special ledger, for they are commonly the largest in number. On the same basis, accounts with creditors are usually the second group of accounts to be eliminated from the original ledger.

In many businesses at least three ledgers are maintained: first, the general ledger, which contains all the accounts except those with customers and creditors; second, the accounts receivable ledger, which contains the accounts with customers; and third, the accounts payable ledger, which contains the accounts with creditors. All the ledgers except the general ledger are called subsidiary ledgers. 192

Ch. 9] 193 

SPECIAL LEDGERS 

Relation of a Subsidiary On the balance sheet the accounts receiv- 

Ledger to the General able and the accounts payable are listed as Ledger totals. If the trial balance shows these items in the same manner, its preparation is facilitated and its usefulness increased. It is therefore customary to construct in the general ledger an accounts receivable account and an accounts payable account that show in summary form what is shown in detail by the accounts receivable ledger and the accounts payable ledger respectively. The balances of these two accounts are used in preparing the trial balance, the work sheet, and the balance sheet.

Accounts in the general ledger that provide a summary of the details in the subsidiary ledgers are called controlling accounts. 

The accounts receivable and the accounts payable accounts are the controlling accounts most frequently found in the general ledger. These accounts will therefore be used as a means of illustrating the method of constructing and operating controlling accounts and special ledgers. 

Accounts with Customers

As explained previously, all sales on account are recorded in the sales journal. 

When a controlling account with accounts receivable is not used, the entries in the sales journal are posted by debiting the account of each customer for the amount of the sale made to him and by crediting the sales account for the total of the sales made to all customers. For example, the following sales on account were made by James King, a wholesale merchant:

[Ch. 9 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES Cash Receipts and the Controlling Account with Accounts Re ceivable Customers are making remittances on account continually. These remittances must be recorded as credits to both the customers' accounts in the accounts receivable ledger and the controlling account in the general ledger. Each remittance is recorded in the cash receipts record. When the cash receipts record is posted, a separate credit is made to each customer's account. It would be possible to post each item separately to the controlling account with accounts receivable, but this method would necessitate posting each item twice, once to the subsidiary ledger and once to the general ledger. To eliminate this double posting, it is customary to construct a cash receipts record with a special column for the receipts from customers. 

This column is commonly known as the Accounts Receivable Cr. column. The total of this column is posted to the credit side of the controlling account in the general ledger, and the individual amounts in the column are posted to the credit side of the customers' accounts in the subsidiary ledger. 

Sales Discount

Since a customer usually makes payment within the discount period, a method must be devised for crediting his account not only for the amount of cash paid but also for the cash discount, the amount deducted from the invoice because of prompt payment. The discount could be recorded in the general journal, but that method would require a separate entry and a detailed explanation for each discount. A column for sales discount is therefore provided in the cash receipts record. The total credit to be given a customer, that is, the sum of the amount of cash received and the sales discount, is then entered in the Accounts Receivable Cr. column. The amounts to be debited to Sales Discount and Cash are entered in the Sales Discount Dr. and the Net Cash Dr. columns. The amount of the sales discount is a debit because it represents a deduction from income, or an expense.

For example, James King received the following remittances during the month of June to apply on the accounts illustrated on pages 194 and 195: 

June 14 Roger Howard, invoice of June 4..... $ 676.20 

18 H. L. Cooper, invoice of June 11. 

24 R. K. House, invoice of June 18. 

29 C. L. Boyd, invoice of June 23. 565.78 910.92 1,069.20

Ch. 9] 199 

SPECIAL LEDGERS 

The record of these remittances is shown in the cash receipts journal illustrated below. The first money column of this journal is for the amounts to be credited to all accounts except customers' accounts. The second money column is for the credits to the customers' accounts. The third is for the debits to Sales Discount, and the fourth is for the debits to Cash. The credits entered in the General Cr. column and the Accounts Receivable 

Cr. column should always equal the sum of the debits in the Sales 

Discount Dr. column and the Net Cash Dr. column. 

The individual items in the Accounts Receivable Cr. column are ordinarily posted daily to the credit side of the appropriate accounts in the subsidiary ledger. The individual amounts entered in the General Cr. column and the totals of the other columns are ordinarily posted at the end of each month. Cash Payments and the Controlling Account with Accounts Payable

Payments on account are made continually to creditors. These payments must be recorded as debits to both the creditors' accounts in the accounts payable ledger and the controlling ac-

[Ch. 9 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

count in the general ledger. Each payment is recorded in the cash payments record. When the cash payments record is posted, a separate debit is made to each creditor's account. It would be possible to post each item separately to the controlling account with accounts payable, but this method would necessitate posting each item twice, once to the subsidiary ledger and once to the general ledger. To eliminate this double posting, it is customary to construct a cash payments record with a special column for the payments made to creditors. This column is commonly known as the Accounts Payable Dr. column. The total of this column is posted to the debit side of the controlling account in the general ledger, and the individual amounts in the column are posted to the debit side of creditors' accounts in the subsidiary ledger. 

Purchases Discount 

Since a business usually takes the cash discount on each purchase invoice subject to discount, a method of recording purchases discount must be provided. A special column is provided in the cash payments record for the discount. This column is known as the Purchases Discount Cr. column.

For example, the following payments on account were made by James King during the month of June:

June 18 M. C. Hickey, invoice of June 8. 

24 G. L. Fox, invoice of June 14. 

26 L. O. Ryan, invoice of June 18.. 

28 H. A. Acer, invoice of June 18... $1,257.34 1,577.70 646.11 666.12

It will be observed that the discount was deducted in each case; hence the amount paid was less than the face of the invoice. 

Provision was therefore made in the cash payments record for recording (1) the face of the invoice, (2) the purchases discount, and (3) the net amount of cash paid. The cash payments journal in Illustration No. 28 contains a record of the remittances made by James King.

The first money column is for the amounts to be debited to all accounts except creditors' accounts; the second is for debits to creditors' accounts; the third is for credits to Purchases Discount; and the fourth is for credits to Cash. The debits to creditors' accounts are usually posted daily. The individual amounts in the General Dr. column and the totals of the other columns are generally posted at the end of each month. Cash Sales

Since the cash sales of a typical wholesale or manufacturing business are not numerous, it is customary to record them in the same manner as sales on account. Accounts Receivable is debited and Sales is credited through an entry in the sales journal. The customer's account is also debited from the sales journal. The cash received in payment is recorded in the cash receipts record. This second entry results in a debit to Cash, a credit to Accounts Receivable, and a credit to the customer's account in the subsidiary ledger. Hence no problem is involved in handling cash sales through the controlling account with accounts receivable. 

Since the cash sales of a typical retail business are numerous, it is not practicable to pass each sale through a customer's account. Except in rare cases, no benefit would be gained by such a record, even if the cost of maintaining it were not prohibitive. Any

Ch. 9] 205 

SPECIAL LEDGERS 

one of several methods may be used in recording the cash sales of a retail business.

The cash sales for each day may be recorded as a total in the cash receipts record and need not be recorded in the sales journal. 

The entry may be made in the following form:

CASH RECEIPTS JOURNAL 

DATE 19-- 

ACCOUNT CREDITED GENERAL EXPLANATION CR. 

ACCOUNTS 

REC. CR. 

SALES DIS. DR. 

NET 

CASH DR. 

June 12 Sales 

Cash sales for the day 4,503 30 4,503 30 This entry results in a debit to Cash and a credit to Sales. A separate posting is made to the sales account for the sales of each day.

Sometimes a special column for cash sales is provided in the cash receipts record. In this case the total of this column is posted to the credit of the sales account at the time that a trial balance is taken. This method eliminates the necessity of posting the sales of each day separately. If this plan is followed, the cash receipts journal may appear as on page 206. 

When a special column was used heretofore, it was provided for a controlling account, the total of the column being posted to the general ledger account and the individual items to the accounts in the subsidiary ledger. The Ledger Page column was therefore needed for the posting to the latter accounts. In the case of a special column for cash sales, however, only the total of the column is posted and the Ledger Page column is therefore not required. When an entry is made in the Sales Cr. column, a check mark is placed in the Ledger Page column to indicate that the item need not be posted.

This method of recording cash sales does not provide for a record of all sales in the sales journal. It also requires posting to the sales account from two sources. In auditing there is an advantage in having a complete record of sales in the sales journal. 

This can be accomplished most satisfactorily in the following manner: 

The cash sales can be recorded in both the sales journal and the cash receipts record, and the entries can be checked so that only one posting will be made from each record. The amounts of the cash sales will be included in the total of the Amount column of the sales record. This total will be posted to the credit of Sales.

Cash Purchases 

The typical firm makes few purchases of merchandise for cash. Most firms make none at all. When such purchases are made, it is usually satisfactory to record them in the same manner as purchases on credit. 

Purchases is debited and Accounts Payable is credited through an entry in the purchases journal. The payment to the creditor is recorded in the cash payments record. This entry results in a debit to Accounts Payable, a debit to the creditor's account, and a credit to Cash.

Ch. 9] 207 

SPECIAL LEDGERS 

The postings to both the creditor's account and the controlling account can be eliminated by making the entry in the cash payments record alone. This method is illustrated by the following entry, which results in a debit to Purchases and a credit to Cash: 

CASH PAYMENTS JOURNAL
