2,312 40 2,312 40 Cash purchases may be entered in both the purchases journal and the cash payments record. The entries can be checked so that only one posting will be made from each record. This method is much the same as that used when cash sales are recorded in both the sales journal and the cash receipts record. This method of recording cash purchases is not used frequently. 

Sales Returns and Allowances

When a customer returns merchandise purchased on account, or when he is granted an allowance from the sales price of goods purchased on account, both the accounts receivable account and the customer's account are affected. Such a transaction may be recorded in the general journal by debiting Sales Returns or Sales Allowances, as the case may be, and crediting both the accounts receivable account and the customer's account. The credit entry is posted to both the accounts receivable account in the general ledger and the customer's account in the subsidiary ledger. 

For example, on August 10, M. W. Cole returns $51.60 worth of merchandise and is granted credit for it. The general journal entry is: 

Aug. 10 Sales Returns.

Accounts Receivable-M. W. Cole.. 

Merchandise sold on account was returned for credit. 28 6/45 51/60 5160 Both the accounts receivable account in the general ledger and M. W. Cole's account in the subsidiary ledger are credited for $51.60. The page numbers of both accounts are indicated in the 

Ledger Page column of the journal. 

If such transactions are numerous, a special column may be provided in the general journal so that the credits to Accounts

[Ch. 9 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

Receivable can be posted as a total. If these transactions are sufficiently numerous, they may be recorded in a separate journal termed a sales returns and allowances journal. The sales returns and allowances journal may be similar to the sales journal. However, the individual items in the Amount columns are credited to the customers' accounts in the accounts receivable ledger; and the totals of the Amount columns are debited to Sales Returns and to Sales Allowances and are credited to Accounts Receivable in the general ledger.

Purchases Returns and When goods purchased on account are re- Allowances turned to a creditor, or when an allowance from the price of goods purchased on account is obtained, both the accounts payable account and the creditor's account are affected. Such a transaction may be recorded in the general journal by debiting both the accounts payable account and the creditor's account, and crediting Purchases Returns and Allowances. The debit entry is posted to both the accounts payable account in the general ledger and the creditor's account in the subsidiary ledger. 

For example, on August 20, $139.70 worth of merchandise is returned to W. J. McGuire, a creditor. The general journal entry is: 

Aug. 20 Accounts Payable-W. J. McGuire.. 

Purchases Returns and Allowances. 

Received credit for goods returned. 15/28 31 139/70 139 70 The debit entry of $139.70 is posted to the accounts payable account in the general ledger and to McGuire's account in the creditors' ledger.

If such transactions are frequent, a special column may be provided in the general journal so that the debits to the accounts payable account can be posted as a total.. Sometimes a separate journal termed a purchases returns and allowances journal is used to record these transactions. The purchases returns and allowances journal may be similar to the purchases journal. However, the individual items in the Amount column are debited to the creditors' accounts in the accounts payable ledger; and the total of the Amount column is debited to Accounts Payable and

Ch. 9] 209 

SPECIAL LEDGERS 

credited to Purchases Returns and Allowances in the general ledger.

In the illustrations given above provision has been made for separate Sales Returns and Sales Allowances accounts and for a single Purchase Returns and Allowances account. This is not essential, but it is desirable to have information on sales returns and sales allowances separately. It is not so necessary to have detailed information on purchases returns and allowances. 

Notes Receivable and 

The practice of customers giving notes in 

Notes Payable settlement of their accounts instead of cash was described in Chapter VII. A note does not pay a debt, but it changes the form of the indebtedness from an oral promise to a written promise. This change must be shown in the records, for oral promises are reported on the balance sheet as accounts receivable whereas written promises are reported as notes receivable.

A note that has been received from a customer may be recorded in the general journal by a debit to Notes Receivable and a credit to both the accounts receivable account in the general ledger and the customer's account in the subsidiary ledger. 

For example, on August 12 a note for $300 is received from 0. H. Taylor to apply on his account. The general journal entry is: 

Aug. 12 Notes Receivable. 

3 300 00 300 00 

Accounts Receivable-O. H. Taylor.. 6/60 

Received Note No. 8 to apply on account.

The credit entry is posted to the accounts receivable account in the general ledger and to the account of O. H. Taylor in the customers' ledger.

If such transactions are numerous, a special column may be provided in the general journal so that the debits to Notes Receivable can be posted as a total. A separate journal known as a notes receivable journal is sometimes used. 

Notes given to creditors to apply on account may also be recorded in the general journal. Both the accounts payable account in the general ledger and the creditor's account in the subsidiary ledger are debited, and Notes Payable is credited.

[Ch. 9 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

For example, on August 14 a note for $650 is given to Ralph Cassady to apply on account. The general journal entry is: 

Aug. 14 Accounts Payable-Ralph Cassady Notes Payable.. 

Issued Note No. 5 to apply on account. 15/30 14 650 00 650 00 The debit entry is posted to the accounts payable account in the general ledger and to the account of Ralph Cassady in the accounts payable ledger.

If such transactions are numerous, a special column may be provided in the general journal so that the credits to Notes Payable can be posted as a total. A separate journal known as a notes payable journal is sometimes used.

Advantages of Controlling Accounts The principal advantages derived from the use of the controlling accounts with accounts receivable and accounts payable are: (1) The trial balance is greatly shortened and is thus made more useful as a basis for preparing the reports. (2) The trial balance can be prepared without reference to the subsidiary ledgers; consequently the possibility of making errors in preparing it is greatly minimized. If errors have been made in posting to the subsidiary ledgers, these can be found after the trial balance of the general ledger has been obtained. (3) By means of the controlling accounts, the total owed by customers and the total owed to creditors can be easily ascertained when the posting has been completed. (4) There are usually a great many accounts with customers and creditors. By maintaining these accounts in separate ledgers, it is possible for several bookkeepers to post to the accounts at one time. This procedure would be impossible if all the accounts were kept in one ledger. (5) It is usually desirable to post to the personal accounts daily. The posting to the general ledger, however, is usually made only at the time a trial balance is to be taken. The daily posting to customers' and creditors' accounts is facilitated by the use of separate ledgers.

Ch. 9] 211 

SPECIAL LEDGERS Controlling Accounts and the Organization of Accounts 

The use of controlling accounts facilitates the organization of accounts when the number of accounts is large. When a controlling account is substituted in the general ledger for a large group of accounts, and the latter accounts are placed in a subsidiary ledger, the total number of accounts in the general ledger can be kept within reasonable limits. Furthermore, in this way detailed information is removed from the general ledger, and attention need not be given to details in preparing the two principal accounting statements.

Practically all the accounts on the balance sheet and several of the accounts on the statement of profit and loss could be controlling accounts. If the business has cash on deposit in a number of banks, the cash account could be a controlling account, and an account with each bank could be placed in a subsidiary ledger. 

The furniture and fixtures account could be a controlling account, and an account with each type of furniture or fixtures could be maintained. In this way the general ledger would be compact, and details would be removed to subsidiary ledgers. 

Accounts are thus organized into major and minor classifications. This organization can be carried into several stages. For example: A manufacturing corporation with plants in several cities might have in its general ledger a controlling account called Manufacturing Plants. The accounts applicable to the operations of each manufacturing plant would be maintained in a subsidiary ledger. In the subsidiary ledger there might be accounts with the various manufacturing departments of the particular plant. 

These accounts would control others kept in a ledger subsidiary to the subsidiary ledger. In this subsidiary ledger there might be controlling accounts with machinery, the details of which would be recorded in accounts in another subsidiary ledger. In this way the accounts would be organized into four levels of major and minor classifications. This type of organization greatly facilitates the use of a large number of accounts. The use of controlling ac counts is further illustrated in Chapter XXIII. 

1. L. M. Burns, a public accountant, recommends that C. R.

Whitney, a merchant, keep a subsidiary ledger and a controlling

CHAPTER X VALUATION ACCOUNTS 

Showing Two Amounts In order that the information conveyed by for a Single Item accounting statements may be complete, it is sometimes desirable to show two amounts for a single item. 

For example, it may be useful to know not only the estimated present value of a machine that has been in use for some time, but also the original cost of this machine. Or it may be useful to know not only the total claims against customers, but also the estimated amount collectible on these accounts. Or, again, an enterprise may have sold some goods that were later returned, in which case it may be useful to know not only the gross sales, but also the net sales, that is, the gross sales less the sales returns and allowances.

The gross amounts for fixed assets, receivables, and sales can be preserved for use in the statements if the amounts to be subtracted from them are recorded in separate accounts. The present value of any fixed asset can then be determined by the use of two accounts, the first of which has a debit balance representing the original cost of the fixed asset, and the second of which has a credit balance representing the estimated decrease in value due to the use of the asset. Similarly, the amount of receivables that is estimated to be collectible is the difference between the balances of two accounts. The first balance is a debit representing the total claims against customers; the second is a credit representing the estimated amount that will be uncollectible. Or, in the case of sales, the total of the effective sales for a period is the difference between a credit account balance that represents the total sales made during that period and debit account balances that represent the sales returns and sales allowances for the same period. 

In each of these instances the effective amount shown on the accounting statement results from the use of the balances of two accounts. The account that has the smaller balance, and that makes possible the determination of the effective amount to be 220

Ch. 10] 221 

VALUATION ACCOUNTS 

used on the statement, is known as a valuation account. The purpose of a valuation account is to preserve in the records statistical data and at the same time to make possible the reporting of the effective amount applicable at present to the account in question.

Valuation accounts are also called (a) offset, or contra, accounts and (b) negative accounts.

Credit balances in balance sheet accounts may represent not only liabilities and proprietorship, but also deductions from assets. 

Accounts representing deductions from assets are known as negative asset accounts. Similarly, debit balances may represent deductions from liabilities or proprietorship. Such accounts are known as negative liability or negative proprietorship accounts. 

The original accounting equation illustrated repeatedly in the early chapters might be expanded to show negative asset, negative liability, and negative proprietorship accounts. expanded equation would be:

The 

A nA 

nL + P nP Debit balances would then represent, in addition to assets, negative liabilities and negative proprietorship; credit balances would represent, in addition to liabilities and proprietorship, negative assets.

The purpose of this chapter is to describe the use of two types of valuation accounts. By the use of the first type of valuation account, the balance sheet will show not only the original cost of fixed assets, but also the estimated present value of these assets. Through the use of the second type of valuation account, the total claims against customers for goods sold but not yet paid for, and the estimated uncollectible portion of this amount, will be shown on the statement. Since these accounts are used in an attempt to value asset accounts, they have credit balances. Such an account usually has, as its title, the designation Allowance for, or Reserve for, followed by the nature of the item with which it correlates. For example, the valuation account applicable to accounts receivable may be called Allowance for Bad Debts, or Reserve for Bad Debts; the valuation account applicable to office equipment may be called Allowance for Depreciation of Office 

Equipment or Reserve for Depreciation of Office Equipment.

[Ch. 10 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

Depreciation

In Chapter V it was observed that when supplies are purchased they are assets, but as they are consumed they become expenses. The total expense of any particular period is equal to the value of the assets consumed in that period. Fixed assets also, with the exception of land, are consumed through use. Furniture and fixtures, delivery equipment, building, and other types of fixed assets required by businesses gradually wear out. Regardless of how well they may be constructed, they cannot be used indefinitely. 

For example, on January 1, David McCann purchased a delivery truck for $2,100, which, it was estimated, would decrease annually $420 because of use. He also purchased, for use in the truck, a 60-gallon drum of oil for $60. On the first of January both the truck and the oil were entirely assets. During the month the truck decreased in value, because of use, to the amount of $35 (of $420). If one tenth of the oil was used, the decrease in the value of the oil was $6 ( of $60). It is therefore apparent that two similar expenses had been incurred: one amounting to $35 and representing the wear on the truck; the other amounting to $6 and representing the value of the oil consumed. All fixed assets, except land, are consumed in the operation of a business in the same manner as commodities and services that represent expenses. From an accounting point of view, the primary difference between a fixed asset and a deferred charge is that the fixed asset is consumed during a period of several years, whereas the deferred charge is usually consumed within a short period of time after its procurement.

The decrease in the value of a fixed asset, which is due to use, is known as depreciation. It must be shown on the balance sheet as a deduction from the cost price of the fixed asset and on the statement of profit and loss as a deduction from gross profit. 

The rate of decrease in the value of a fixed asset depends in part upon the quality of maintenance provided. If a fixed asset is maintained properly, its useful life tends to be lengthened and the annual cost of depreciation is lessened. It is therefore evident that maintenance and depreciation are definitely related. Both are expenses arising from the use of fixed assets. Maintenance, however, results in current expenditures. Depreciation, on the other hand, is a decrease in the value of a fixed asset that is not identified with current expenditures of the business. During any

Ch. 10] 223 

VALUATION ACCOUNTS 

fiscal period a reduction in either of these expenses may increase the other. Maintenance alone, however, cannot prevent depreciation. No matter how well a fixed asset is cared for, there will come a time when it will no longer be usable. 

Method of Reporting 

Depreciation on the

Financial Statements 

When supplies are partially consumed during a fiscal period, the value of the amount used is deducted from the cost price of the supplies, and only the balance, or the net asset value, is shown on the balance sheet. Similarly, when a fixed asset decreases in value through use, the net asset value at the end of the fiscal period, that is, the original cost less the depreciation, may be reported on the balance sheet. Ordinarily, however, the original cost, the depreciation, and the net value are shown. This procedure is followed because persons using the balance sheet desire to know original cost as well as estimated present book value. 

The deduction from the value of a fixed asset due to depreciation is commonly known by the technical name of reserve for depreciation, although the somewhat more descriptive name of allowance for depreciation is also used. The term reserve for depreciation will be used in this text. 

Accounts such as Reserve for Depreciation are known as valuation accounts. They are balance sheet accounts with credit balances and affect the valuation of certain asset accounts. Taken together with the asset account to which it belongs, a valuation account gives the estimated present book value of a particular fixed asset. Instead of decreasing the balance of the asset account the bookkeeper increases the credit balance of the valuation account and thus obtains the same effect on the net asset value. 

The effect of the valuation account on the delivery equipment account of David McCann during the life of the truck is illustrated in the following table:

END OF YEAR COST OF ASSET RESERVE NET BOOK VALUE 

12345 $2,100 $ 420 $1,680 2,100 840 1,260 2,100 1,260 840 2,100 1,680 420 2,100 2,100

[Ch. 10 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

It is estimated that a truck costing $2,100 will last five years. 

Instead of showing the successive annual values of the truck as $1,680, $1,260, $840, $420, and nothing; at the end of the first, second, third, fourth, and fifth years, the valuation account is increased $420 each year. The increase in the valuation account has the same effect on the asset as a similar decrease would have if it were applied directly to the asset account. 

Since valuation accounts are balance sheet accounts with credit balances, but are neither liability nor proprietorship accounts, they are sometimes called minus asset accounts or negative asset accounts. The effect on the balance sheet of such accounts is, not to increase the liabilities and the proprietorship, but rather to reduce the assets. Such items are sometimes reported on the liabilities and proprietorship side of the balance sheet, but this method results in overstating the assets by the total of the balances of the valuation accounts. The more approved method therefore is to show the balances of these accounts as deductions from the assets to which they apply. 

If David McCann has, at the opening of his business, two fixed assets-a delivery truck valued at $2,100, on which the depreciation is estimated to be 20 per cent a year, and furniture and fixtures valued at $1,200, on which the depreciation is estimated to be 10 per cent a year—the fixed assets may be reported as follows on the balance sheet prepared at the end of the first year of operation:

FIXED ASSETS: 

Delivery Equipment.. 

2,100.00 

Less Reserve for Depreciation.. 420.00 1,680 00 

Furniture and Fixtures... 

1,200.00 

Less Reserve for Depreciation.. 120.00 1,080 00 

Total Fixed Assets... 

2,760 00 Since depreciation is an expense, it is also reported on the statement of profit and loss. Depreciation on various fixed assets is classified according to the use of the assets. If the asset is used in the sales department, the depreciation is classified as a selling expense. If the asset is used by all departments of the business, the depreciation is classified as a general expense. Depreciation on delivery equipment is classified with either the selling expenses

Ch. 10] 225 

VALUATION ACCOUNTS 

or the delivery expenses; depreciation on furniture and fixtures, with the general expenses.

Method of Recording If the original cost of each fixed asset and 

Depreciation the amount that the asset has depreciated are to be shown as separate items on the balance sheet, it is evident that two accounts must be kept with each fixed asset that is subject to depreciation. The first account will show the cost of the asset, and the second account will show the accumulated depreciation on this asset. A third account also must be maintained to record the expense caused by depreciation. For example, McCann's fixed assets are recorded in the following accounts: 

DELIVERY EQUIPMENT 

19Jan.

2,100 00 19Jan.

FURNITURE AND FIXTURES 

J 1,200 00 IT At the end of the year the depreciation of the delivery equipment is estimated to be $420 and that of the furniture and fixtures to be $120. As the depreciation is an expense, it must be debited to the proper expense accounts. Common descriptive titles of these expense accounts are Depreciation of Delivery Equipment and Depreciation of Furniture and Fixtures. The decrease in the value of each asset is recorded by crediting the corresponding re serve account, Reserve for Depreciation of Delivery Equipment or Reserve for Depreciation of Furniture and Fixtures. The journal entries on December 31 to record the depreciation for the year are:

[Ch. 10 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

The depreciation accounts in the preceding entries are expense accounts. The amounts of these expenses will be included in the statement of profit and loss, and the accounts will be closed into the profit and loss summary account at the end of the fiscal period. The depreciation accounts are opened in the expense division of the ledger. Depreciation of delivery equipment is classified with the delivery or the selling expenses; depreciation of furniture and fixtures, with the general expenses. 

The reserve for depreciation accounts are valuation accounts. 

On the balance sheet the balances of these accounts will be deducted from the cost values of the corresponding fixed assets: 

Each reserve account is opened in the ledger directly after the asset account to which it applies. This arrangement facilitates the determination of the book value of each fixed asset and provides the information about fixed assets in the order in which it appears on the balance sheet. Throughout the life of the asset, the reserve account gradually increases and the book value of the asset gradually decreases. 

The purpose of the preceding entries is to charge to the current period the decreases in the value of the fixed assets. In order that the net profit of the period may not be overstated, or that the assets on the balance sheet may not be overvalued, it is necessary to include in the expenses of the period the depreciation applicable to that period.

The depreciation charged during the five years was $4,000, or $800 a year. Since the estimated depreciation was recorded at the end of each year, the asset account and the reserve account appear as follows on December 31, 1939: 1935 Jan. 1

In the preceding example it was assumed that the life of the asset and the scrap value had been estimated with absolute exactness. Such an accurate estimate, however, is usually not possible. The life of the asset will be either longer or shorter than the estimate. To illustrate the procedure in such cases, it may be

[Ch. 10 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

assumed that Charles Hardin purchased machinery for $4,200 on January 1, 1936. The life of the machinery was estimated to be five years; the scrap value at the end of that time, $200; and the annual depreciation, $800. The machinery was so inefficient, however, that at the beginning of the fifth year, 1940, it was sold as scrap for $500.

The book value of the machinery at the time of the sale was $1,000 ($4,200 the cost value, minus $3,200, the recorded estimated depreciation for the four years the machinery had been used). Since the machinery was sold for $500, a loss of $500 was incurred. This loss was not applicable, however, to the year 1940 only. The $500 represented actual depreciation that had occurred during the four years in which the machinery was in use, but that had not been recorded as a charge against the profits of those years and as a credit to the reserve account. The error was due to the fact that both the scrap value and the life of the asset had not been estimated correctly. If the actual depreciation, $3,700, had been recorded during the four previous years instead of the estimated depreciation, $3,200, which is the balance of the reserve account, the profits of those years would have been $500 smaller. This error was corrected by debiting the capital account for $500. The following entry not only recorded the sale of the machinery, but also corrected the error in the profits of the previous years and closed the asset and the reserve accounts:

1940

If an asset should continue in use beyond its estimated life, the opposite condition to that given above would exist. For example, Frank Dawson owned furniture and fixtures that cost $1,000 and was depreciated at the rate of 10 per cent a year for eight years. At the beginning of the ninth year, on January 1, 1940, it was sold for $400. It is evident that too much depreciation had been charged during the previous nine years. For example, C. K. Newman owned store fixtures that had cost $6,750. Newman estimated that, after a life of six years, the asset would have a scrap value of $750. He therefore charged depreciation at the rate of $1,000 a year. At the beginning of the fifth year, however, Newman estimated that the asset could serve satisfactorily for a total estimated life of ten years. On this basis the depreciation should have been $600 a year, or $2,400 for the four years, instead of $1,000 a year or $4,000 for the four years, which was the amount that had been charged as depreciation. In order to adjust the reserve in accordance with the revised estimate, Newman made the following entry:

Jan. 1

Methods of Computing Depreciation is only an estimate, but it is Depreciation usually possible to make the estimate with reasonable exactness. When a business has had considerable experience with a certain kind of fixed asset, the rate of depreciation can be based on past experience. When the experience of the business is insufficient to be used as a basis, assistance can be obtained from published tables of depreciation rates. 

The amount of depreciation to be charged to a fiscal period is usually expressed as a certain percentage of the original cost of the fixed asset or of the amount to be depreciated if there is a scrap value. This method of calculating depreciation is based on the principle that depreciation is a function of time. The rate of depreciation is occasionally expressed in terms of units of output or service. When the output or the service fluctuates from period to period, the output or service basis probably provides a more accurate method than the time basis. Because business people believe that depreciation is, at best, an estimate and that the time basis is likely to be about as accurate as the output basis, the time basis, because it is the simpler method, is more generally used.

If the extent to which a fixed asset is used varies greatly from year to year the output method has certain advantages. It charges the cost of the machine in proportion to its use, rather than in proportion to its age. For example, if the estimated output of a machine costing $1,000 were 50,000 units and production requirements caused these units to be spread over five years as shown below, the output method would make the annual depreciation the varying amounts shown instead of $200 per year. 

YEAR 

1 year 2 3 

VOLUME OF PRODUCTION 

5,000 units 2,500 7,500 20,000 15,000

AMOUNT OF DEPRECIATION 

$ 100 50 150 400 CIT 

5 years 

50,000 units 300 $1,000

When the time basis is used, any one of a variety of methods may be employed in determining the depreciation for a period. 

Three methods will be discussed at this time. They are:

[Ch. 10 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

1. The straight-line method. 

2. The reducing-fraction method. 

3. The sinking-fund method.

Under the straight-line method an equal amount of depreciation is charged to each fiscal period. Under the reducingfraction method the depreciation of the early years is heavy, whereas that of the following years becomes increasingly lighter. 

The purpose of this method is to make the sum of depreciation and maintenance charges more or less equal during the life of the asset. Under the sinking-fund method the charge to each successive period increases so that, when the asset is discarded, the interest income of the various years plus the principal will equal the total depreciation. These methods will be better understood by reference to examples.

Straight-Line Method. It is estimated that a machine purchased for $1,500 will be serviceable for five years. If the straightline method of calculating depreciation is used, the depreciation will be distributed equally among the various fiscal periods. The annual depreciation will be computed in the following manner: $1,500 $300 Reducing-Fraction Method. If it is desirable that more depreciation be charged during the early periods than during the late ones, a simple and reasonably accurate method that can be used is as follows: (1) the numbers representing the fiscal periods in which the asset will be used are added; (2) a fractional part of the total estimated depreciation is apportioned to each period, the numerators of the various fractions being the numbers of the fiscal periods in inverse order, and the denominators being the sum of these numbers. The depreciation for the problem given above can be obtained in the following manner: 

1+ 2+ 3+ 4+ 5 = 15, sum of numbers of periods 

5/15 of $1,500 $500, depreciation for the first year 

4/15 of $1,500 

3/15 of $1,500 

2/15 of $1,500 $400, depreciation for the second year $300, depreciation for the third year $200, depreciation for the fourth year 

1/15 of $1,500 $100, depreciation for the fifth year 

$1,500, total depreciation

Ch. 10] 233 

VALUATION ACCOUNTS Sinking-Fund Method. 

When the sinking-fund method is used, the depreciation charged for each year during the life of the asset is considered equivalent to a fund established to replace the fixed asset. Such a fund would not be established, however, by setting aside equal amounts at the ends of the various years, for it is assumed that the fund would earn interest if it were actually invested. Hence the depreciation charged in one year should be less than that charged in a succeeding year by the amount of interest that would be earned if the amount applicable to the first year had been invested at a given rate. For example, if, in the problem given previously, money is considered to be worth 6 per cent, the depreciation applicable to each of the five years, when computed according to the sinking-fund method, would be 

INTEREST AT 6% 

YEAR

The computation of depreciation according to this method is difficult unless tables for sinking funds are available.

The effects of the three methods of charging depreciation are illustrated by the chart at the top of page 234. 

Because of its simplicity and its approximation to scientific accuracy, the straight-line method is used most commonly in business. It will be used in all the problems of this text. 

Inadequacy and Obsolescence In some cases the value of a fixed asset may be decreased by a cause other than use.

For example, a printing plant has a press that has been in use for only two years and is in excellent condition. Because of an increase in the business of the concern, it is found profitable to dispose of this machine at much less than its book value and to purchase a new and larger one. When a fixed asset decreases in value because it must be replaced by larger equipment capable of meeting the demands of the business, inadequacy is said to have taken place.

[Ch. 10 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

Book Value of Asset 

$1,100 1,000 900 800 700 600 500

2. Reducing-fraction method. 

3. Sinking-fund method. 400 300 200 100 1 2 3 

ILLUSTRATION No. 30, CHART SHOWING EFFECTS OF THE THREE METHODS OF CHARGING DEPRECIATION 

The printing plant may also have a job press that must be fed by hand. A new automatically fed press, however, becomes available on the market. As the new type of press will do the work much more efficiently than the old, a new press is purchased, although the first must be sold at a fraction of its book value. 

When an improvement has been made in the equipment available, and this improvement makes it desirable to discard the old equipment, the decrease in the value of the latter asset is known as obsolescence.

While obsolescence and inadequacy can be distinguished by definition from depreciation, it is customary in practice to include provision for all three factors in a single depreciation item. The depreciation charge presumably covers inadequacy and obsolescence as well as depreciation. Depletion

A fourth cause of a decrease in the value of a fixed asset may be depletion. A paper mill may have a certain acreage from which wood is cut for wood

Ch. 10] 235 

VALUATION ACCOUNTS 

pulp. The value of the acreage decreases each year because the wood cut is more than the new growth. This expense is known as depletion. A depletion account is debited for the expense of each period, and a reserve for depletion account is credited for the same amount. The difference between the cost value of the acreage and the reserve is the book value. 

For example, if the Hamilton Paper Company estimates that the value of its timber land decreases $10,000 in a year because the amount of wood cut is greater than the new growth, an annual operating expense of this amount must be recorded and the books must show that the acreage is worth less than it was at the beginning of the period. This is accomplished by the following journal entry made on December 31: 

Dec. 31 Depletion of Timber Land..

Reserve for Depletion of Timber Land. 

To record the decrease in the value of the timber land, resulting from depletion. 

10,000 00 

10,000 00

The depletion account represents an expense of the period, but the reserve for depletion account will be retained on the books to show the decrease in the value of the asset. 

Identifying Bad Debts with Sales 

Businessmen have learned by experience that during each year they sell merchandise to some customers who fail to pay their accounts. This is true no matter how carefully they may select those to whom they sell on account.

Since sales are made on account, bad debts, or uncollectible receivables, are often not ascertained until the accounting period subsequent to that in which the sales were made. Losses from bad debts are properly chargeable, however, to the period in which the credit is granted. At the end of each accounting period provision must therefore be made for losses on accounts receivable resulting from the sales of that period. For example, on December 31, George Weigel has $10,000 due from customers. It is probable that he will be unable to collect this entire amount. He does not know which customers will not pay, but he does know from past experience that about 12 of 1 per cent of sales will be uncollectible. If his sales volume was $40,000, the estimated un-

[Ch. 10 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

collectible amount would be $200. The amount that he expects to collect, then, is $10,000 - $200, or $9,800. 

The estimate of loss from bad debts may be expressed as a percentage of either accounts receivable or sales. Whichever base is used, the percentage is determined by past experience. Since sales ordinarily exceed receivables in volume, the percentage applicable to sales is less than that applicable to receivables. In the preceding illustration the application of 2 per cent to accounts receivable would give an amount of uncollectible receivables the same as that determined by applying 12 of 1 per cent to the sales. 

Method of Showing Bad Debts on the Financial Statements 

Bad debts must be shown on the balance sheet and on the statement of profit and loss. If the balance sheet is to reflect the true financial condition of the business, the amount of accounts receivable that is expected to prove uncollectible must be reported as a deduction from the total of the accounts receivable. On the statement of profit and loss the amount expected to be uncollectible must be reported as an expense of the period. 

For example, it is not proper for George Weigel, who was mentioned in the preceding discussion, to show his accounts receivable on the balance sheet as $10,000, even though the loss from bad debts has not yet taken place. Allowance must be made for the $200 that will probably prove uncollectible. The $200 is therefore subtracted from the $10,000 owed by customers, and the accounts receivable are shown on the balance sheet at the net amount of $9,800.

Accountants and businessmen believe it best to show on the balance sheet the gross amount of the accounts receivable with the estimated allowance for bad debts subtracted from it for two reasons: (1) the amount of accounts receivable expected to prove uncollectible is an estimate and may have to be adjusted and (2) the gross amount agrees with the subsidiary customers' ledger. 

The estimated allowance for bad debts is usually called reserve for bad debts. If this method is used by George Weigel, his accounts receivable and reserve for bad debts will be reported on the balance sheet in the following manner:

Accounts Receivable. 

Less Reserve for Bad Debts.. 

10,000.00 200.00 9,800 0

Ch. 10] 237 

VALUATION ACCOUNTS 

The provision for uncollectible accounts represents a cost of operating the business and should therefore be reported as an expense on the statement of profit and loss. There is a difference of opinion among accountants concerning the classification of this expense. Since the granting of credit usually rests with the credit department, a nonselling department, it is treated as a general expense in this text.

If the allowance set aside at the close of a period has been estimated exactly, it will equal the amount of accounts receivable actually ascertained to be worthless and charged to the reserve account. This will rarely, if ever, occur. The reserve for bad debts account usually has a credit balance at the end of a fiscal period. The additional allowance for bad debts provided for at the close of the period is added to this balance, and the total is carried forward into the next period. As was explained previously, this total is subtracted from the total of the accounts receivable on the balance sheet.

Bad Debts Collected 

Sometimes a customer's account that was thought to be uncollectible, and was charged against the reserve for bad debts account, is subsequently collected. Such a collection should be shown in the customer's account so that his account will contain all the information needed for credit purposes. The amount that was formerly written off as a bad debt is re-entered in the customer's account by debiting Accounts Receivable in the general ledger and the customer's account in the accounts receivable ledger. The income-tax regulations require that bad debts collected be reported as income of the year in which they are collected. It is desirable, therefore, to

[Ch. 10 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

credit them to a special account so that their amount can be easily ascertained when the income tax return is prepared. This account is called Bad Debts Collected.

For example, if R. C. Howell, whose account was closed on 

February 25 because it was believed to be uncollectible, pays on 

September 20 the $72.30 he owes, the following entries will be. made in the journal and the cash receipts journal: 

Sept. 20

Accounts Receivable-R. C. Howell... 

Bad Debts Collected.. 

To reinstate a customer's account that was charged off as uncollectible on February 25 but that has now been collected. 72 30 72 30

7230 72 30 The amount entered in the Accounts Receivable Cr. column will be posted to the credit side of R. C. Howell's account and will also be included in the total that will be posted to the credit side of the accounts receivable account. The customer's account will then be in balance.

At the end of the fiscal period the balance of Bad Debts Collected may be transferred directly to the capital account. The same final result is obtained if the credit is made to profit and loss summary. If the credit is posted to the capital account, it is assumed to be a reduction in the expenses recorded in previous periods; if it is posted to the profit and loss summary account, it is assumed to be an income of the present period. Either point of view may be assumed, but the credit to the capital account is usually considered to be the more logical. 

If the reserve for bad debts is maintained at a certain percentage of accounts receivable or sales, the amount of bad debts collected may be credited to the reserve account instead of to the bad debts collected account. This procedure makes the credit balance of the reserve account greater and thereby reduces the

Ch. 10] 241 

VALUATION ACCOUNTS 

amount to be debited to Loss from Bad Debts and credited to Reserve for Bad Debts. It therefore increases the profit, and thus the capital, in the same manner as if the transfer had been made directly to Profit and Loss Summary or to the capital account. 

Obviously, this method can be used only when the amount credited to Reserve for Bad Debts at the end of a fiscal period is an amount sufficient to increase the balance of the reserve account to a fixed percentage of total sales, sales on account, or accounts receivable. If the amount added to the reserve is a fixed percentage of total sales, sales on account, or accounts receivable, and is added regardless of the balance of the reserve account, the reserve account cannot be credited for bad debts collected, for then the increase in the profit, derived from the collection, would not be shown and the reserve would become too large. 

Other Valuation Accounts 

The two types of accounts described in this chapter have been asset valuation accounts. In addition to asset valuation accounts, as suggested at the opening of the chapter, there may be valuation or offset accounts affecting liability, proprietorship, income, and expense accounts. In later chapters reference will be made to Deficit, which is a proprietorship offset account, and to Discount on Bonds Issued, which is a liability offset account. In previous chapters use has been made of Sales Returns, an offset income account, and of Purchases Returns, an offset expense account.

1. K. D. Lester has typewriters worth $1,100. During a fiscal period he sells a new machine for $100, the cost price. To record the increase in cash and the decrease in the value of the typewriters, he debits Cash and credits the typewriters account. At the end of the fiscal period he estimates that the rest of the typewriters have depreciated 10 per cent. To record this decrease in value, he debits Depreciation of Typewriters and credits the depreciation reserve account. Since the amount of the decrease in the value of the typewriters was the same in each case, why was not the asset account credited in each instance? 

2. (a) What is the trade-in value of machinery purchased for $1,000 and depreciated at the rate of 10 per cent, if, at the end of

[Ch. 10 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

seven years, the machinery is traded in at its book value? (b) 

What journal entry must be made to record the depreciation at the end of each of the seven years? (c) What entries must be made at the end of the seventh year if the old machinery is turned in at book value on new machinery worth $1,500, the balance of the purchase price being paid in cash? 

3. Richard Staun has office equipment worth $2,000 that has been depreciated at the rate of 15 per cent for five years. At the end of the fifth year it is sold for $300 in cash. (a) What entry must be made to record the depreciation for each of the five years? (b) What entries must be made at the time the equipment is sold? 

4. Richard Staun also has delivery equipment valued at $3,000, for which he has set up a depreciation reserve at the rate of 25 per cent a year. At the end of the third year he trades in this equipment and receives an allowance of $1,000 to apply on new delivery equipment worth $2,500, the balance being paid in cash. 

Give the entries required at the time that the new equipment is purchased. 

5. Richard Staun has office furniture worth $1,000 that has been depreciated at the rate of 10 per cent a year for ten years. At the end of the tenth year it is estimated that the office furniture can be used for at least another five years. How should the depreciation for each of the next five years be recorded? 

6. The Monroe Manufacturing Company has fixed assets that, according to estimates, can be used for the following periods: building, fifty years; office furniture, fifteen years; office equipment, ten years; machinery, eight years. How should the rate of depreciation for each of these assets be determined? 

7. During a fiscal period the Ridge Coal Company mines 4,000 carloads of coal. It considers this amount to be about 1 per cent of the coal in the mines. Record in a journal entry, omitting amounts, the decrease in the asset value of the mines. 

8. Robert Tulane has delivery equipment costing $3,000 which he has depreciated for three years at the rate of 20 per cent a year. At the end of the fourth year he estimates the equipment will last two more years. 9. Miller and Company buy your delivery truck for $300, paying $100 in cash on January 5. The truck cost you $800, and you have recorded $450 in Reserve for Depreciation. Ignoring the four days' depreciation in January, give the journal entry for the sale of the truck. 

10. On January 1, 1940, Earl Miller trades his old delivery truck for a new one costing $1,500 and receives $500 allowance on the old truck. The old truck cost $1,400 on August 1, 1938, and has been depreciated on December 31 each year on the assumption that it would be used five years and then have a $200 trade-in value. The balance on the new truck is paid in cash. Give the journal entry. 

11. Illustrate by means of a chart the straight-line method and the reducing-fraction method of calculating depreciation for a machine costing $1,650, estimated to last five years and to have a scrap value of $150. 

12. The Regal Clothing Company loses on bad accounts about ½ of 1 per cent of its net sales. When an account proves to be uncollectible, the company debits Loss from Bad Debts and credits Accounts Receivable. Do you consider this method satisfactory? 

13. The Hammar Hardware Company decides to establish a reserve for bad debts, to which a credit is to be made at the end of each fiscal period. The management is uncertain whether the reserve should be a percentage of the accounts receivable at the end of the period, a percentage of the total sales for the period, or a percentage of the sales on account for the period. Which do you consider to be the most logical plan of recording the loss from bad debts? 

14. H. C. Browning maintains a reserve for bad debts amounting to 2 per cent of the accounts receivable at the end of the fiscal period. At the beginning of one fiscal period the reserve for bad debts account has a credit balance of $1,000. During the fiscal period bad debts amounting to $1,200 are written off the books. 

Give the journal entries required to write off the bad debts and to set up a new reserve. (The balance of the accounts receivable account may be assumed to remain at $50,000.) 

15. Marshall Carroll adds to his reserve for bad debts 1 per cent of the sales made during each fiscal period. As the actual amounts

[Ch. 10 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

written off are small, the reserve gradually increases until, after a few years, the balance of the reserve account is $10,000. Since the maximum loss in any one fiscal period has not exceeded $2,000, it is evident that the reserve is excessive. How can the reserve account be adjusted so that the reserve will be reasonable? 

16. Marshall Carroll has on his books an account receivable for $100 due from John Hay. He decides that this account is uncollectible. Give the journal entry required to write off this debt. 

17. In the next fiscal period John Hay pays the debt that Marshall 

Carroll had written off his books (Problem No. 16). (a) Give two ways in which Carroll may record this transaction in his books. (b) Explain the advantages of each method. 

18. There is a balance of $19,000 in the Reserve for Bad Debts account, a balance of $20,000 in Accounts Receivable. Assuming a reserve of 5 per cent of accounts receivable to be adequate, give the correcting entry. 

19. “All machinery is on an irresistible march to the junk heap, and its progress, while it may be delayed, cannot be prevented by repairs." Discuss the application of this sentence to depreciation procedures in accounting.

CHAPTER XI 

Need for Consideration The life of a business is divided into periods of time known as fiscal periods. It was formerly customary to make the fiscal period one year; the present tendency is to make it a shorter period of time. At the end of each fiscal period two statements are prepared: one showing the assets, the liabilities, and the proprietorship at the end of the period; the other showing the income and the expenses for the period. True statements of the financial condition of the business and of the incomes and expenses ordinarily cannot be prepared from the ledger accounts alone, since there are some income and expense items that are not recorded currently in the accounts. 

Commodities and services that have been purchased during the period are frequently on hand at the end of the period, and payments may have been received during the period for commodities and services that have not been delivered by the end of the period. 

These conditions make it necessary, at the end of the fiscal period, to consider accrued and deferred items.

Under the heading of accruals are the income and the expenses, and the corresponding assets and liabilities, that have accrued from day to day but have not been recorded. For example, if a business borrows $1,000 at 6 per cent, the interest expense and the liability interest payable increase each day. Similarly, if it lends $1,000 at 6 per cent, the interest income and the asset interest receivable increase each day. Obviously, it is not practicable to record the increase in the expense and the liability, or in the income and the asset, each day. Furthermore, no purpose would be served by this method because complete information is needed only at the end of the fiscal period. 

Under the heading of deferred items are deferred charges to expense and deferred credits to income. Deferred charges to expense are the amounts that have been paid for expenses but that are really applicable to a future period. Deferred credits to income 249

[Ch. 11 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

are the amounts that have been received in advance of the service rendered and that will be earned in succeeding periods. 

Accrued Income

Income may be earned in one fiscal period, but it may not be due and receivable until a subsequent period. Accurate accounting demands that each period be given credit for the income earned during that period, whether it has been collected or will be collected later. To illustrate: On November 1 a business receives from a customer a note for $1,000 due in four months with interest at 6 per cent. On 

December 31 one half of this interest has been earned; that is, one half of the service for which the interest is to be received has been performed. If the books are closed on December 31, one half of the total interest to be received, or $10, should appear on the balance sheet as an asset and also on the statement of profit and loss as income earned during the period. 

Since this item of accrued interest will be reported on the balance sheet and the statement of profit and loss, it must also be recorded in the accounts. This is accomplished by an adjusting entry that debits the asset account termed Interest Receivable and credits the income account Interest Earned. To illustrate: 

During the year $97 was earned and received as interest on notes receivable. In each case when the payment of the note receivable was recorded, Interest Earned was credited for the amount of the interest. At the end of the year, however, there is accrued interest on notes receivable amounting to $10. This amount represents interest that the business has earned but that will not be collectible until a later date. The accrued interest is recorded by the following journal entry:

GENERAL JOURNAL

[Ch. 11

The entry may be recorded by debiting Cash for the payment and crediting Interest Earned for the amount of the income applicable to the present period and Interest Receivable for the amount already recorded as income for the previous period. 

It is inconvenient, however, to analyze each receipt of interest to see if a part of it should be credited to an asset account. This is especially true if there is a number of notes on which interest has accrued. At the beginning of the new fiscal period, therefore, the amount of the accrued interest is transferred from Interest Receivable to Interest Earned by a reversing entry, so called because it reverses the adjusting entry made at the end of the preceding period. Ch. 11] 253 

When the $20 in interest is received on March 1, the entire amount may be credited to the interest earned account. The difference between the credit item of $20 and the debit item of $10 in the interest earned account will be the interest earned in the new period. This amount will be the same as that which would have been obtained if the entry had been analyzed and divided between Interest Earned and Interest Receivable.

The discussion of the treatment of accrued interest on notes receivable serves to illustrate the method of handling items of accrued income in general. If, in addition to accrued interest on notes receivable, there are other items of accrued income at the end of a fiscal period, separate accounts may be set up to record the assets arising from the income that will be received in a subsequent period. Each of these accounts will be of the same nature as the account with interest receivable. When such items are numerous, one asset account, termed Accrued Receivables, may be opened. All accrued assets may be recorded as debits to this account instead of to separate accounts. In this case the nature of each item should be stated in the Explanation column. These accrued assets are reported on the balance sheet as current assets. 

Whether the one account or separate accounts are set up to record the accrued receivables, the proper income accounts are credited for the income earned during the period but not yet collected. 

The accounts thus reflect the accrued income. This income is reported on the statement of profit and loss as income of the period just ended.

Accrued Expenses 

Just as the accounts should show, at the end of the fiscal period, all the income earned during the period, they should also show all the expenses incurred in earning that income. In addition to the expenses recorded in the accounts, there may be expenses that have accrued but have not been recorded because payment will not be due until later. For example, a business firm may pay its employees their weekly salaries on Saturday. If its fiscal period ends on Wednesday, it will owe its employees salaries for three days, but it will not pay the employees until three days later. The accrued salaries should be reported as a liability on the balance sheet and as an expense of the current period on the statement of profit and loss.

[Ch. 11 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

If accrued salaries are reported on the balance sheet and the statement of profit and loss, they must be recorded in accounts. 

This is accomplished by an adjusting entry that debits an expense account such as Sales Salaries and credits a liability account such as Salaries Payable. To illustrate: The sales salaries account has a balance of $3,632.90 at the end of the year. The accrued sales salaries amount to $57. To record the amount that the business owes the salespeople, payment of which will be made in the new fiscal period, the following adjusting entry is made:

When the closing entries are made on December 31, the sales salaries account will be closed into the profit and loss summary account just as other expense accounts. When the $114 in wages is paid on January 3, the entire amount can be debited to the sales salaries account. The difference between the debit item of $114 and the credit item of $57 in this account will be an expense of the year in which the $114 is paid. 

The discussion of the treatment of accrued salaries serves to illustrate the method of handling items of accrued expense in general. If, in addition to accrued salaries, there are other items of accrued expense at the end of a fiscal period, separate accounts may be set up to record as liabilities the claims of others that represent expense items consumed during the period but not payable until a subsequent period. Each of these accounts will be of the same nature as the account with accrued salaries. When such items are numerous, one liability account, termed Accrued Payables, may be opened. All accrued liabilities may be recorded as credits to this account instead of to separate accounts. In this case the nature of each item should be stated in the Explanation column. The accrued liabilities are reported on the balance sheet as current liabilities. Whether the one account or separate accounts are set up to record the accrued liabilities, the proper expense accounts are debited for the expenses incurred during the period but not yet paid. The accounts thus reflect the accrued expenses. These expenses are reported on the statement of profit and loss as expenses of the period just ended. All entries for accrued income and accrued expenses are, of course, made in the general journal when the other adjusting entries are recorded at the end of the fiscal period.

Interest Payable on sume payment of a note, on which interest 

Transferred Notes has accrued. The interest accrued on a note receivable is an asset and could be charged to an asset account. 

It is usually more convenient, however, to debit Interest Earned so that, when payment of the note is received, the amount of interest will not have to be analyzed to determine which portion of the interest is a cancellation of the asset and which represents income of the current period. This is the same reason why a reversing entry for the accrued asset is made at the beginning of a fiscal period. For example: On December 15 Robert Chase receives from B. H.

When a business assumes payment of a note, the accrued interest on the note could be credited to a liability account, but it is more convenient to credit Interest Cost. This method is used for the same reason that a reversing entry is made at the beginning of a fiscal period to transfer the accrued liability to the interest cost account. Deferred Charges to Deferred charges to expense represent com- Expense modities and services that have been purchased for consumption but are unconsumed at the end of the fiscal period. Although such items are purchased and paid for in one period, the unused portion will not become an expense until consumed in a subsequent period. They are therefore frequently termed prepaid expenses. Prepaid expenses are of various kinds. 

In this chapter office supplies and prepaid insurance are discussed to illustrate the procedure in handling all prepaid expenses at the close of a fiscal period. 

Office Supplies. The cost of stationery, pencils, pens, ink, and similar items is debited to Office Supplies at the time of purchase. 

At the end of the fiscal period an inventory of office supplies is taken, and the amount of the inventory is subtracted from the balance of the office supplies account to ascertain the cost of the supplies used during the period. The inventory of office supplies will appear on the balance sheet as an asset under the heading "Deferred Charges" or "Deferred Charges to Expense." The cost of the office supplies used will appear on the statement of profit and loss as a general expense. Since these facts will appear on the reports, they should also be recorded in accounts. 

To illustrate, it may be assumed that the office supplies account has a balance of $837.50 at the close of the year. This amount represents the value of the supplies on hand at the beginning of the year plus the cost of the supplies purchased during the year. On December 31 the inventory of office supplies is $66.90. Since office supplies costing $837.50 were available for use during the year, and supplies valued at $66.90 are on hand on December 31, supplies amounting to $770.60 must have been used during the year. When there is a large number of expenses

Ch. 11] 259 

that are to be classified according to their nature on the statement of profit and loss, it is usually preferable to record each expense in a separate account. In this case the expense due to the consumption of office supplies is charged to the office supplies used account. The journal entry to adjust the office supplies account is: 

Dec. 31

Office Supplies Used. 

Office Supplies..

It would have been possible to debit the profit and loss summary account and to credit the office supplies account for the amount of the supplies consumed. The better practice, however, is to show in the expense section of the ledger all the expenses of the period, including those arising from the consumption of assets. 

Prepaid Insurance. When insurance premiums are paid, the payments are debited to a prepaid insurance account. At the end of the fiscal period the amount of the insurance premiums expired during the period is ascertained from the policies; or, if an insurance policy register is maintained, the amount of expired

[Ch. 11 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

insurance is found by obtaining the total of the column showing the expirations for the period. The difference between the total of the premiums paid, which is shown by the prepaid insurance account, and the total of the premiums expired, which is determined from the policies or the insurance policy register, is the amount of unexpired insurance. This amount is reported as a deferred charge to expense on the balance sheet. The expired insurance is analyzed according to the various types of property insured, and the amount of expired insurance applicable to each kind of property is reported on the statement of profit and loss in the proper expense group. For example, expired insurance on merchandise is considered a selling expense; that on delivery equipment, a delivery expense; and that on office equipment and buildings, a general expense.

To illustrate, it may be assumed that the prepaid insurance account has a balance of $499.74 at the close of the year. This amount represents the unexpired insurance at the beginning of the year plus the total of the premiums paid during the year. 

On December 31 it is ascertained from the insurance policy register that the following insurance has expired during the year: $105 on merchandise, $211.30 on delivery equipment, $46.84 on store fixtures, and $9.34 on office equipment. The expired insurance is recorded by the following adjusting entry:

Inventories of Expense Not infrequently deferred expenses are Accounts charged directly to expense accounts as they are incurred. There is no objection to this practice if the assets are consumed largely during the current fiscal period. For example, if office supplies are purchased frequently and in small amounts, they may be charged to an office supplies used account on the assumption that they will be consumed largely during the current fiscal period. At the end of the fiscal period the amount of the inventory is debited to an office supplies inventory account and credited to the office supplies used account. The balances of the office supplies inventory and the office supplies used accounts will show the same facts as the accounts that were used in the method discussed in the preceding paragraphs. The financial reports will also show the same data. 

Deferred Credits to

Income Income that is received during one period may not have been earned during that period but will be earned in a following period. Such items of income are known as deferred credits to income. For example, a publishing company receives payments for subscriptions to its publications in advance for a period of time extending beyond the end of the current fiscal period. That portion of the payments that is applicable to subsequent fiscal periods represents a deferred credit to income. When a bank discounts the note of a customer, it may deduct interest for a period extending beyond the end of its current fiscal period. That portion of the interest that is applicable to a subsequent fiscal period represents, on the records of the bank, a deferred credit to income. Other examples of deferred credits to income are rent received in advance by a business on property owned, storage charges received in advance by a warehouse company or a garage, premiums received in advance by an insurance company, tuition received in advance by a school, an annual retainer's fee received in advance by an attorney, and amounts received in advance by an advertising firm for advertising service to be rendered in the future. In each of these cases the unearned portion of the income received is, at the close of the fiscal period, a deferred credit to the income of the subsequent period or periods in which it will be earned.

[Ch. 11 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

By accepting payment for the commodity or the service in advance, a business renders itself liable to furnish the commodity or the service at some future time. If, at the end of the fiscal period, some portion of the commodity or the service has been furnished, part of the liability has been discharged and part of the income has been earned. The earned income appears on the statement of profit and loss as part of the income of the period. 

The unearned portion of the payment represents a liability of the business to furnish the commodity or the service in a future period, and is reported on the balance sheet as a liability under the heading "Deferred Credits to Income." Since these facts appear on the reports, they should also be recorded in accounts. 

To illustrate: On December 31 the subscriptions income ac count in the ledger of the Daily World has a credit balance of $29,175.50. The records of the company show that $3,927 of this balance represents unearned subscriptions. The earned income from subscriptions therefore is $25,248.50. The unearned subscriptions are transferred to the account Unearned Subscriptions by the following journal entry: 

Dec. 31

The amount of subscriptions

Periodic Entries 

In this and the preceding chapter, some accounts that are not kept currently from day to day are presented. Such accounts ordinarily represent income or expenses that are not recorded as they occur. They must, however, be brought up to date before the accounting statements are prepared at the end of the fiscal period. The entries that bring accounts up to date arise, not from business transactions, but from the necessity for synchronizing account balances with predetermined accounting periods. Such entries may be called periodic entries since they are made only at the close of the accounting period.

The three classes of periodic entries are (1) adjusting entries, (2) closing entries, and (3) reversing entries. Adjusting entries cause the account balances to agree with a predetermined fiscal period. Closing entries close the temporary proprietorship accounts and summarize the items reported on the statement of profit and loss. Reversing entries reopen temporary proprietorship accounts from which the balances were removed temporarily for the purpose of closing the books. All these entries are incidental to the accounting procedures that take place at the end of a fiscal period. They record no new transactions; they simply synchronize previously recorded transactions with the appropriate fiscal periods. 

Periodic entries occasionally require the opening of new accounts in the ledger. Such accounts may be called periodic accounts. 

Periodic accounts are opened in the ledger solely to facilitate the preparation of the accounting statements. They are distinguished

Ch. 11] 265 

from other accounts appearing on the balance sheet and the statement of profit and loss in that they have no definite period of existence so far as the ledger is concerned. They are opened at the time the books are closed and are closed immediately thereafter.

When accounts are classified according to the period of their use, they may be divided into three groups. The first group contains those accounts that are used as long as the business exists; the second, those that are used only during the predetermined accounting period; and the third, those that are used only at the time the ledger is closed. Balance sheet accounts, like cash, office equipment, and accounts payable, function during the life of the business. They are opened when the business begins; they are closed when the business ends. Statement of profit and loss accounts, like sales, purchases, sales salaries, and delivery expense, last as long as the accounting period. They are then closed, and a new set of accounts is opened. These accounts have no significance except when the period of their life is known. They repeat themselves in each accounting period, for after entries have been recorded in them during the period they are closed. The accounts belonging to the third group have no material period of existence. 

They are opened at the end of the accounting period and are closed at approximately the same time. Depreciation of Furniture and 

Fixtures is an example of this class of accounts. 

1. Harold Cobb, the manager of a weekly newspaper in a town. with a population of ten thousand, records receipts from subscriptions as income and payments of expenses as operating costs.

CHAPTER XII  

Need for Adjusting,

Closing, and Reversing 

Entries 

At the end of the fiscal period it is necessary to make certain adjustments in order that the accounts will reflect the correct financial condition of the business. The merchandise inventory at the end of the period, the estimated loss from bad debts, the depreciation on fixed assets, the accrued income and the accrued expenses, and the deferred charges to expense and the deferred credits to income must be recorded. These adjustments are made in the accounts by means of adjusting entries. After the adjustments have been made in the accounts, it is necessary to transfer the balances of the income and expense accounts to the profit and loss summary account in order to summarize in the ledger the income and expenses for the period and to transfer the net profit or the net loss to the proprietor's capital account. The entries by which this is done are known as closing entries. After the statement of profit and loss accounts have been closed and only balance sheet accounts remain on the ledger, the balances in certain accounts representing accrued and deferred items should be transferred to new statement of profit and loss accounts. This transfer is accomplished by reversing entries described in the previous chapter. 

Procedure for Preparing There is no standard procedure for making the Periodic Summary the periodic summary. A convenient procedure, which will be followed in this chapter and in later ones, is as follows: 

1. Prepare a trial balance. 

2. Compile the data that will be needed in adjusting the accounts. A good way to insure that none of the data will be omitted is to consider each item in the trial balance in the order in which it appears and to determine if any adjustment will be necessary. 

3. Prepare a work sheet from the trial balance and the data for the adjustments. 271

[Ch. 12 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

4. Prepare a statement of profit and loss from the Statement of 

Profit and Loss columns of the working sheet, and a balance sheet from the Balance Sheet columns. 

5. Record the adjusting entries in the journal, using the Adjustments columns of the working sheet as a basis. 

6. Record the journal entries required to close the income and expense accounts, and to transfer the net profit or the net loss to the proprietor's personal account and the balance of this account to the capital account. 

7. Post the adjusting and closing entries to the accounts in the ledger, and rule the accounts that are in balance or that it is desirable to balance. 

8. Prepare a post-closing trial balance. 

9. Prepare reversing journal entries to close the accrued income, accrued expenses, deferred charges to expense, and deferred credits to income into the income and expense accounts that will be used in recording the transactions of the new fiscal period. Post the reversing entries, and rule the accounts that are in balance.

If this procedure is compared with that followed in Chapter VI, it will be observed that the principal differences in completing the periodic summary are (1) the number of adjustments and (2) the preparation of reversing entries, which are required by accrued and deferred items.

Preparation of the Work The work required at the end of the period Sheet may be illustrated from the records of R. A. Dunn. After the data for the adjustments have been compiled, the trial balance is copied in the Trial Balance columns of the work sheet. The adjustments are then entered in the debit and credit 

Adjustments columns of the work sheet according to whether the amounts are to be posted as debits or credits to the accounts. 

When an adjustment affects an account that does not appear in the trial balance, the name of the account is written below the trial balance in the Name of Account column and the amount is entered on the same line in the proper Adjustments column. (See the work sheet in Illustration No. 31, pages 274 and 275.)

The debit and the credit for each adjustment are identified on the work sheet by small letters in parenthesis. This plan is helpful when the bookkeeper later records the adjusting entries in the journal. 

After the adjustments have been entered in the Adjustments columns, the equality of the debits and the credits of the adjustments is proved by adding the columns. The amounts in the Adjustments columns are then combined with the amounts in the trial balance, and the resulting amounts are taken over into the Adjusted Trial Balance columns on the work sheet. These amounts are then extended into the Statement of Profit and Loss and the Balance Sheet columns of the work sheet. Preparation of the State ment of Profit and Loss from the Work Sheet 

The statement of profit and loss is prepared from the entries in the Statement of Profit and Loss columns of the work sheet. The items that appear in the Statement of Profit and Loss columns are shown in the order in which they are used on the report with the exception of those that are listed below the trial balance. 

The latter items must therefore be used in the proper places on the report. The method of preparing the statement of profit and loss from the Statement of Profit and Loss columns of the work sheet will be understood readily by tracing each amount in these columns to the report shown in Illustration No. 32 on page 277. 

Preparation of the Balance Sheet from the

Work Sheet 

The balance sheet is prepared from the entries in the Balance Sheet columns of the work sheet. The method of preparing the balance sheet from these columns will be understood readily by tracing each amount in the Balance Sheet columns of the work sheet to the report shown in Illustration No. 33 on page 278.

The proprietorship is the difference between the total assets and the total liabilities. It is also the balance of the capital account used on the work sheet plus the difference between the net income for the period and balance of the proprietor's personal account. 

Adjusting Entries 

After the financial reports have been prepared, the adjusting entries are recorded in the journal. The Adjustments columns of the work sheet are used as the basis of these entries. After these entries have been posted, the accounts will reflect the financial condition of the business at the end of the fiscal period. 

Closing Entries 

The closing entries are then recorded in the journal. These entries summarize in the profit and loss summary account the income and expenses for the period. By means of these entries the net income is also transferred from the profit and loss summary account to the proprie tor's personal account, and the balance of the latter account is
