Ch. 12] 279 

closed into the capital account. The adjusting and closing entries of R. A. Dunn are shown in Illustration No. 35 on pages 280 to 282. 

It will be observed that the operating expenses have been closed into the profit and loss summary account by three entries, one entry being used for each classification of expenses. These expenses might have been closed into Profit and Loss Summary by means of separate entries, but the method used by R. A. Dunn is sometimes desirable because the profit and loss summary account then shows the totals of the various groups of the separate expense items. The accounts representing other income and other expenses are also closed in this manner. 

The profit and loss summary account may be composed of a number of sections. In the following illustration this account is divided into three sections corresponding to the major divisions of the statement of profit and loss. The first section is known as the trading section; it includes all the items that are used in determining the gross profit on sales for the period. The credit balance in this section of the account, which represents the gross profit, is brought down to the second section. All the items that are needed in determining the net profit or the net loss from operations are shown in this section. The balance then represents the net profit or the net loss, and it is brought down to the third section. 

Other income and expense items are closed into this section, and the balance of the account is transferred to the proprietor's personal account.

Ch. 12] 283 

After the adjusting and closing entries shown in Illustration No. 35 on pages 280 to 282 have been posted to Dunn's ledger, and the accounts have been balanced and ruled, only the asset accounts, the liability accounts, and the proprietor's capital account remain open. The balances of these accounts correspond exactly with the amounts on the balance sheet in Illustration No. 33. 

The Post-Closing Trial 

Balance At the end of a fiscal period the equality of the debits and the credits in the ledger is determined by means of a trial balance. The adjusting and closing entries are then made. If these entries are recorded and posted correctly, the equilibrium of the debits and the credits in the ledger is not disturbed because the entries consist of equal debits and credits. A post-closing trial balance, Illustration No. 36 

is therefore taken to test the accuracy of these entries.

ILLUSTRATION No. 36, R. A. DUNN'S POST-CLOSING TRIAL BALANCE 

Reversing Entries

In Chapter XI it was explained that, after the ledger has been closed, the balances of the accounts containing accrued expenses, deferred charges to

[Ch. 12 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

expense, and deferred credits to income are usually transferred to the income and expense accounts to which they pertain. The entries by which this is accomplished are called reversing entries, and they are dated as of the first day of the new fiscal period. 

The ledger of R. A. Dunn contains one accrued income account (Interest Receivable), two accrued expense accounts (Interest Payable and Salaries and Wages Payable), a deferred charge to an expense account (Prepaid Interest on Notes Payable), and one deferred credit to an income account (Prepaid Interest on Notes Receivable). The reversing entries required to close these accounts are shown below. The other accounts with deferred charges to expense (Store Supplies, Delivery Supplies, Office Supplies, and Prepaid Insurance) are not to be closed because transactions will be recorded in these accounts during the new fiscal period. After the reversing entries have been posted, the ledger of R. A. Dunn will be ready for the entries to record the transactions of the new period.

1. Four businesses located within a short distance of one another have fiscal periods of one month, three months, six months, and one year respectively. Which do you consider is following the best accounting practice? 

2. C. S. Berne hires a new bookkeeper shortly before the close of the fiscal period. At the end of the period the bookkeeper plans to close the books without preparing a work sheet. As Berne never examines the work sheet, he considers that the bookkeeper would be wasting time if he prepared it. Do you recommend that the bookkeeper prepare a work sheet? Why? 

3. In the preparation of the work sheet, the bookkeeper of R. B. 

Samuels records the reserve for bad debts and the reserves for depreciation in the credit column under the heading "Balance

Sheet." Samuels believes that this method overstates the liabilities. He therefore suggests that the balances of the reserve accounts be deducted from the amounts of the corresponding assets and only the net values of the assets be shown in the Assets column under the heading "Balance Sheet." Which method do you prefer? 

4. In the work sheet illustrated on pages 274 and 275, the bookkeeper has extended the amount of the purchases returns and allowances into the credit column under the heading "Statement of Profit and Loss." Do you consider purchases returns and allowances to be an income of the business? 

5. The personal account of R. A. Dunn had a debit balance in the trial balance. This amount was extended into the debit 

Balance Sheet column of the work sheet (Illustration No. 31 on pages 274 and 275). (a) Do you consider this method to be correct? (b) Is there any other way in which this item might have been handled? (c) Which method do you consider preferable? 

6. It has been suggested to R. A. Dunn that, instead of the tencolumn work sheet illustrated on pages 274 and 275, he use an eight-column work sheet. This form would have the same columns as those shown in the illustration except the columns entitled "Adjusted Trial Balance Dr. and Cr." This form would involve fewer entries than the one illustrated. Which type do you consider preferable?

[Ch. 12 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

7. At what stage in the completion of the work at the end of a fiscal period could the bookkeeper take a trial balance that would correspond to the adjusted trial balance on the work sheet? 

8. Do you recommend that the reversing entries be dated to correspond to the last day of the old fiscal period or to the first day of the new? 

9. R. G. Dawson, a retail merchant, sells on account and occasionally receives notes from customers in payment of accounts. 

He issues notes to banks in procurement of loans with which to pay creditors. He rents the building in which his business is conducted, paying the rent quarterly in advance. He owns the furniture and fixtures used in his store and in his office and the two motor trucks used in delivering merchandise to customers. Sales salaries are paid monthly; other salaries, weekly. State the items on Dawson's trial balance that will probably require adjustment at the end of the fiscal period. 

10. On December 31 19--, the end of the annual fiscal period, the bookkeeper of the H. R. Mason Company must make adjustments for the following items: (a) Interest accrued to date on notes receivable is $6.85. (b) The loss from bad debts for the past three years has averaged 1 per cent of sales. The sales for the current year are $100,000. (c) The inventory of merchandise on December 31, 19--, is $16,245.65. (d) Office equipment costing $275 and store fixtures costing $750 were purchased on July 1. It is estimated that this equipment will be of service for ten years from the date of purchase and, at the end of that time, will have the following scrap values: office equipment, $25; store fixtures, $50. (e) Office supplies costing $150 and store supplies costing $425.15 were available for use during the fiscal period. Office supplies amounting to $50 and store supplies worth $100 are on hand on December 31, 19--. (f) On January 1 the prepaid insurance on merchandise was $40. This policy expired on June 30. On July 1 a new policy on merchandise stock was taken out for a period of one year, the premium being $80. This amount was debited to the prepaid insurance account. (g) Interest accrued to date on notes payable is $17.90. (h) On November 1, 19--, the firm's 90-day, non-interest-bearing note for $2,000 was discounted at the Mercantile Trust 

Company at 6 per cent.

Ch. 12] 287 

(i) Salaries that have accrued since the last pay day amount to $360. Of this amount $110 is owed to office employees and $250 to store clerks. (a) Give the entries that will be necessary to make these adjustments. (b) Assuming that a work sheet is prepared, state how each of these adjusting entries will be entered on it. (c) How will the items be shown on the reports? 

11. W. M. Older closes his books monthly. He owes $5,000 on a 

6 per cent note on which the interest is payable semi-annually. 

His customers owe him $10,000, which amount is represented by several notes, each due six months from date and bearing interest at 6 per cent. (a) Describe the method by which he will find the amount of accrued interest at the end of each month. (b) How will the accrued interest be recorded? (c) Will reversing entries be equally necessary for the accrued interest on both the note payable and the notes receivable? 

12. What is the difference in the purpose of (a) adjusting entries, (b) closing entries, and (c) reversing entries?

CHAPTER XIII BUSINESS PAPERS 

First Evidence of In modern business the journal record is Transactions no longer the original written evidence of the transaction. Every important business action is today described on some paper. If it is of sufficient importance, the paper includes the signature of a responsible officer. The movement of business involves the accompanying preparation and use of papers. 

The business paper is made up at the time the transaction takes place. This paper gives certainty to the understanding between the two parties to the contract. It may be a slip from a cash register that is given to the customer who has made a cash purchase, or it may be an elaborate many-paged contract involving the production of a specialized commodity. In either case it reduces the transaction to writing and thus greatly lessens the chance of subsequent misunderstandings on the part of the two parties. 

The business paper may be a single form, such as a check signed by the debtor and indorsed by the creditor. In the case of a sale it may be duplicate sales tickets made out by a sales clerk, the original being given to the purchaser and the carbon kept as the seller's record. Whatever its form, whether single, duplicate, triplicate, or more, the business paper has become an increasingly useful device in modern business. 

Business papers enable the accountant or the bookkeeper to demonstrate that his records are accurate and that they truthfully represent the results of transactions completed. They keep the recording department in touch with the transactions of the business, no matter where or by whom the transactions are carried out.

The larger the organization, the greater the need for business papers. In a large department store hundreds of clerks make sales in the different departments of the store; a number of buyers purchase goods in the scattered markets of the world; cash is received in the various departments as a result of sales, as well as from other sources, and is sent to the cashier; and cash is paid out 291

[Ch. 13 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

under the direction of the treasurer. Such an organization would find it impossible to operate without the use of innumerable business papers.

Similar conditions are found, though perhaps on a smaller scale, in practically every business. Those who record the transactions perform few, if any, of them. 

The information necessary to make the record must be communicated to them by some means. The business paper provides the means. Even if the same person performs a transaction and makes the record of it, he is usually unable to do both simultaneously; hence he must have some memorandum of the transaction to use as a basis of the record to be made later. 

The business paper prepared by one completing the transaction facilitates the performance of the transaction, informs the accountant or the bookkeeper of the effect of the transaction on the accounts, and serves as his authority for making an accounting entry.

Business papers are commonly called vouchers. A voucher may be defined briefly as any written evidence supporting a business transaction. The use of some of the more common vouchers and the relation of these vouchers to the accounting system will be discussed in this chapter. ness Vouchers

Classification of Busi- The papers, or vouchers, used in business are as various and as complex as the transactions that characterize business. Only those that are most generally employed in the average business enterprise will be discussed here. Other business papers will be presented in subsequent chapters in connection with the accounting subjects treated. The following classification will cover those presented now: 

1. Vouchers used in purchases. 

2. Vouchers used in sales. 

3. Vouchers used in transactions with a bank. 

4. Negotiable vouchers, used primarily in the settlement of obligations. 

5. Miscellaneous vouchers. 

Vouchers Used in

Purchases 

There are many ways of handling purchases, every business having its more or less individual method. The purchase routine, like the entire

Ch. 13] 293 

BUSINESS PAPERS 

accounting system, should be adapted to the internal organization of the business. In a small business the buying may be done by the owner himself or by an assistant. In a large business there is usually a purchasing department under the control of a purchasing manager or agent.

Four business papers are commonly used in connection with purchases. Their names and functions are as follows:

Name 1. Purchase requisition 2. Purchase order 3. Purchase invoice 

4. Debit or credit memorandum

Function initiates the buying activity. authorizes the vendor to ship goods. shows the indebtedness for the goods bought. adjusts the indebtedness.

Other papers may be used, but these are typical of the average business.

The Purchase Requisition. If the business has a purchasing department, the purchasing agent will make a purchase in response to a written request from an individual representing some department of the business. For example, the general office may request the purchasing department to buy stationery for office use, or the stores department may request the purchase of materials to be used in manufacturing the commodity produced by the business. Such a request is termed a purchase requisition. The requisition may be made in various forms, but it should contain at least the information shown in Illustration No. 37, page 294.

The purchasing agent, it should be noted, does not determine what is to be bought or when the purchase is to be made. His function is to obtain goods when they are wanted. This does not mean that he cannot take the initiative in calling to the attention of departments the advantage of anticipating their needs in order to benefit from favorable markets. It does mean that whatever is bought results from an agreement between the person representing the department that needs the commodity and the purchasing agent. 

The purchase requisition initiates buying activity on the part of the purchasing department. Except for extraordinary purchases the purchasing agent should have a purchase requisition as authority for each purchase order that he issues.

The Purchase Order. Whatever the organization of a business may be, the relations with a vendor are initiated by the placing of a purchase order for the commodity to be bought. When the business has a purchasing department, this order is usually made on a special order blank. In other cases the order may consist of only a letter written by the proprietor or his assistant, or merely a verbal order given to a representative of the firm from which the purchase is made. In the last case, however, the oral order should be confirmed by a written statement, and a copy of this statement should be retained in the files, because the business should have, in some form, a copy of each purchase order issued. 

The purchase order may be made in any one of various forms, but it should contain at least the information shown in Illustration No. 38.

At least two copies of the purchase order should be made. 

The original should be sent to the vendor, and the duplicate should be retained by the purchasing department. Additional copies may be made for the use of other departments if they are desired. 

Neither of the two business papers just described is used as the basis of an accounting entry; yet both of them provide useful information. They are preliminary to the purchase invoice, which is the basis of the entry in the purchases journal.

The Purchase Invoice. The purchaser usually receives an invoice from the creditor before the goods are delivered. A purchase invoice is an itemized statement of goods bought, and should contain the names and the addresses of both the buyer and the seller, the date of the sale, the terms, the method of shipment, and the quantities, descriptions, and prices of the merchandise. It may contain other information. An invoice is shown in Illustration 

No. 39, page 296.

An invoice received by a purchaser should be sent directly to the purchasing department or to the person who is responsible for the purchase. The terms, quantities, prices, and other information indicated on the invoice should be compared with the corresponding items on the copy of the purchase order. When the goods arrive, they should be counted and inspected. Counting and inspection are ordinarily performed by the receiving department. The receiving department may check the quantities received against those indicated on the invoice, or it may give an independent report of the quantities received to the purchasing agent. In this case the report will be clipped to the invoice. 

Although it is preferable that, before payment is made, the goods be counted and inspected and the quantities be checked with those listed on the invoice, this procedure is sometimes im-

The Debit or the Credit Memorandum. If the goods are damaged, inferior in quality, or short in quantity, an adjustment in the charge made by the vendor must be obtained. In such a case the purchaser may prepare a debit memorandum, which charges the vendor for the amount of the adjustment, or the vendor may issue a credit memorandum, which shows the credit allowed to the purchaser. Vouchers Used in Sales The method of handling sales, like the method of handling purchases, varies greatly, depending upon the organization of the business. Every sale is made in response to an order received from a customer. 

The order given a retail store is usually oral; that given a manufacturing, a wholesale, or a mail-order business is ordinarily written.

Five business papers are commonly used in connection with sales. Their names and functions are as follows:

Name 

1. Sales ticket 

2. Sales invoice 3. Bill of lading 4. Statement of account. 

5. Debit or credit memorandum

Function gives the details of a retail sale. gives the details of a sale made by a manufacturing, a wholesale, or a mail-order business. provides for a shipment to be made by freight. serves as a collecting device. adjusts the indebtedness.

The Sales Invoice. In a manufacturing, a wholesale, or a mailorder business, a written order is received from the customer or from the salesman who obtained it from the customer. After the order has been approved by the credit department, it is sent to the billing department, where the invoice is prepared. 

At least two copies of a sales invoice are made by the billing department. The original is sent to the customer, and the carbon copy is sent to the accounting department for use as the basis of an entry in the sales record. Sometimes additional copies of the invoice are made for the use of different departments of the business. For example, the credit department may desire a copy for use in following up the payment of the invoice, or the treasurer may desire a copy so that he can better estimate the funds to be obtained from sales. Furthermore, a copy of the invoice may be sent to the storeroom or warehouse to provide shipping instructions. In other cases a copy of the order serves this purpose. If a copy of the invoice is sent to the storeroom or warehouse, the original invoice should not be mailed to the customer until the shipping room has reported that the goods have been shipped.

Ch. 13] 299 

BUSINESS PAPERS 

The sales invoice of a seller becomes the purchase invoice of the buyer. The business paper shown in Illustration No. 39, page 

296, is therefore both a sales invoice and a purchase invoice. The same paper serves as the basis of an entry in the sales journal of the seller and in the purchases journal of the buyer. 

The merchandise sold by a manufacturing, a wholesale, or a mail-order business may be delivered by parcel post, express, or freight. When merchandise is shipped by express, the shipper receives an express receipt; when it is shipped by freight, the shipper retains a copy of the bill of lading that is prepared. 

The Bill of Lading. A bill of lading contains the contractual provisions affecting the goods accepted for carriage by the transportation company, and states the duties of the shipper and the transportation company.

The Interstate Commerce Commission has prescribed a standard form of bill of lading that must be used by all transportation companies doing interstate business. The shipper may provide his own form as long as it agrees in content with the standard form. There are two kinds of bills of lading: the straight

ILLUSTRATION No. 42, BILL OF LADING

[Ch. 13 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

bill of lading, which is not negotiable, and the order bill of lading, which is negotiable. The form of a straight bill of lading is shown in Illustration No. 42.

The bill of lading is made in triplicate. The original copy which is signed by the shipper and by the agent of the transportation company, is sent to the consignee as his authority to claim the goods from the transportation company. The second copy, called the shipping order, is signed by the shipper only; it is retained by the transportation company as evidence of the authority to make the shipment. The third copy, which is a duplicate of the first and is signed by the shipper and the agent of the transportation company is held by the shipper as a duplicate receipt. 

If the goods are lost or damaged while in transit, the original bill of lading is required as the basis of a claim for the loss or damage against the transportation company.

If the credit rating of the purchaser is doubtful, the seller may be unwilling to ship the merchandise on open account. In this case the shipper may draw a sight draft on the purchaser, attach this draft to a copy of an order bill of lading, and deposit the bill of lading and the draft with his local bank. The bank will forward the order bill of lading with the draft attached to a bank in the city of the purchaser. In the meantime the shipper will notify the purchaser where the bill of lading and the draft can be obtained. The purchaser will call at the bank to which the bill of lading was sent and, by paying the draft, will obtain the bill of lading. He can then obtain his merchandise from the transportation company. This method of shipping goods is known as bill of lading with sight draft attached. 

The Statement of Account. In a wholesale business the terms of sale are stated on each invoice. If the invoice is not paid on the due date, the customer is reminded of the fact that it is due and is requested to remit. If several invoices become due and are not paid, a statement showing the total amount due may be sent to the customer. The practice of sending such statements is not prevalent in the wholesale trade, except in the case of overdue amounts. Some firms, however, send statements to all their customers on the first of each month. This method is not only a reminder of the amount due but also an effective means of detecting errors in accounts.

Ch. 13] 301 

BUSINESS PAPERS 

On the sales ticket for a retail sale, the terms are not stated, for it is understood that the amount is to be paid on the first of the next month. It is customary to mail the customer a statement showing the amount due on the first of the month. This practice has become so prevalent in the retail trade that the customer usually awaits the arrival of a statement before paying the amount due. 

The statement sent to a customer is called a statement of account.

The form of a statement of account is shown in

A businessman places in the custody of a bank the cash funds with which he carries on his business. He pays his obligations by giving his creditors checks drawn on this bank, and his customers usually pay their obligations by giving him checks on their banks. 

Although, in most cases, the checks received from customers are drawn on banks different from the bank in which the businessman keeps his funds, the latter deposits in his bank all checks received from customers; that is, he gives the checks to his bank, and the bank adds the total of the amounts to the balance that he has on deposit and then collects from the banks on which the checks are drawn.

The forms commonly used in transactions completed with a bank are:

[Ch. 13 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES Name 

1. Signature card 

2. Deposit ticket 

3. Pass book 4. Checkbook 

5. Bank statement

Function provides the bank with the authentic signature of the depositor. furnishes the bank with the depositor's record of a deposit. provides the depositor with the bank's record of a deposit. furnishes the depositor with a paying medium. provides the depositor with a periodic statement showing the condition of his account.

The Signature Card. Before the first deposit is made, the bank requires the depositor to sign his name on a signature card. This card provides the bank with a means of identifying the depositor's signature when it appears on checks drawn on the bank.

The Deposit Ticket. When a deposit is made, the depositor lists the currency and other items of cash on a printed form provided by the bank. This form is known as a deposit ticket. On it the depositor enters his name, the date, the total amounts of bills and specie, and the amounts of the individual checks. Opposite the amount of each check, the depositor should enter the name or the address of the bank on which the check is drawn, or the numbers of the American Bankers' Association that are printed on the check. CENTS The Pass Book. At the time of the first deposit the bank gives the depositor a book known as a pass book, in which a record is made of each deposit. When the depositor presents his deposit and deposit ticket to the bank teller, he should also present this pass book. teller enters the amount of the deposit in the pass book. This entry serves as the depositor's record of the transaction. The teller retains the deposit ticket, and from this a record of the transaction is made in the books of the bank. In some instances a duplicate deposit ticket, signed by the teller, is used in place of the entry in the pass book.

The Checkbook. A person having money on deposit in a bank is permitted to draw orders upon the bank, directing the bank to pay to certain individuals or to the order of those individuals sums not in excess of the amount on deposit. These written orders are known as checks. Checks are usually issued from a bound book that provides stubs on which a record of the issued checks is kept. 

The stub should always be filled out before a check is written; otherwise it may be overlooked until after the check has been sent out and the data for the stub are no longer available. 

If one person handles the cash receipts and payments, each stub may show the balance on hand, the amount of a deposit, the

[Ch. 13 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

amount of the check, and the new balance. This record prevents overdrawing the bank account and is of value when the accuracy of the records with the bank is being proved. Of course, if different persons record cash receipts and payments, or if some one other than the bookkeeper writes the checks, such a record cannot be kept conveniently on the stubs. In such a case the stubs are used to record only the data relative to the various checks. Illustra tion No. 46 shows one page of a checkbook containing two checks with the stubs properly filled out.

The Bank Statement. Most banks give monthly statements to their depositors. The bank statement shows the amount on deposit at the beginning of the month, the amounts deposited during the month, the amounts withdrawn during the month, and the balance on hand at the end of the month. This statement is accompanied by the depositor's checks that have been paid by the bank. These checks are stamped, or canceled, to show that they have been paid and are therefore known as canceled checks. 

A typical form of the bank statement is shown in Illustration 

No. 47 on page 305.

The balance on the bank statement will usually not agree with the balance shown by the depositor's record on the check stubs. The reason for this discrepancy is that some checks which the depositor has issued and has therefore subtracted from his

Ch. 13] 305 

BUSINESS PAPERS 

bank balance will not have been presented to the bank at the time the bank statement is prepared, and consequently will not have been subtracted from the depositor's balance in the records of the bank.

The depositor should reconcile the bank statement with his record in the following manner: (1) All deposits listed on the bank statement should be compared with the deposits shown on the check stubs. If there are any discrepancies that cannot be accounted for, the bank should be notified at once. (2) All canceled checks received from the bank should be compared with the check stubs, and on each stub that corresponds to a canceled check a heavy check mark should be made. The numbers and the amounts of all checks that have not been returned by the bank, which are indicated by the absence of check marks on some of the stubs, should be listed. The total of these

[Ch. 13 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

amounts is the amount of the checks outstanding and as yet unpaid by the bank. When this amount has been subtracted from the balance on the bank statement, the difference should equal the balance shown by the depositor's record on the check stubs. 

L. M. Sanderson, whose bank statement is shown in Illustration No. 47, finds that he has the following checks outstanding at the end of July:

No. 278. 

No. 281. .$20.00 $34.00 $71.50

His balance, as shown by his check stubs, is $942.67. The reconciliation of his bank statement is made in the following manner: 

BANK STATEMENT RECONCILIATION 

Balance, July 31, as shown by the bank statement.. 

Less outstanding checks:

No. 278. 

No. 281 

Total outstanding checks.. 

Balance as shown by the checkbook.... $1,068 17 .$20.00 34.00 71.50 125 50 942 67 There may, of course, be reasons other than outstanding checks for discrepancies between the balance on the bank statement and the amount shown by the depositor's record. The bank may charge for services rendered to the depositor or for taxes imposed by the Government. The amount of charges deducted will be listed on the bank statement but will not be shown on the depositor's record. These amounts must be subtracted from the depositor's balance when the reconciliation is made. 

The bank or the depositor may have made errors in addition or subtraction or may have omitted an item. If the procedure outlined previously is followed, however, these errors will be found. 

The depositor must then notify the bank of any error it has made; or, if the error is his, he must make the necessary correction in his own records.

Special Bank Accounts. In large businesses it is customary to transfer from the regular bank account sums to be used for special purposes. The amount transferred is equal to the checks drawn

Ch. 13] 307 

BUSINESS PAPERS 

for the special purpose, so that, when all the checks are cleared, the account will have no balance. This procedure relieves the regular checking account of a large volume of detail. For example, a special bank account may be sent up for each pay roll. If a business has one thousand employees, the checks to employees are identified with this special account in the bank. A single check is drawn against the regular account for the total of the pay roll; one thousand checks would be drawn to employees on the special pay-roll account.

In addition to the principal advantage mentioned above, there are other benefits arising from the use of a special bank account for each pay roll. Checks on the pay-roll account can be drawn without accompanying vouchers; they are more simple. in form. A subordinate officer may be designated to sign pay-roll checks. The reconciliation of the regular bank account is greatly simplified and the recording time for pay roll is shortened. 

Another example of a special bank account is for the purpose of paying dividends to stockholders or interest to bondholders. 

These special bank accounts, like the special pay-roll account, relieve the regular bank account of a great volume of detail. 

Whenever a large number of similar payments are to be made on one date, it is helpful to set up a special bank account for the purpose. 

Negotiable Vouchers

Checks and notes, and similar unconditional orders or promises to pay, are known as negotiable instruments. To encourage the use of these instruments, special laws applying to them have been established. 

In order to constitute a negotiable instrument, a form must have the following characteristics: 

1. The instrument must be in writing and signed by the maker or the drawer. 

2. It must contain an unconditional promise or order to pay a certain sum of money. 

3. It must be payable on demand or at a time that is either fixed or determinable. 

4. It must be payable to bearer or to order. 

Any form that fulfills these requisites is a negotiable instrument. No particular words and no particular order or construction of these words are necessary so long as the requisites are complied

[Ch. 13 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

with. There are, however, more or less conventional forms in which the various negotiable instruments are prepared. For the sake of both brevity and clearness, these forms should be used. 

The most important negotiable instruments, in addition to the note and the check, are: 1. Draft Name 

2. Cashier's check 

3. Express money order 

4. Postal money order

Function provides means whereby one party requests another to make payment. provides payment through a bank from its own funds. provides payment through an express company. provides payment through a post office.

The Draft. A draft is a written order by means of which one party directs another to pay a specified sum of money to the order of the first party or to the order of a third party. A bank check is a draft, since it is a written order drawn by a party who has money on deposit in a bank, directing the bank to pay a certain sum of money to a third party. Drafts other than checks may be classified as follows: Name 

1. Bank drafts 

2. Commercial drafts 

3. Trade acceptances

Function provide payment from interbank deposits. request debtors to pay obligations. provide for the payment of invoices for purchases of merchandise. A bank draft is really a check drawn by one bank on another and is evidence that the bank drawing the draft has money on deposit in the bank on which the draft is drawn. Individuals and businesses frequently purchase bank drafts for use in making remittances when, for any reason, it is not desirable or advisable to send checks. The form of a bank draft is shown at the top of page 309.

CASHIER ILLUSTRATIOn No. 48, Bank Draft 

Another type of draft, the commercial draft, is shown in Illustration No. 49. This draft is used largely as a collection device. 

The draft shown in Illustration No. 49 was drawn by King & Wilson, who wished to collect $326.39 from C. R. Ankromm & Son. 

King & Wilson gave the draft to their bank for collection. Their bank would forward the draft to its correspondent bank in the debtor's city. The local bank of the debtor then presented the draft for payment. When C. R. Ankromm & Son made payment, their bank forwarded the proceeds to King & Wilson. $32632

ILLUSTRATION No. 49, COMMERCIAL DRAFT 

Instead of drawing the draft in favor of themselves, King & Wilson might have made it payable to their bank in Topeka. The procedure of collecting the draft would have been the same as that outlined above.

King & Wilson would not make an entry in their accounting records until they had received payment or they had been notified by their bank that the draft had been collected and the proceeds

[Ch. 13 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

had been placed to their credit. Then they made an entry debiting 

Cash and crediting C. R. Ankromm & Son. If a fee was charged by the bank for making collection, an expense account, Collection Expense, was debited for the fee; Cash was debited for the difference between the face of the draft and the collection fee; and C. R. 

Ankromm & Son was credited for the face of the draft. 

Commercial drafts are classified according to time of payment as sight drafts and time drafts. Illustration No. 49 shows a sight draft, that is, a draft payable at sight or as soon as it is presented for payment. A time draft is one that is payable a certain number of days "after sight" or "after date." "After sight" means after the date of acceptance, and "after date" means after the date of the draft itself. If the person directed to pay the draft is willing to make payment, he indicates this fact by writing across the face of the draft the word "Accepted" and the date of acceptance, and by signing his name. An accepted draft is called an acceptance. 

When accepted, a time draft becomes the written promise of the one accepting it to pay the amount stipulated at the specified time. It is in the nature of a note and is so treated in the accounting records. $37,500

ILLUSTRATION No. 50, ACCEPTED DRAFT 

When an accepted draft is received, the notes receivable account is debited and the account of the debtor who accepted the draft is credited. If a business accepts a time draft drawn on it by a creditor, the account of the creditor is debited and the notes payable account is credited. For example, when Mosley & Logan received the accepted time draft shown in Illustration No. 50, they debited Notes Receivable and credited the account of the

Ch. 13] 311 

BUSINESS PAPERS 

de debtor, M. B. Hall & Co. M. B. Hall & Co. debited the account of their creditor, Mosley & Logan, and credited Notes Payable. 

There are three parties to a draft: the one who makes the request or order, called the drawer; the one who is directed to make the payment, called the drawee; and the one to whom the amount is to be paid, called the payee. Mosley & Logan were the drawer of the draft shown in Illustration No. 50; the First National Bank was the payee; and M. B. Hall & Co. was the drawee. Since 

M. B. Hall & Co. accepted the draft, it became the acceptor. 

The Trade Acceptance. A trade acceptance is a negotiable instrument that shows on its face that it has arisen out of a purchase of goods by the acceptor. An ordinary commercial draft may result from any transaction in which the acceptor became indebted to the drawer. Since a trade acceptance results from a purchase of goods by the acceptor, it ordinarily represents a current obligation that can be paid when the goods purchased have been sold. A trade acceptance is therefore considered to be more desirable than a commercial time draft. 

The use of the trade acceptance may be explained by an illustration. Long & Humphrey, of Jackson, Michigan, sell $547.08 worth of merchandise to The Hall Hardware Co., of 

Lansing, Michigan, on terms of a thirty-day trade acceptance. No. 4........

ILLUSTRATION No. 51, TRADE ACCEPTANCE 

Long & Humphrey draw a trade acceptance, using the prescribed form, which is shown in Illustration No. 51. The Hall Hardware 

Co. accepts this instrument and returns it to Long & Humphrey. 

The latter may keep it for thirty days and then present it for

[Ch. 13 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

payment, or, if they desire, they may discount it at their local bank. In the latter case the bank will give Long & Humphrey credit for the proceeds of the acceptance at the time of discount, and upon the maturity of the trade acceptance will send it to some Lansing bank, which will collect it from The Hall Hardware Co. 

The Cashier's Check. A cashier's check is a check drawn on a bank and signed by the cashier. It is used chiefly by a bank in the payment of collections made for persons who are not customers, or in the payment of obligations of the bank. The ordinary form of a cashier's check is shown in Illustration No. 52.

ILLUSTRATION No. 52, CASHIER'S CHECK CASHIER The Express Money Order. An express money order is a check issued by an express company. It is used as a means of transferring money from one city to another. The one desiring to transfer an amount of money deposits the money with the express company and receives an express money order. The money order is then sent to the person to whom payment is to be made. The latter presents the money order to the local office of the express company and receives his money.

The Postal Money Order. Postal money orders are issued by the United States Postal Department and are used for the same purpose as express money orders. The postmaster issues a postal money order on the sender's written application. This order can be cashed at any post office upon the proper identification of the holder.

Indorsement of Nego- It is frequently necessary to transfer a tiable Instruments negotiable instrument from one party to another. The legal transfer of a negotiable instrument is effected

Ch. 13] 313 

BUSINESS PAPERS 

by (1) indorsement by the payee or the holder and (2) delivery to the party to whom the instrument is to be transferred. In addition to effecting the transfer of the negotiable instrument, the indorsement serves as a guaranty that the instrument is valid and genuine and that the indorser has a legal title to it. In case the payer fails to pay the instrument on the date of maturity, the holder can usually hold the indorser responsible for payment, provided the holder complies with the legal requirements in reference to presentation and to giving notice to the indorser.

ILLUSTRATION No. 53, POSITION OF AN INDORSEMENT 

The indorsement is customarily written or stamped on the back of the negotiable instrument. The first indorsement is, through custom, placed near the left end of the instrument. The position of an indorsement is shown in Illustration No. 53. 

James Smith 

1. Indorsement in Blank. An indorsement in blank is effected by writing the name of the payee or the holder across the back without any qualifying or limiting words. It has the same effect as making the paper payable to bearer.

The instrument may be transferred by any subsequent holder without further indorsement, but the indorsement of each subsequent transferor is usually required so that each can be identified at any time.

[Ch. 13 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

Pay to the order of 

C. W. King, James Smith 

Pay without recourse,

L. C. Marshall 

Pay to the order of

The Hyde Park 

State Bank, for collection,

L. C. Marshall 

2. Indorsement in Full. This indorsement is effected by writing "Pay to the order of" above the name of the person or the firm to which the instrument is to be transferred and by signing the name of the payee or the holder who is transferring the instrument. The person or the firm to which the instrument is transferred must indorse it before any suc ceeding holder can obtain title. Instru ments that are indorsed before they are sent through the mail should be indorsed in full. An "indorsement in full" is also known as a "special indorsement." 

3. Qualified Indorsement. The effect of a qualified indorsement is to pass the title without rendering the indorser liable if the maker or the drawer arbitrarily refuses or negligently fails to pay the instrument or cannot do so because of insolvency. It is accomplished by writing "without recourse" above the signature of the indorser. 

4. Restrictive Indorsement. The effect of a restrictive indorsement is to constitute the indorsee an agent of the indorser. 

For example, a note left at a bank for collection may have "for collection" written above the signature of the indorser. This indorsement prevents the instrument from being used as the property of the bank.

When a partial payment of a negotiable instrument is made, a receipt for the payment should be written by the holder on the back of the instrument. The date and the amount are all that is necessary. The name of the holder should not be written, for it might be interpreted as an indorsement. 

A negotiable instrument should be signed by the party or parties responsible for its payment or by an authorized agent. 

Any partner in a trading partnership may sign a negotiable instrument and thus bind the firm by his action if the instrument is issued in the regular conduct of the business. In a corporation such instruments are signed by some officer or employee author-

Ch. 13] 

315 

BUSINESS PAPERS 

EN ized by the by-laws to do so. When issued by a partnership or a corporation, negotiable instruments are signed with the name of the business and in addition the name of the officer who is authorized to sign the name of the business. 

Miscellaneous Vouchers The vouchers used in transactions involving purchases, sales, cash receipts, and cash payments are those most frequently used in the average business. 

There are, however, a few vouchers that, although used occasionally, are to be found among the records of most businesses. If a business owns real property, it should have a deed from the previous owner transferring the title to the property. Property, real and personal, is ordinarily insured against fire and other hazards. 

Insurance policies describe the obligations of the insuring companies. Other business papers include service contracts with officers, warehouse receipts, state and city licenses, and lifeinsurance policies.

Recent social security legislation has produced numerous business papers having to do with relations between employer and employee. The federal and the state governments as administrative agencies provide forms to be filled out and require other records to be maintained by employer and employee. This legislation stresses the emphasis to be placed on the pay roll of the business and requires a more extended accounting for pay roll than has heretofore been needed. 

In a similar way legislation has prescribed papers and forms for corporate enterprise, involving data relating to bonds and stocks and to the relations between the corporation and its stockholders and bondholders. Income-tax laws, both state and federal, set up additional papers for the businessman to fill out. 

Some firms use what are known as journal vouchers. After being approved by the chief accountant, a journal voucher is the authority for an entry to be made in the general journal. Although entries in the general journal are not so numerous as those recorded in other books of original entry, they are usually very important. 

These entries include all unusual and infrequent entries, as well as the adjusting and closing entries. Because such entries are both unusual and important, it is necessary that a very complete explanation be made for each of them. The journal voucher provides for this explanation, as well as for all other necessary data.

[Ch. 13 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

The only explanation given in the general journal is a reference to the number of the journal voucher that contains a complete explanation. If there is a supporting document or a memorandum for the entry, it may be attached to the journal voucher. 

These vouchers are filed numerically. The form of a journal voucher is shown in Illustration No. 54.

L. E. ERWIN LOUISVILLE, KY.

ILLUSTRATION No. 54, JOURNAL VOUCHER Business Papers and Business Adminis tration

Business administration consists largely in co-ordinating the activities of the individuals within a business enterprise in such a way that the purposes of the enterprise may be accomplished. To each of these individuals are delegated various responsibilities corresponding to the capacity and ability of each person. The performance of each can be measured only if some record of measurement is available. The business papers prepared or signed by each person provide this record. 

A business enterprise can operate only through the services of the individuals who comprise its organization. The operations of these individuals ultimately result in the summary shown by the statement of profit and loss. Business papers, evidencing the acts of these members of the business organization, are summarized in the periodic statement of profit and loss. 

A business paper may evidence the understanding existing between two individuals within the enterprise or between one individual within the enterprise and another on the outside. The

Ch. 13] 317 

BUSINESS PAPERS 

purchase requisition illustrates the former; the purchase invoice, the latter. Business papers may represent transactions that are to be recorded in the books, or they may simply facilitate the completion of transactions that will later be reduced to accounting entries. The purchase invoice results in an accounting entry; the purchase order represents a preliminary stage and is not journalized. Business papers may be prepared within the business for internal use or for issuance to other businesses, or they may be received from other firms.

PRACTICE SET No. I Preliminary Instructions. The transactions in this practice set were completed by Carl Clarke, the proprietor of a wholesale shoe business. Although these transactions deal with the operations of a wholesale shoe business, they are intended to illustrate general principles of accounting rather than the technique of the accounting system of a particular business.

Since controlling accounts with accounts receivable and accounts payable are maintained in the general ledger, the individual accounts of customers and creditors are kept in separate subsidiary ledgers. 

The general ledger accounts to be used are: Cash

The books of original entry consist of a general journal with two amount columns; a purchases journal like that on page 196; a sales journal having the same columns as that on page 194; and a cashbook with columns like those in the cash receipts journal on page 199 and the cash payments journal on page 203. 

At the end of the month the posting is to be proved by a trial balance. Schedules of the subsidiary ledgers are to be prepared in order to compare, in each case, the balance of the controlling account with the total of the balances in the subsidiary ledger. In addition, a ten-column work sheet, a balance sheet, and a statement of profit and loss are to be prepared; the accounts are to be adjusted and closed; and a post-closing trial balance is to be taken. Reversing entries must then be prepared and posted.

In actual practice, cash would be proved and the personal accounts would be posted on each day. Because of the comparatively small number of transactions in this set, however, this work will be completed only at the end of each week and each month.

Opening Entry. Under the date of April 1, 19--, record in the general journal the assets, the liability, and the proprietorship of Carl Clarke, as shown by the balance sheet of March 31, 19--. 

Record the cash balance also in the cashbook. Check the entries for

[Ch. 13 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

the cash balance in both the general journal and the cashbook so that double posting will be avoided.

Open the accounts in the general ledger. Arrange the accounts and allow space for them in accordance with the instructions given on the inside front cover of the ledger, which is contained in the set of bound books of account. If you are using loose sheets of paper, however, in place of these bound books, allow one-half page for each account except Profit and Loss Summary, which will require one and a half pages. After the accounts have been opened, post from the general journal.

Open accounts for customers and creditors in accordance with the instructions given on the inside front cover of the ledger in the set of bound books of account. If you are using loose sheets of paper, allow one quarter of a page for each of the accounts receivable and the accounts payable. Record in the proper accounts the balances shown by the schedules of accounts receivable and accounts payable.

CHAPTER XIV RECORDING ROUTINE 

The Function of Records Business records must be so constructed that at any time in the future it will be possible to trace a transaction from the record of its occurrence to its effect on some item in one of the two principal statements. Or, reversing the procedure, it should be possible to account for any amount on either statement by tracing backward to the first record of the transactions affecting this amount. This may be illustrated graphically as follows: 

Trace forward 

Transaction-Paper-Journal-Ledger-Work Sheet Statement 

Trace backward

If a sale of $250 is made on account, it should be possible to trace that sale until it appears on the statement of profit and loss as part of the total sales. Or, if the statement of profit and loss shows rent expense of $1,500, it should be possible to trace back from the statement, through the work sheet, ledger, journal, and business papers to rent transactions totalling $1,500. 

The minimum of commonly used records was explained in an early chapter where the accounting system consisted of a journal, a ledger, a trial balance, and the financial statements. This threefold sequence of journal, ledger, and statements was at the time stated to be adequate for any business not interested in efficiency and economy in recording. If efficiency and economy are disregarded, this simple routine of recording is sufficiently flexible to take care of any transaction, no matter how complex it may be. 

The previous chapter introduced another step in the routine of recording: the business paper evidencing the transaction. Business papers bring the accounting department into touch with the transactions, no matter where the latter may occur or who may be responsible for their occurrence. Business papers add a first step to standard routine so that it now commonly consists of four steps: (1) the business papers evidencing the transactions; (2) 335

[Ch. 14 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

the journal record of the transactions; (3) the ledger record, each transaction affecting two or more accounts; (4) the statements, summarizing the effect of the transactions performed during the accounting period.

Standard routine is constantly being modified in the interest of economy and efficiency. Special journals result in less flexible records, but save much labor in recording and in posting. Subsidiary ledgers further complicate the posting from journals and the preparation of statements with supporting schedules, but provide useful means of checking the accuracy of the recording routine. Special columns in books of original entry make these books more complex, but conform to the requirements of transactions that occur frequently. There have already been presented repeated evidences of specialization in recording that make possible the more efficient accomplishment of the purposes of accounting. 

If economy in analyzing and in summarizing could be disregarded, the single journal and the single ledger would probably represent the simplest and the safest kinds of accounting records. 

In order that summaries may be provided economically and that analyses may be adapted to particular needs, accounting records cease to be simple and become increasingly complicated. The purpose of this chapter is to discuss the modifications in accounting procedure made possible through the use of business papers, which result in a saving of labor in maintaining the accounting records. 

Summarizing in the The principal function of the ledger is to 

Business Records summarize the effect on the accounts of all the transactions of an accounting period. This summarization eliminates the inefficiency of preparing new statements after each transaction. The effect of transactions is summarized in the ledger, and statements are prepared only occasionally. In the standard routine, summarizing is a function of the ledger. 

The journal, which was presented first as a perfect chronological record of transactions, has been modified to permit summarizing purchases in the purchases journal, summarizing sales in the sales journal, and summarizing cash receipts and cash disbursements in the two cash journals or the cashbook. When special journals are used, transactions are summarized in these journals, and the ledger accounts affected by the entries in the special journals show only the totals for the period. The complete record of transactions affecting sales is no longer to be found in

Ch. 14] 337 

RECORDING ROUTINE 

the sales account, but in the sales journal. There is a saving of labor in avoiding the duplication of entries in the journal and in the ledger.

These conclusions are shown in the illustrations below: 

WITHOUT A SPECIAL JOURNAL GENERAL JOURNAL 

(Individual transactions of all kinds are entered as they occur in this record; postings are made individually to accounts in the ledger. This journal is a 

E perfect chronological record.)

LEDGER SALES 

Oct. 1 200 100 300 200 2 350 400 150 122233 1 31 400 16,450 (Individual postings to this account from the general journal make it the summarizing device for Sales; thus, the total of sales is obtained in the sales account.)

WITH A SPECIAL JOURNAL SPECIAL JOURNAL SALES JOURNAL 

Oct. 1122233 2/00 100 300 200 350 400 150 

LEDGER SALES 

Oct. 31 16,450 31 

Sales Cr. 400 16,450 (The similarity between the special sales journal and the sales account above is apparent. The journal record takes over the summarizing formerly found in the account record. With a number of special journals there is no longer a perfect chronological record.) (The detail of sales for October has been removed from the ledger account. 

A single posting saves posting labor, but omits the complete detailed record of sales in the sales account.)

[Ch. 14 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

The accounting periods summarized in the special journals need not correspond to the periods summarized in the ledger or covered by the statements. For instance, the cash receipts and cash payments journals, or the cashbook, could be closed at the end of each week; the sales and the purchases journals could be closed twice a month; the trial balance should be taken once a month; and the balance sheet and the statement of profit and loss could be prepared only annually. The accounts affected by many transactions could be summarized often; those affected by a small number of transactions, less often.

When business papers become an integral part of the accounting system, they can sometimes be used as summarizing devices. When they are so used, a journal may contain a record of only the total for the day. The amounts of cash sales for a day may be listed on an adding machine from the sales tickets; the tickets may be filed as supporting records; and only one entry need be made in the cash receipts journal. In this case summarizing has been moved forward another step, to the business papers; and individual transactions no longer appear in the journal. 

Detail and the Routine The maximum of detail regarding each of Recording transaction probably appears on the business paper first evidencing the transaction. If it is possible to make carbon copies of this detail and to file these carbon copies for ready reference, much saving in time and labor can be effected in journalizing and posting. For example, keeping a carbon copy of a sales ticket or of a sales invoice avoids the necessity of recording much detail in the sales journal. In some recording routines the carbon copies of sales invoices, properly bound, serve as the sales journal. An adding machine tape showing the total for the month serves as the basis for posting to the controlling account and the sales account at the end of the month. Furthermore, each invoice is a medium for posting to the customers' ledger. 

Business Papers and

Registers 

Control over business transactions may be aided by issuing pads of business papers, serially numbered, to those responsible for the transactions and requiring a record of all the business papers used. For example, checks are often numbered serially in a checkbook. Accompanying the pad of checks there is a book containing a line for the record of each check, the number of the check having been entered on

Ch. 14] 339 

RECORDING ROUTINE 

the line. This book contains columns for the essential information appearing on each check. Such a book is called a register. Since copies of the business papers are filed and are available for reference, the information to be written in the register is reduced to a minimum.

The register may or may not serve as a posting medium. 

When it does, it takes the place of some special journal. The check register, mentioned above, would displace the cash payments record. A sales register, containing a record of sales invoices, would take the place of the sales journal. If a business paper is spoiled, the original, as well as the duplicate, is filed, and a notation of the nonuse of the paper is made on the proper line in the ! register.

Modern accounting practice seems to indicate an increasing 5 displacement of special journals by registers. This tendency to supplant journals by special journals, and special journals. by registers, increases the efficiency of recording but at the same time decreases flexibility. No other set of accounting records is so flexible as the set consisting of one journal and one ledger. Any and all transactions can be recorded in such a set. At the same time no other set of accounting records is so uneconomical or inefficient. 

The planning of recording routine follows a constant trend toward more efficient and less flexible records. Economy, here as elsewhere, is obtained at the sacrifice of versatility. 

Among the registers that might be used in recording transactions of the types presented thus far are the following: 

Name 

1. Sales register 

2. Check register 

3. Journal voucher register 

4. Credit memorandum register 

Forms accounted for 

Sales invoices 

Checks drawn 

Journal vouchers 

Credit memoranda

In the following chapter the voucher register will be described. 

A sales register, which takes the place of a sales journal and also registers serially numbered sales invoices, is shown on page 340. A separate line is reserved for each numbered sales invoice, and on that line is entered the information that is considered most useful in a book record. All other details of each sale are available in the file containing the copies of the sales invoices. 

In the register shown on page 340 provision is made for posting to the customer's accounts in the subsidiary ledger. If the

[Ch. 14 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

posting were made from the copies of the sales invoices, the Ledger Page column would be omitted. Inasmuch as customers' accounts are usually kept on loose-leaf sheets in a binder and are arranged alphabetically, it is impossible to indicate a ledger page in the folio column of the register. A check mark is used to show that the amount has been posted.

The register may apply to business papers prepared internally or received from without. The purchasing department may keep a register of purchase invoices received from others; the financial department may keep a register of checks drawn. The register may be a book of original entry, from which postings are made to the ledger, or it may be merely a supplementary record. A sales

ILLUSTRATION No. 55, SALES REGISTER 

register is commonly a posting medium; an insurance policy register, described later in this chapter, is usually not such a record. 

The register is likely to be more accurate if it is an integral part of the accounting system, for then errors that otherwise might pass unnoticed become evident.

It is perhaps not exactly accurate to call a register a book of original entry since the business paper is the original entry and the register is principally an accumulating device. The register frequently is a means of accumulating a total that is to be posted to the general ledger, whereas details are posted to the subsidiary ledger from the business papers themselves. In some cases the register serves as a sort of subsidiary ledger. This use of the voucher register is illustrated in Chapter XV. 

The use of serially numbered business papers and registers ties closely together the papers used and the corresponding entries. 

Since all papers are numbered and space is provided in the registers

Ch. 14] 341 

RECORDING ROUTINE 

for recording the transactions they represent, there is assurance that each paper will be accounted for. The omission of the record of any transaction becomes at once apparent. The numbering of business papers and the identification of certain papers with the individuals who use them establish a control over the acts of those individuals. Any error can thus be traced to the person responsible. 

In a large organization such control is essential; in a small enterprise it is doubtful whether so much formality and lack of flexibility are desirable.

Business Papers as The standard posting medium is the jour- 

Posting Media nal. The ordinary rule of accounting procedure requires that a record of all transactions arrive in the ledger by way of a journal. The journal record must intervene between the transaction, or the business paper evidencing the transaction, and the record of the transaction in the ledger. 

As mechanical devices the bound journal and the loose-leaf journal are not so satisfactory for posting as loose business papers. 

Since a business paper can be brought close to the account to which the entry is to be posted, posting is facilitated. When much posting is to be done, there is a mechanical advantage in avoiding the use of a bound or loose-leaf journal. 

Business papers are being used constantly to a greater extent in posting to subsidiary ledgers, which receive the greatest volume of posting. As posting media to a creditors' ledger, checks or carbon copies of them are better than a cash payments journal or the cash payments side of the cash book. Postings can be made more rapidly to a customers' ledger from copies of sales tickets or of sales invoices than from a sales journal. 

The use of business papers as posting media also facilitates the proof of posting. When many transactions occur daily, posting should be proved daily. For example, as debit postings are made to customers' accounts from copies of tickets or invoices, an index slip may be left in the ledger wherever a posting has been made. 

After the postings for the day have been completed, an adding machine total of the postings can be made by listing the items posted on that day, which are indicated by the slips in the ledger. 

This total should agree with the total obtained by listing the amounts of the tickets or invoices posted. By proving the postings of each day, the bookkeeper need not check previous postings if an error occurs in the subsidiary ledger.

[Ch. 14 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

If the ledger is kept on loose-leaf sheets and a bookkeeping machine is used, it is possible to prepare a journal record of the day's transactions at the same time that the posting is done. For example, a loose sheet representing a sales register may be placed in the bookkeeping machine with the sheet representing a customer's account. At the time that an amount is posted to a customer's account, the record of the sale is also made in the sales register by means of carbon paper. Proof of the posting is furnished automatically by the machine, which accumulates the amounts. 

The business papers may contain space for posting information. This information can be stamped with a rubber stamp or written in by the posting clerk after the accuracy of all the postings has been proved.

Double Record of Cash The use of business papers to provide an independent and externally prepared record is perhaps best illustrated when relations with a bank are so conducted that the bank's record of cash is a complete check on the cash receipts and cash payments journals or the cashbook. Such a record is known as a double record of cash and is invariably advocated in the handling of this asset.

In order that the bank's record may correspond with the cash record, all cash received must be deposited in the bank. If that is done, it follows that all cash disbursed must be withdrawn from the bank by means of checks. The bank's record of deposits will correspond to the cash receipts record; its record of checks paid will correspond to the cash disbursements record; and the balance on its monthly statement should agree with the cash balance of the business. These balances will agree except when there have been delays in depositing or when there have been delays in clearing checks drawn. 

Petty Cash

When a business has established a policy of depositing all cash receipts and making all payments by checks, it will probably find that some small payments can be made more conveniently with cash. A fund for such payments is established, not by taking an amount directly from the cash receipts, but by drawing a check payable to a special fund, known as the petty cash fund. The check is usually drawn to the order of Petty Cash for the estimated amount of the disbursements to be made in currency during a certain period of time,

Ch. 14] 343 

RECORDING ROUTINE 

frequently one month. This check is recorded in the cash payments record by debiting Petty Cash and crediting Cash. The check is then converted into currency, and the currency is placed in a

Whenever the petty cash fund has been exhausted or is very nearly exhausted, and at the end of each fiscal period, a check is drawn for the amount of the disbursements to date. This check is recorded in the cash payments record. Petty Cash is not debited, however, for the amount of the check; the accounts that are indicated in the petty cash book, and that show the purposes for which payments were made, are debited. The check is then converted into currency, the currency is placed in the petty cash drawer, and the receipt of cash is recorded in the petty cash book. 

The amount of currency in the petty cash drawer, as well as the balance shown by the petty cash book, is then equal to the balance of the petty cash account.

As shown by the illustration on page 343, a check was issued on October 1 to establish the petty cash fund. This check was recorded in the cash payments record by a debit to Petty Cash. It was also entered in the Receipts column of the petty cash book. 

As payments were made from the petty cash fund, they were recorded in the Payments column of the petty cash book. They were then extended into the Miscellaneous Selling Expense, Miscellaneous General Expense, and Sundry Accounts columns, according to the reason for each payment. The explanation of each entry was written in the Explanation column. The title of the account to be debited for each entry in the Sundry Accounts column was written in the space at the left of the amount. 

Special columns other than Miscellaneous Selling Expense and Miscellaneous General Expense could have been introduced into the petty cash book if payments for other purposes were to

Ch. 14] 345 

RECORDING ROUTINE 

be made with sufficient frequency to justify the use of such special columns. 

On October 20 only $1.45 remained in the petty cash drawer.

The columns of the petty cash book were totaled and ruled, and the balance of $1.45 was brought down below the ruling. A check for $23.55, an amount equal to the total of the Payments columns, was issued to replenish the fund to its original amount. This check was recorded in the cash payments record. Miscellaneous Selling 

Expense, Miscellaneous General Expense, Store Supplies, Freight 

In, and Sales Salaries were debited. These accounts then contained the same debits as they would have if the payments had been recorded in the cash payments record as they were made. 

The amount of the check to renew the fund was then entered in the Receipts column of the petty cash book.

ILLUSTRATION No. 58, ENTRY IN CASH PAYMENTS RECORD TO REPLENISH THE PETTY CASH FUND 

If the petty cash book is not a posting medium, but only a supplementary book, provision is commonly made in the cash payments record for an analysis of the replenishing check. There is one exception to this general rule. When petty cash payments are made for a large number of items for which special columns are not available in the cash payments record, an analysis of the replenishing check in the cash payments record would require much space. In this case an entry debiting Petty Cash and crediting Cash is made in the cash payments record, and another entry debiting each item for which petty cash payments were made and crediting Cash is made in the general journal. In the cash payments record the debit to Petty Cash is checked, and in the general

[Ch. 14 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

journal the credit to Cash is checked. Cash is then credited through the cash payments record, and the various items for which payments were made are debited from the general journal. 

For example, the replenishing check for $23.55 that is shown in Illustration No. 58, page 345, might have been recorded in the cash payments journal and then analyzed by means of an entry in the general journal. Both entries would have been:

At the end of each accounting period, before the statements are prepared, it is necessary to draw a check to replenish the petty cash fund. Otherwise, the expenses represented by the disbursements would not appear in the statements for the period. 

The charges made to the expense accounts when the replenishing check is recorded bring these expense items into the ledger and keep the petty cash fund at the predetermined amount. The petty cash fund will remain at the same amount unless it is decided that the fund is too large or too small.

The insurance policy register and the fixed assets register accumulate the data needed in determining the monthly charges for insurance expense = and for depreciation.

A register for notes payable is similar to that for notes receivable. The columns in a notes payable register may be similar to those in the notes receivable register shown in Illustration No. 59. The supplementary record of notes payable provides the detailed information that could not be recorded conveniently in the general journal.

The Insurance Policy Register. When there is a possibility that property may be destroyed by fire or any other accidental cause, the owner may protect himself against such loss by entering into a contract with an insurance company. He agrees to pay to the company a certain amount in return for the protection afforded by the contract. This contract is known as an insurance policy, and the compensation paid to the insurance company is known as the insurance premium. The insurance policy is made for a definite period of time, and the premium is payable in advance.

A business firm frequently has several insurance policies, taken out at different times with different companies and covering different risks. A record of each of these policies should be kept in such a form that information pertinent to the insurance contracts can be obtained at any time. This is accomplished by keeping an insurance policy register similar to that in Illustration No. 60. 

In this record the cost of the insurance, or the premium, is divided into the monthly charges representing insurance expense. 

At the time that an insurance policy is obtained, the information shown on it is entered in the insurance policy register. The portion of the premium applicable to each of the remaining months of the current year is entered in the proper column. The amount not chargeable to the current year is entered in the last column. 

At the same time that the policy is obtained, the amount of the premium paid is debited to the prepaid insurance account. At the end of each fiscal period the amount of the insurance expired during the period is ascertained by obtaining the total of the column or columns showing the insurance expense for that period. The prepaid insurance account is then adjusted to show the unexpired insurance, and the amount of the expired premiums is recorded as an expense of the period.

At the close of the year the insurance policy register is ruled so as to close out the premiums that expired during the year. The unexpired premiums, which are shown in the last column, are

RECORDING ROUTINE

The Fixed Assets Register. When a business has a large number of fixed assets, a record is needed to show the cost of each asset, the estimated trade-in or scrap value, the estimated life, the annual rate of depreciation, the number of years during which the asset has been owned, the total amount of depreciation charged in previous years, and the monthly charges for depreciation during the current year. Maintaining such a record of fixed assets insures that the proper amount of depreciation will be calculated on each item, and also calls attention to errors in estimates, which might otherwise be overlooked.

Since this register is used to accumulate depreciation charges, a separate page should be set up for each class of fixed assets. For example, the illustration on page 350 contains only office equipment and provides for a monthly charge for depreciation on office equipment. Another page would contain only store equipment and would provide a monthly charge for depreciation on store equip-
