In some cases a card is set up for each fixed asset. If this is done, the card record amounts to a subsidiary ledger record of fixed assets. Such a record is more fully described in the chapter on accounting for manufacturing.

Business Papers and Reference to machines has been made in 

Bookkeeping Machines the previous discussion. Modern book keeping practice makes more and more use of machines to provide the requisite detail at a low cost. Because books do not fit into bookkeeping machines, an increasing amount of recording is done on loose sheets and on cards. At the same time that a machine records information on sheets or cards, it may be accumulating a variety of totals. 

It has been estimated that in modern big business enterprises 90 per cent of the bookkeeping records are products of mechanical equipment. The larger the enterprise, the greater the advantage in the use of machines. Machines may be highly specialized, and through their specialization great volumes of work may be accomplished at a relatively low cost. This low cost has made possible the recording of an amount of detail that would otherwise be prohibitively expensive.

Ch. 14] 351 

RECORDING ROUTINE 

The machines most widely used in mechanical bookkeeping practice can be classified under the following headings: 

1. Auxiliary machines, such as adding machines, calculating machines, and duplicating machines 

2. Modified listing-adding machines, such as the various posting machines 

3. Modified typewriting machines, such as the various bookkeeping and accounting machines 

4. Tabulating machines 

The Flow of Accounting The record of transactions may be con- Information sidered as a stream of information that flows from the business papers to the periodic accounting statements. This flow must be uninterrupted and rapid so that the accounting statements will be complete and timely. Any procedure or any mechanism that will insure the continuous and speedy flow of this information merits the attention of the bookkeeper or the accountant.

It is necessary not only that this information flow steadily towards the statements that are to be prepared, but also that meters be so stationed that the occurrences can be reviewed. In this chapter various procedures that assist and also control the flow of information have been described. The adaptability of business papers to machine use, together with the efficiency and the low cost of the use of machines, has resulted in removing much bookkeeping from the pen and books to papers and machines. 

The advantages that are to be found in the use of business papers and machines should be appreciated. Their disadvantages should also be recognized. Modifications in the standard procedure of recording are to be accepted only when the advantages of efficiency and economy of operation outweigh the disadvantages of inflexibility and formalized procedure.

Accounting Systems CHAPTER XV  

The application of accounting records to a given business practice constitutes a system. The records used in handling petty cash, which were discussed in Chapter XIV, are commonly called the imprest system of keeping petty cash. If cash sales are recorded in the cash book and only sales on account are recorded in the sales journal, this procedure is called the system of recording sales. The use of the term system in accounting implies the existence of a given business practice and the adaptation of the accounting records to that practice. 

The modification does not need to be entirely on the side of the accounting records. In some cases the business practice is adapted to the records. To effect control over cash and to provide a double record of that asset, the business practice of depositing in the bank all cash received and making all payments by check was developed. The result was a system, that is, a co-ordination of business practice with accounting records. Systems ordinarily result in more effective business control. 

The accounting papers, journals, accounts, and reports to be used by any business constitute the accounting system of that business. When accounting is considered in its application to any given enterprise or to any set of conditions in a going business, the result is an accounting system. The term suggests the selection of the elements comprising the system from the entire field of accounting practice and the arrangement of these elements into an organized whole.

One of the most complete accounting systems is that concerned with the payment of obligations incurred in the course of business activity. This system not only adjusts the accounting records to a practice of account payment, but also affects the accounting from the first record of the transaction. An accounting procedure used in recording the purchase of merchandise, services, and fixed assets, as well as the payment of the obligations resulting 356

Ch. 15] 357 

from these transactions, constitutes an organized group of accounting practices known as the voucher system. This chapter will be devoted to a complete discussion of the voucher system. This system should be considered as concrete evidence of a more general principle of organizing accounting practices. 

The Voucher System of In the operation of either a mercantile or 

Recording Purchases a manufacturing business, it is necessary to purchase the following: 

1. Goods or Materials. In a mercantile business goods are purchased and resold without modification. In a manufacturing business materials are purchased and used in the production of the commodity or the service that the business sells. 

2. Assets for Use in the Business. Both mercantile and manufacturing businesses purchase assets such as land, buildings, machinery, equipment, and furniture and fixtures with which to carry on their operations. 

3. Services. In order to operate, all businessès must purchase services such as labor, heat, light, and power. Such services, which are ordinarily termed expenses, may be used in the production of the commodity or the service that the business sells, or they may be used in selling the product after it has been purchased or produced. Usually the most important of these purchased services is that of labor.

The volume of operations performed by even the small business is such that the total of the expenditures is a sufficient amount to be of importance. In a large business such expenditures must, of necessity, amount to enormous sums. In both the small and the large business it is necessary that some effective control be exercised over these expenditures. In a small business this control is usually accomplished by having all purchases and all payments for these purchases made under the direct supervision of the chief executive of the business. In a large business such supervision is not practicable. The responsibility for incurring obligations and making expenditures must be delegated to subordinates, who are, at best, supervised only indirectly by the chief executive of the business. Since the personal control of the chief executive is no longer possible, it is necessary to devise some means by which a proper control can be effected.

[Ch. 15 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

The Voucher 

One method of obtaining a control over expenditures is to require for each expenditure a written authorization. This authorization is usually in the form of a voucher. The voucher is prepared from the invoice or other data that serve as evidence of the expenditure to be made. The front of the voucher provides space for an explanation of the expenditure and for the signatures of those approving the expenditure. The back of the voucher provides space in which to indicate the accounts that are to be charged for the expenditure. The front and the back of a voucher are shown in Illustrations Nos. 62 and 63.

When the voucher system is in use, every invoice or bill received must be vouchered. This procedure necessitates that the invoice or bill be approved as to: 

1. Quantities, prices, grades, sizes, and quality by the purchasing agent. 

2. Actual receipt by the receiving clerk. 

3. Mathematical accuracy of extensions by the checking clerk in the accounting department.

[Ch. 15 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

Vouchers are most frequently filed under the date of payment, for this method automatically brings to the attention of the disbursing officer the vouchers that are to be paid on each day and provides a convenient means by which those who are interested can determine the amount that must be paid at any time. This method of filing does not provide a very direct means of referring to an unpaid voucher. A voucher can, however, be located with reasonable effort if the due date is determined first from the book of original entry.

The second method of filing vouchers, that is, under the name of the vendor, is sometimes used. Under this method a more direct means of locating each voucher is afforded, and the amount owed to each creditor can be determined more easily by referring to the vouchers filed under the name of the creditor. When the vouchers are filed alphabetically, a supplementary record, known as a tickler, is ordinarily maintained for cross reference. The number of each voucher is entered in this tickler under the date when the voucher is to be paid. On each day the disbursing officer refers to the tickler to determine the vouchers that are to be paid on that date, and then removes them from the alphabetical file. 

Alphabetical filing, however, obviously complicates the procedure at the time of payment.

The advantages of time filing and alphabetical filing can be obtained, and at the same time the use of the cross-reference tickler can be avoided, if a carbon copy of each voucher is made. 

The original copy of the voucher may then be filed under the due date, and the carbon copy under the name of the creditor. The carbon copies may remain permanently in the creditors' file and thus provide a continuous record of the purchases made from each creditor. 

When reference to vouchers is made frequently by voucher numbers, the vouchers may be filed numerically, and a file containing cards on which the vouchers are listed according to their due dates may be maintained. This method is convenient in that it provides an easy means of referring to vouchers. It does require, however, the use of the extra card record. The use of that record may be avoided if a carbon copy of each voucher is prepared. The original copy may then be filed according to the due date; and the carbon copy, numerically.

When the due date arrives, the voucher is taken from the file and a check is issued in payment. If this check shows the

Ch. 15] 361 

invoice or invoices for which it is issued in payment, it is known as a voucher check. After the voucher has been paid, it is filed in the paid vouchers file.

The Voucher Register It has been explained in the preceding paragraphs that, when the voucher system of authorizing payments is used, a voucher is prepared for each expenditure, whether the expenditure is for goods or materials, assets for use in the business, or services. In fact, under the voucher system, a check is never issued except in payment of a properly authorized voucher.

When this method of controlling expenditures is employed, it is more convenient to record all purchases in one record rather than in the purchases journal and the general journal. This may be accomplished by employing what is known as a voucher register. 

The voucher register contains a record of all vouchers issued. As soon as a voucher has been prepared and approved, it is entered in the voucher register. When it is paid, the date of payment and the number of the check issued in payment are entered in the appropriate columns of the register. The total of the unpaid vouchers as shown by the voucher register must at all times agree with the total of the vouchers in the unpaid vouchers file. 

The form of the voucher register may vary widely, depending largely on the size of the business and the number of accounts maintained with purchases and expenses. It is usually ruled to provide columns for the date of entry, the voucher number, the name of the creditor, the explanation, the terms, the date of payment, and the number of the check issued in payment. In addition, it contains one amount column for credits to the vouchers payable account and as many columns for debits as are necessary to show the distribution and classification of all purchases and expenses. A space containing columns for the amount, the name of the account, and the ledger page is provided at the extreme right for sundry debits. Every amount entered in this record appears as a credit to the vouchers payable account and as a debit to the account or accounts charged for the commodities or the services represented by the invoice or the bill. 

The items in the voucher register are not posted until the end of the month. At that time all the columns are totaled, and the total of each column is posted to the account stated at the head

Unless these totals agree, an error has been made either in entering amounts in the columns or in obtaining the various totals. This error must be corrected before the posting is begun.

payments journal becomes a check register, that is, a record that accounts for each check issued. 

For each item for which payment is to be made, a voucher must be prepared. When merchandise or supplies are purchased on credit or for cash, when salaries are to be paid, or when any bill for an expense is received, a voucher is first prepared in the usual manner. This voucher is then entered in the voucher register, and the amount is credited to Vouchers Payable. At the time payment is made, an entry is recorded in the cash payments journal, and the amount of the voucher is debited to Vouchers Payable. 

Relation of the Voucher When the voucher system is employed, the System to the Accounts accounts payable ledger is seldom used. 

Payable Ledger

The vouchers in the unpaid vouchers file serve as a record of the amounts owed individual creditors; and since they are filed under the due dates, they automatically provide for payments to creditors. The total amount of the vouchers in the unpaid vouchers file must agree at all times with the total of the unpaid vouchers recorded in the register, and the latter total must agree with the balance of the vouchers payable account in the general ledger. 

The Voucher System and Petty Cash Dis bursements 

Record of Cash."

The voucher system is an extension of the control of cash disbursements discussed in 

Chapter XIV under the title "Double 

Under the voucher system each disbursement

Ch. 15] 365 

not only must be represented by a check (which later appears on the depositor's bank statement) but also must be authorized in - advance by a voucher. The voucher thus serves as authorization for the check that is to be written. It would be uneconomical and inadvisable to prepare a voucher for each disbursement to be made from petty cash. Petty cash disbursements are therefore given special treatment under the voucher system.

When a petty cash fund is to be established, a voucher authorizes the drawing of a check payable to Petty Cash. The account Vouchers Payable is credited in the voucher register, and the account Petty Cash is debited. The check is then drawn; an entry debiting Vouchers Payable and crediting Cash or the bank account is made in the cash payments journal; the check is cashed; and the money is turned over to the person in charge of the fund. 

Whenever a disbursement is made from the fund, a receipt is obtained from the one to whom payment is made. The entries required in establishing a petty cash fund are shown in Illustrations Nos. 64 and 65 (Voucher No. 3), pages 362, 363, and 364.

When the fund is to be replenished, a voucher authorizes a check to be drawn for the total amount of the disbursements that have been made. The various accounts to be debited are determined from the receipts showing petty cash disbursements, and are indicated on the voucher. The entry to record the voucher in the register consists of a credit to Vouchers Payable and debits to the accounts indicated on the voucher. The check is recorded in the cash payments journal by debiting Vouchers Payable and crediting Cash or the bank account. The money obtained by cashing the check is placed in the petty cash fund, which should then equal the original amount. The entries required in replenishing a petty cash fund are shown in Illustrations Nos. 64 and 65 (Voucher No. 65), pages 362, 363, and 364. 

Adjustments in

Authorized Dis bursements

In the preceding paragraphs the usual routine involved in the use of the voucher system was explained. Some difficulties arise, however, when special adjustments are necessary because of changes in either the amounts due or the conditions of payment, or when, for other reasons, the usual routine must be varied. 

An adjustment must be recorded in such a way that the accounts will show correctly the effect of the transaction and the voucher

[Ch. 15 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

register will show the proper amount payable. The procedures to be followed in handling purchases returns and allowances, partial payments, corrections, and notes payable illustrate the methods of adjustment that may be used under the voucher system. 

Purchases Returns and Allowances. When credit is received for a return or an allowance on a purchase, the amount of the unpaid voucher is thereby reduced. In order that the voucher register will show the correct amount payable, an entry may be interlined in red ink immediately above the entry for the voucher. 

The debit to vouchers payable is entered in the Vouchers Payable Cr. column, and the amount of the asset or the expense on which the return or the allowance was made is adjusted by indicating the deduction in the corresponding debit amount column. When the columns are totaled, the figures in red should be added separately and written immediately above the columnar totals for the figures in black. The totals indicated in black ink are posted in the usual manner. The total in red ink in the Vouchers Payable Cr. column is posted as a debit to the vouchers payable account, and the corresponding red totals in the debit amount columns are posted as credits to the appropriate accounts. In practice, accountants often show a "red ink" entry in black, encircling the amount to show that it represents a deduction. The method of recording credit received for a return or an allowance is shown in Illustration No. 64 (Voucher No. 61), pages 362 and 

363. At the end of the month, after the columns were totaled, Vouchers Payable was debited for $25, and Purchases Returns and Allowances was credited for the same amount. The credit memorandum from the creditor or a note showing the amount of credit received should be filed with the voucher.

When credit is received for a return or an allowance in a later month, after the voucher register has been totaled and posted, the method just outlined cannot be followed. In this case a new voucher for the adjusted amount payable must be prepared, and the original voucher should be marked "Canceled" with a reference to the new voucher. The amount of the original voucher has, however, already been posted in the columnar total for the previous month. A correcting entry can be made in the general journal to reverse the entry that had been made in the voucher register in the previous period. This entry would debit Vouchers 

Payable and credit the account that had been charged in the

Ch. 15] 367 

དྲ་ previous period. A new voucher is then made out for the correct amount and entered in the Voucher Register, thus authorizing payment of the corrected figure. Reference to the journal entry disposing of the original disbursement authorization should be made in the When and How Paid columns on a line with the entry for the original voucher. 

1 The method of making an adjustment that affects a voucher issued in a previous month is shown in Illustration No. 64 (Voucher 

No. 62). When store equipment had been purchased for $180 from C. C. Shaw in May, an entry debiting Store Equipment for $180 and crediting Vouchers Payable for the same amount was made in the voucher register. In June, however, an allowance of $30 was received from Shaw because the equipment was slightly damaged. The original voucher for $180 was therefore canceled, and a new one for $150 was prepared. A journal entry was made debiting Vouchers Payable for $180 and crediting Store Equipment for a like amount. This entry canceled the entry of the previous month and made way for the new charge of $150 and its authoriz- 

Ition for payment.

Partial Payments. The plan of the voucher register assumes that each voucher is a unit and will be paid as a unit. No provision is made in the register for partial payments. If, then, a business customarily makes partial payments on its invoices, it should not plan to use a voucher register but should use some other method that is more adaptable to the recording of partial payments. 

If, at the time an invoice is received, the management knows that it will be paid in installments, several vouchers may be prepared, one for each installment.

Corrections. The careful checking of the records used in the voucher system will not eliminate errors entirely, but it should reduce them to a minimum.. If an error is discovered in the same month in which a voucher was recorded, the voucher and the entry in the register may be canceled and a new voucher may be issued. If the error is discovered after the amount of the voucher has been posted, the correction may be made in the way described above for an adjustment in the purchase of store equipment. 

Notes Payable. If a voucher is paid by the issuance of a note or by the acceptance of a draft or a trade acceptance, an entry debiting Vouchers Payable and crediting Notes Payable should be made in the general journal or the notes payable journal. An

Ch. 15 5 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

explanation indicating the method of payment is written on the voucher; and a similar explanation, with reference to the page of the journal in which the entry was made, is entered in the When and How Paid columns of the voucher register. The voucher is then filed in the paid vouchers file. 

At the time the note or the acceptance is to be paid, a new voucher will be issued. The entry in the voucher register will consist of a credit to Vouchers Payable and a debit to Notes Payable. The payment of this voucher will then be recorded in the usual manner. The entries required in the payment of a note are shown in Illustrations Nos. 64 and 65 (Voucher No. 63), pages 

362, 363, and 364.

The Voucher Register as a Combined Book of Original Entry and Sub sidiary Ledger

The use of the voucher register proves very convenient. Liabilities are recorded as soon as they are incurred, an accounts payable ledger is not needed, and a great saving of labor in posting is effected through the use of the columns in the register.

Each line of the voucher register may be considered as a separate account with a voucher. This account is credited when the voucher is recorded; it is debited when the voucher is indicated as paid. At any time, then, the voucher register will give the detail regarding the balance of the vouchers payable account. In this respect the voucher register is comparable to the accounts payable ledger, and it may be considered as a subsidiary ledger. 

The entry of the date of payment in the voucher register from the paid voucher is in the nature of a posting to a subsidiary ledger. 

The voucher itself is a business paper co-ordinating the activities of a number of specialized employees in a business organization. The vouchers, which are numbered serially, constitute a journal record of the obligations incurred and of the accounts to be charged. When the vouchers are filed in an unpaid vouchers file, they provide a subsidiary ledger showing the amounts due at various times. When vouchers are placed in the file, they represent credits; when they are removed from the file, they represent debits. 

Hence, vouchers themselves serve as business papers, a journal record, and a ledger record. 

The voucher register is a record that facilitates the accumulation of summaries to be posted to the general ledger. As indicated in a preceding paragraph, the voucher register may be con- The e C

Ch. 15] 369 

Isidered as a subsidiary ledger of authorized obligations, supporting the controlling account Vouchers Payable. 

The Voucher System and Financial Manage ment

The voucher system illustrates the adaptability of accounting procedure to new conditions in business practice. As a welldefined system it proves of great use to financial management. 

Its adoption has resulted in the following advantages: 

1. Information regarding invoices to be paid is furnished promptly. When the prevailing practice of a business provides for the payment of creditors from funds received through the collection of accounts receivable, the purchases journal and the accounts payable ledger provide the needed information. When emphasis is placed on meeting the terms of payment on all invoices and bills, the voucher system serves the needs of the financial officer more effectively than do the purchases journal and the accounts payable ledger. This officer is more interested in knowing when payments should be made than in knowing to whom the amounts are payable. The voucher system provides a flexible record in furnishing prompt information regarding invoices and bills authorized for payment. 

2. Misunderstandings are avoided by having payments cover specific invoices. In the interest of both the debtor and the creditor, the voucher system provides that each check issued shall cover a specific invoice or a specific group of invoices. This practice provides for the easy identification of amounts charged and amounts paid so that there can be agreement regarding the balance owed. The payment of round amounts to apply on account is unsatisfactory from the point of view of both the creditor and the debtor. Discrimination among creditors by debtors is equally unsatisfactory from both points of view. The voucher system represents a modification of accounting practice to fit improved financial practices. 

3. The practice of taking cash discounts is facilitated. The voucher system is particularly useful in providing the necessary information for those concerns that follow the practice of taking cash discounts. As a matter of financial policy it is always less costly to borrow funds at a local bank in order to take advantage of cash discounts than to permit such discounts to be neglected. 

Under the voucher system each invoice can be approved promptly and filed in the unpaid vouchers file under the last date when the

[Ch. 15 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

discount can be taken. The unpaid vouchers file provides an orderly list of all future obligations arranged according to the dates of payment. By taking the discounts, the business gets the maximum use of its cash balance and at the same time provides for a maximum return from available cash discounts. 

4. Authorization of future disbursements is given at the proper time. The voucher system provides for the approval of expense items at the time when such approval can be given most intelligently. The adequacy of a service purchased should be determined at the time the service is rendered rather than at a later date when payment is to be made. If the service is satisfactory, future payment should be authorized at once. Hence, no further investigation is necessary when the check is to be drawn. 

5. Specialized employees are organized as a working unit. The voucher system also represents a closely co-ordinated arrangement of journalizing and posting to carry out a specific purpose in business management. One or more special business papers are prepared to facilitate the record of a transaction that is to occur. 

The journal record of this transaction is entered in a book of original entry, the voucher register, from which postings are made to the ledger at the end of the month. The voucher system is, however, adapted to what is to be done rather than to what has been done, for it not only provides a record of the obligations owed to creditors for goods and services purchased, but also facilitates the subsequent procedure of payment. 

Such advantages are, however, accompanied by a lack of flexibility in the use of the register. Whenever records with special columns are used, the procedure is necessarily rigid. It has already been shown that a special journal does not allow so complete an explanation as the general journal. In the use of the voucher register the procedure is even more formal. This fact has been shown in the difficulties encountered in making corrections and in handling purchases returns and allowances, partial payments, and notes payable.

Definition 

CHAPTER XVI PARTNERSHIPS 

If a sole proprietor finds that he cannot continue to conduct his business advantageously as a sole proprietorship, or if a person starting a business does not have sufficient capital or ability to manage it by himself, he may have one or more associates contribute their capital or services, or both, to the development of the business. By these means a partnership is established. 

An early definition of the partnership called it "a contract of two or more competent persons to place their money, effects, labor, and skill, or some or all of them, in lawful commerce and business, and to divide the profits and bear the losses in certain proportions.' More recently the Uniform Partnership Act, adopted by a large number of the states, calls the partnership "an association of two or more persons to carry on as co-owners a business for profit."

Although these definitions state clearly the nature of a partnership from the legal point of view, the partnership may be better understood from a business point of view by considering the characteristics that distinguish it from other forms of legal organization. 

Characteristics of the

Partnership The fundamental characteristic of a partnership is expressed in the definitions given above, in that it is an association of persons. It is distinctly a personal organization, and from that personal aspect the following essential characteristics are derived: 

1. Mutual Agency. Each partner is the agent of the partnership. For example, one partner can make an agreement to purchase goods or services for the firm, and the other partners will be bound by his act if the goods or the services are such as the business uses or might use in the course of its affairs. 376

Ch. 16] 377 

PARTNERSHIPS 

2. Limited Life. A partnership, because of its personal nature, cannot be perpetual. It may be dissolved as the result of any one of a variety of causes, some of which are likely to occur at any time. The death, incapacity, or withdrawal of one of the partners causes a dissolution of the partnership; if a partner sells his interest, the partnership terminates; if the members admit another partner, the old partnership ends but a new partnership is brought into existence. 

3. Unlimited Liability. Each member of a partnership is jointly and individually liable for the debts of the partnership. 

Therefore a partner may not only lose what he has invested in the partnership, but also be required to use his private property to pay the debts of the partnership if the business becomes insolvent. 

For example, if Jones and Smith each invest $3,000 in a partnership, and through ill luck or poor management the partnership later has liabilities of $6,000 and assets of only $5,000, the creditors may recover the $1,000 excess of the liabilities over the assets from either Jones or Smith individually. Since each partner is free to represent the firm as an agent, and the partners are liable individually, as well as jointly, for the debts of the firm, an illchosen partner may bring financial difficulties to the other members of the firm. If the partners are to feel certain that their interests are being protected properly, accurate accounting records that will at all times show the financial condition of the firm, as well as the tendency of that condition, are essential. 

4. Co-ownership of Partnership Property. The property in vested by a partner in a partnership is no longer his own personal property but is jointly owned by all partners. For this reason it is essential that the valuation placed on the contributed property be a fair and just one. After contribution, the partner has only a dollar and cents claim against all the property of the partnership. 

5. Participation in Partnership Profits. Another feature essential to a partnership is the co-ownership of the profits. If the profits are shared on any basis other than co-ownership, a partnership does not exist. For example, an employee may be given a percentage of the profits as a remuneration for his services. This does not, however, constitute a partnership. If the intention of the parties is that co-ownership of the profits shall exist, the agreement between them will be construed as constituting a partnership.

[Ch. 16 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

Accounting for Part- In the main the accounting for a partnernerships ship is the same as that for a business with any other form of ownership. The current transactions completed in the general operation of the business are similar; and the accounting process, as well as the papers, the records, and the reports employed, is essentially the same. The legal relationship existing between the members of a partnership gives rise to transactions that are not common to the sole proprietorship. These transactions have to do with the formation, the operation, and the dissolution of the partnership. The operation phase is principally that of participation in partnership profits, which will be discussed in this chapter. 

Formation of a Partnership

As between the parties themselves, a partnership is founded upon a voluntary contract having all the essential elements of any other contract. 

Since the interest in the partnership here is primarily from the point of view of accounting, the various legal questions arising from the relationship of the partners will not be discussed at any length.

Disputes, misunderstandings, and difficult situations are likely to arise in a partnership because of the peculiar relationship existing between the partners. To prevent these, or at least to reduce them to a minimum, a written contract should be made. This contract is known as the articles of copartnership. It should contain the following data, as well as any additional provisions that may be applicable to the particular undertaking: 

1. Date. 

2. Names of the parties to the contract. 

3. Statement that these parties are to be partners. 

4. Nature of the business or undertaking. 

5. Duration of the relationship. 

6. Name and location of the business. 

7. Amount of the capital and of each partner's investment. 

8. Provision for the payment of expenses. 

9. Provisions for the distribution of profits and the sharing of losses. 

10. Rights of the partners to withdraw money for personal use. 

11. Provision for the maintenance of books of account. 

12. Duties of the several partners. 

13. Restraint upon the partners as to becoming sureties, etc. 

14. Provision for dissolution. 

15. Provision for the division of the assets after dissolution. 

16. Special provisions and stipulations.

Ch. 16] 379 

PARTNERSHIPS

If the partners invest asşets in addition to cash and transfer liabilities to the partnership, either of two methods may be used in recording the investment. An entry may be made in the cashbook to record the cash investment, and another may be made in the general journal to record the other assets and the liabilities; or the entire investment may be recorded in the general journal, and the cash investment may be recorded in the cash book. In the latter case the cash items will be checked in both the general journal and the cashbook so that they will not be posted twice. 

To illustrate such entries, it is assumed that on January 2 A. E. Allen and N. E. Hale, who have been conducting rival hardware stores, enter into a partnership by consolidating their assets and liabilities. The assets and the liabilities of A. E. Allen are as follows: cash, $1,025.25; accounts receivable, $4,165; merchandise inventory, $3,790.65; store equipment, $2,300; notes payable, $1,156.65; accounts payable, $2,124.25. The assets and the lia-

[Ch. 16

Ch. 16] 381 

PARTNERSHIPS 

Personal Accounts of The articles of copartnership may provide the Partners that each partner will be permitted to withdraw cash at intervals during the course of operations. If this is the case, the withdrawals are debited to the partners' personal accounts. A partner's personal account is, in nature, the same as the personal account of a sole proprietor. At the end of the fiscal period the partner's share of the profit or the loss is closed into his personal account. The debit or the credit balance of the personal account is permitted to remain in that account or is transferred to the capital account, the procedure depending upon the partners' agreement.

For example, at the end of the first fiscal year, the personal accounts of Allen and Hale have debit balances of $2,500 and $1,800 respectively. If the profit for the year, $4,200, is to be distributed equally, the entry to close the profit and loss summary account will consist of a debit of $4,200 to Profit and Loss Summary and a credit of $2,100 to each of the partners' personal accounts.

Allen's personal account will now have a debit balance of $400; Hale's personal account, a credit balance of $300. These balances may be permitted to remain in the personal accounts; or, if they are considered as representative of permanent changes in proprietorship, they may be transferred to the capital accounts. 

Division of Profits 

The distribution of profits or losses frequently presents one of the most difficult problems in partnership accounting. If the partners have no agreement in this respect, the law provides that profits or losses should be divided equally, regardless of the amounts of the investments. The partners may, however, make any agreement they wish in regard to the division of profits or losses. The agreement should be quite specific so that misunderstandings can be avoided. Even if the agreement is written in as definite terms as possible, some problems may arise because of changes in proprietorship, interest on investments, and loans. The partners should therefore understand the effect of different transactions relating to the proprietorship so that agreements that will fully carry out their intentions can be made.

Partnership profits include a return for partners' services, for partners' capital investments, and economic or pure profit.

[Ch. 16 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

Since the partnership is a personal organization, the services of the partners come free of cost to the partnership. In the absence of a specific agreement, the partners receive no salaries. The capital contributions of partners do not bear interest. Profits must therefore be interpreted to include these two factors as well as pure profit.

If the service and capital contributions of partners are equal, an equal sharing in partnership profits will be equitable. But if one partner contributes three times as much capital as the other, the distribution of profits should take into account the unequal contribution of capital. Or, if the services of one partner are much more valuable to the partnership than those of the other, provision for unequal service contribution should be made in profit participation.

Arising out of the recognition of these three factors in partnership profits, many methods of distributing profits or losses are employed. Fundamentally, they give effect to the three elements found in partnership profits.

The basis for distributing profits or losses is ordinarily one of the following: 

1. According to a fixed ratio. This includes an equal distribution, which is a fixed ratio. 

2. According to investments. This may be based on the opening capital or the average capital. 

3. According to a fixed ratio after salaries have been allowed. 

4. According to a fixed ratio after salaries and interest on capital have been allowed.

While Methods 3 and 4 provide for a salary allowance, it should be understood that this is considered as a mode of distributing profits and that the salaries are not expenses of the partnership. As said above, the partnership organization includes services and capitals of partners free of cost to the partnership. These four methods will be discussed and illustrated on the following pages. 

(1) Fixed Ratio-Equal Shares. When profits are distributed equally, the problem is simple. After the profit for a fiscal period has been determined, it is divided equally among the partners, the profit and loss summary account being debited and the partners' personal accounts being credited. If the partners wish, the balances of the personal accounts can be transferred later to the capital accounts.

Ch. 16] 383 

PARTNERSHIPS 

For example, in the partnership of A. E. Allen and N. E. 

Hale, the profit at the end of the first year is $4,200, the credit balance of the profit and loss summary account. The profit will be divided equally if the partners have agreed to do so or if there is no agreement regarding the division. The personal account of 

Allen and that of Hale will be credited for $2,100, regardless of inequalities or changes in the capital accounts. The profit and loss summary account will be debited for $4,200. The journal entry on December 31 to record the distribution of the profit will be as follows: 

Dec. 31

Profit and Loss Summary. 

A. E. Allen, Personal. 

N. E. Hale, Personal.

To distribute the net profit for the year to the partners' personal accounts. 4,200 00 2,100 00 2,100 00 Fixed Ratio-Unequal Shares. In some cases one partner may contribute more in investment, time, or ability to the partnership than any of the others. Under such circumstances it is only fair that he should receive more of the profits than the other partners. 

This may be done by allowing him a larger salary than the other partners, and then dividing the profits equally; or it may be accomplished by dividing the profits according to a fixed ratio that allows for the difference in the contributions of the partners. 

For example, the partnership agreement of Allen and Hale might have stipulated that Allen was to receive 30 per cent of the profits and Hale 70 per cent. The latter was allowed a greater share of the profits because his contribution was greater than that of Allen, and his experience and ability would be responsible largely for the success of the enterprise. Under these circumstances the journal entry made on December 31 at the end of the first year, in order to transfer the profit from the profit and loss summary account to the partners' personal accounts, would have been as follows: 

Dec. 31

Profit and Loss Summary. 

A. E. Allen, Personal. 

N. E. Hale, Personal. 4,200 00 1,260 00 2,940 00 To distribute the net profit for the year to the partners' personal accounts.

[Ch. 16 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

(2) According to Investments-Opening Capital. In the di vision of profits an allowance may not be made for any difference in the ability and experience of the partners, but an allowance may be made for the difference in the investments. In such a case the profits may be divided according to the beginning investments. 

For example, if Allen and Hale had agreed to divide profits or losses on the basis of their original investments, their respective shares of the profit for the first year would have been computed in the following manner: 

A. E. Allen, Investment of January 2 

N. E. Hale, Investment of January 2.

Total Investment... 

$ 8,000.00 12,000.00 $20,000.00 

A. E. Allen, Profit, December 31: 8,000-$4,200.00-$1,680.00 20,000 20,000 

N. E. Hale, Profit, December 31: 12,000-$4,200.00 = 2,520.00

Total Profit.. 

.$4,200.00

The journal entry to record this division of the profit would have been: 

Dec. 31

Profit and loss Summary. 

4,200 00 

A. E. Allen, Personal. 1,680 00 

N. E. Hale, Personal. 2,520 00 To distribute the net profit for the year to the partners' personal accounts.

When profits are to be divided according to the investments, the simplest method of computing the amounts to be transferred to the personal accounts is to use, as the basis, the investments that the partners made at the beginning of the partnership. This method does not, however, encourage partners to leave profits in the business and to provide the additional funds that will be needed in expanding the business. To encourage the partners to leave the profits in the business, the agreement may specify that the profits are to be divided according to the proprietorship at the beginning of each period. If this is done, the agreement should indicate clearly whether the proprietorship of each partner is to be interpreted as being the balance of the capital account or the combined balance of the capital and the personal accounts.

Ch. 16] 385 

PARTNERSHIPS According to Investments-Average Capital. When profits are divided according to the investments, the partners may wish to recognize all changes in proprietorship, regardless of whether these changes have been recorded in the capital or the personal accounts. Not only the investments may be different at the beginning of a fiscal period, but also their ratio may change during. the fiscal period because a partner withdraws a part of his investment or invests an additional amount. Such changes will be shown in the partners' capital accounts. On the other hand, one partner may withdraw for living expenses a greater amount than the other. 

This withdrawal will be shown in the partner's personal account. 

To take care of such differences, the profits may be divided according to the average capital. The calculation of the average capital may be based upon the capital accounts of the partners or upon both the capital and the personal accounts. If the latter plan is used, the debits and the credits in the personal accounts are considered as if they had been made directly in the capital accounts.

When the profit is to be divided according to the average capital, a simple method of calculation is as follows:

The beginning investment is multiplied by the number of months from the time of the investment to the time when there was a change in the investment. The investment on the latter date is then multiplied by the number of months from that date until the time when another change occurred. This procedure is continued for the entire fiscal period. These amounts are totaled. 

The results then express capital investments in the same unit, that is, month-dollars. This unit takes into account the time factor as well as the amount factor. Each partner's participation in the profits is determined by the ratio obtained by placing his total month-dollars over the total month-dollars of the partnership. This is the share of the profit to be assigned to the partner. 

Any unit of time may be used in determining the average capital. Days or weeks, instead of months, can be used if significant capital changes occur at other than month periods. In such cases the units would be day-dollars or week-dollars. 

For example, Allen and Hale might have decided to divide their profit of $4,200 according to the average capital as shown by their capital accounts. Their original investments, additions

[Ch. 16 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

to capital, and withdrawals from capital might have appeared as follows in their capital accounts at the end of the year:

(3) According to a Fixed Ratio after Salaries Have Been Al lowed. The articles of copartnership often provide that each partner is to receive a salary for his services. If the salaries are paid at regular intervals in fixed amounts, in the same manner as salaries of employees, the partners' personal accounts may be debited for such amounts. At the end of the fiscal period the amounts that the partnership agreement allows as salaries may be debited to the profit and loss summary account and credited to the partners' personal accounts. The balance of the profit and loss summary account is then distributed in the ratio agreed upon by the partners.

Assume that Allen and Hale agreed to divide profits equally after salaries of $100 and $125 a month, respectively, had been allowed. When the partners withdrew their salaries in cash each month, their personal accounts were debited and the cash account was credited. At the end of the year the following journal entry was made:

Dec. 31 Profit and Loss Summary. 

A. E. Allen, Personal. 

N. E. Hale, Personal. 

To transfer from the profit and loss summary account to the partners' personal accounts an amount that is equal to the amounts allowed the partners as salaries. 2,700 00 70000 1,200 00 1,500 00 The total of the salaries, $2,700, was then deducted from the net profit, $4,200, and the remainder, $1,500, was distributed equally. 

Dec. 31

Profit and Loss Summary. 

A. E. Allen, Personal. 

N. E. Hale, Personal.

To distribute the balance of the net profit equally to the partners. 1,500 00 750 00 750 00

Statements for a Part nership

It is often desirable in preparing a statement of profit and loss for a partnership to show the distribution of the profit for the fiscal period. This information is added to the report after the net profit has been shown. This section of the statement of profit and loss of Allen and Hale (based on the last distribution of profit) might be as follows:

Ch. 16] 391 

PARTNERSHIPS 

The method of reporting the capital of a partnership on the balance sheet depends primarily on the amount of information that it is thought desirable to show. Sometimes the balance sheet shows for each partner the investment at the beginning of the period, any additional investment, the partner's share of the net profit or the net loss for the period, any withdrawal from capital, and the personal withdrawals made during the period. Instead of reporting such detailed information on the balance sheet, it is often more satisfactory to prepare a report that supplements the balance sheet. This report is known as a statement of the partner's capital accounts. It is of value to the partners because it shows in detail the reasons for the changes in the proprietorship. 

A statement of the capital accounts of Allen and Hale might be prepared in the following form:

ALLEN AND HALE STATEMENT OF PARTNERS' CAPITAL ACCOUNTS FOR YEAR ENDING DECEMBER 31, 19—— Capital, January 2, 19--. 

Share of Profit for Year. 

Less Personal Drawings. 

Capital, December 31, 19--. 

A. E. ALLEN N. E. HALE

The profit at the end of the year is $7,500. (b) What would have been the journal entries if the profit had been $6,000?

New Partnerships Formed From Old 

Partnerships 

CHAPTER XVII PARTNERSHIPS (Concluded) 

In the preceding chapter the creation of a partnership by two or more individuals was presented. The relations among the partners in the conduct of the partnership were shown to be embodied in the articles of copartnership. When an existing partnership admits another partner or consolidates with another firm, new articles of copartnership are needed to define the relationships among the new partners. Although the old business may continue as before, a new partnership exists from the legal point of view. 

Since the partnership is distinctly a personal form of business organization, any change in partners necessarily means the end of the old and the beginning of a new partnership. The problems incident to the formation of a new partnership from an old partnership will now be discussed.

Admission of a New 

Partner The admission of a new partner must be made with the consent of all the other partners in the business. Furthermore, new articles of copartnership that include the future profit-and-loss ratio for the partners, as well as all other necessary provisions applicable to the undertaking, must be drawn. A new partner may be admitted to a partnership as the result of the purchase of an interest in the proprietorship or as the result of an investment. When an interest in the partnership is purchased, the proprietorship of the new partner is obtained from one or more of the old partners. When an interest is obtained through an investment, the total assets of the firm are increased by the property invested by the new partner. 

Admission of a Partner by the Purchase of an Interest

When a share of the proprietorship in a partnership is sold, the assets and the liabilities of the firm remain unchanged.

The amount paid for the new partner's interest in the proprietorship is a matter wholly outside of the operations of the partnership. 

The only result of the transaction is a transfer of a part of the 398

Ch. 17] 399 

PARTNERSHIPS 

proprietorship of the original partners to a new partner. The amount paid by the new partner is not recorded on the books of the partnership, for the payment is a transaction between the individuals and does not affect the accounts of the firm. For. example, P. A. Henry and J. P. Jones are partners, each having a proprietorship of $10,000. They sell to M. O. King a one-fourth, or $5,000, interest in the partnership. The amount of this new interest is to represent equal reductions in the capital accounts of the old partners. Since the two old partners, instead of the partnership, receive the payment made by King, the only entry required in the books of the firm is: Feb. 1 

P. A. Henry, Capital.. 

2,500/00 

J. P. Jones, Capital.. 

2,500 00 

M. O. King, Capital... 

5,000 00 

To record the sale of a one-fourth interest to M. O. King.

This entry will be made regardless of the amount that King may pay. The total proprietorship remains $20,000, and King has a one-fourth interest, or $5,000 in capital. 

The amount paid by King to the partners for the one-fourth interest has no effect on the partnership accounts. This transaction is between the new partner and the old partners as individuals and is not a partnership transaction. The only effect on the partnership accounts is that illustrated above. 

Admission of a Partner A new partner who makes an investment by an Investment may be admitted in the partnership. If a person makes an investment in a partnership, he does not buy an interest from the other partners; he makes a new contribution to the business, and the proprietorship is thereby increased. For example, J. M. Ade and C. F. Harper are partners, each having a proprietary interest of $10,000. On October 1, R. C. Reed invests $5,000 in cash in the business. For this investment he is to receive a proprietorship in the business equal to his investment. 

The entry, in journal form, to record this transaction is: Oct. 1 Cash. 

R. C. Reed, Capital. 

To record the investment of R. C. Reed. √ 5,000 00 5,000/00

[Ch. 17 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

Reed has a one-fifth interest in the proprietorship, which now totals $25,000. It should be noted, however, that a one-fifth interest in the partnership does not mean that Reed is entitled to one fifth of the profits unless the partnership agreement states that profits are to be divided in the ratio of the original investments. Reed will share profits equally with the other partners in the absence of any agreement regarding the division of profits. 

If, instead of investing cash in the business of Ade and Harper, Reed transfers the property and the liabilities of a business that he has been operating, an account must be debited for each of the assets taken over; an account must be credited for each of the liabilities assumed by the firm; and Reed's capital account must be credited for the difference, which is his proprietorship in the new business.

For example, if Reed invests a $6,000 building on a $2,000 lot, which are covered by a $3,000 mortgage, the entry to record his investment is:

Oct. 1 

Building. 

Land... 

Mortgage Payable. 

R. C. Reed, Capital.. 

To record the investment of R. C. Reed.

6,000 00 2,000 00 173,000 00 5,000 00 Under certain conditions a person may be willing to invest in a partnership more than the value of the interest in it that he will receive. Either of two methods is commonly used when a partner is admitted on such a basis. The method used will depend upon the agreement between the parties. (1) Recording a Bonus. For example, R. B. Adams and A. 0.

Burr form a partnership, each investing $4,000 in cash. After a few years the firm of Adams and Burr becomes quite prosperous. A sufficient amount of the profits has been added to the partners' capital accounts to make the credit balance of each $9,000. William Chase wishes to join the partnership and is willing to invest $10,000 in cash. As the firm is unusually prosperous, he is willing to accept a one-fourth interest for this amount, although his investment is greater than that of either of the other two partners. 

After the new investment of $10,000 is added to the old pro-

A one-fourth interest in a $28,000 proprietorship is $7,000. Since 

Chase is investing $10,000 in cash for a $7,000 interest, he is, in effect, paying the old partners a bonus of $3,000 to become a member of the partnership. The entry made upon the admission of Chase on January 2 is as follows: 

Jan. 2 Cash.

The capital account of Adams and that of Burr are credited with one half of the bonus, for the partners share equally in profits and losses. If, for example, the articles of copartnership of Adams and Burr had stated a fixed profit-and-loss ratio of 60 per cent for Adams and 40 per cent for Burr, the excess contribution of 

Chase would have been divided as follows: Adams, $1,800; Burr, $1,200. The bonus received is always divided among the former partners in the profit-and-loss ratio that was used before the admission of the new partner. (2) Recording Goodwill. The preceding entry records the transaction satisfactorily, but Chase may object to having his capital shown as $7,000 when he has actually invested $10,000. 

He is, however, satisfied with a one-fourth interest in the firm. 

An alternate method of recording the investment will avoid this objection.

It can be seen that since Chase is willing to invest $10,000 for a one-fourth interest, the value placed on the partnership is $40,000, that is, four times his investment. The value of the partnership without the additional investment is therefore $30,000, which is $12,000 in excess of the proprietorship shown by the partnership accounts. If Chase is willing to invest $10,000 for a onefourth interest, the firm must possess certain rights, privileges, or advantages that are of great value. Such rights, privileges, or advantages are assets, but they are different from other assets in that they are not tangible and ordinarily have value to the business only as a going concern. They are therefore called in-

Ch 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

[Ch. 17 tangible assets and are shown on the balance sheet under this heading. The most common asset in this classification is goodwill. 

Goodwill has been defined by the Treasury Department of the United States Government as "the value attached to a business over and above the value of the physical property." Goodwill arises when a business wins the favor of its customers to such an be extent that the customers will probably return to trade in the future.

It is not considered desirable to record goodwill as an asset unless it has been purchased or sold. In the example used previously, Chase is willing to invest $10,000 for a one-fourth interest in a partnership having a capital of $18,000. In this case it is permissible to record the asset goodwill at an amount sufficient to increase the capital of the former partners so that the investment of $10,000 made by Chase will represent one fourth of the total proprietorship. The combined capital of the two former partners must therefore be increased from $18,000 to $30,000. The amount of the increase, $12,000, represents the value of the goodwill. Goodwill is set up as an asset, and its value is divided between the former partners in the ratio in which they previously shared profits and losses. Since Adams and Burr share profits and losses equally, the following entries are made in the books of the new partnership upon the admission of Chase: Jan. 2 Goodwill..

R. B. Adams, Capital. 

A. O. Burr, Capital. 

To record the asset goodwill and thus to increase the capital of 

Adams and Burr to three fourths of the total proprietorship after the admission of Chase. 

12,000 00 

6,000 00 

6,000 00 2 Cash. 

10,000 00 

William Chase, Capital. 

10,000 00 

To record the investment of Chase, which is to represent one fourth of the total proprietorship.

When the asset goodwill is set up, the capital of the partners may seem to be unduly inflated, but any one investigating the records of the company can observe that goodwill is on the books and can draw his own conclusions regarding the actual capital of the firm. Although this method of recording goodwill may be

Ch. 17] 403 

PARTNERSHIPS 

followed when the incoming partner insists that his capital account show his actual investment, the method of recording a bonus is recommended because it is simple and results in the proprietorship being based on the net tangible assets. 

The goodwill that a new partner is willing to pay for should be reflected by the profits of the old partnership. Therefore, when a partnership earns a profit in excess of the return normally expected, a new partner should expect to pay for this goodwill. 

Various ways of measuring the amount of goodwill exist. Two methods will be mentioned. (1) The value of goodwill may be estimated by the capitalization of the excess profits. For instance, if the capital of a business is $100,000, a yearly return of 10 per cent, or $10,000, may be normal. If the average yearly profit, however, is $14,000, $4,000 represents the excess profit. The goodwill may be determined by capitalizing the $4,000 excess profit; that is, by dividing $4,000 by 10 per cent, the normal rate of profit. 

The result, $40,000, is the estimated value of the goodwill. In other words, the investment in this business might be $140,000, and still a 10 per cent return would be made on the capital. (2) 

The value of goodwill may be estimated to be equal to the excess profit of several years. If an excess yearly profit of $4,000 is expected for at least the next five years, the goodwill may be estimated at $20,000 (5 times $4,000). 

Admission of a Partner with a Bonus or an Allowance for Goodwill to the New Partner

In some cases the capital account of a new partner may be credited with an amount in excess of his investment. Either of two methods is commonly used when a partner is admitted on such a basis. The method to be used will depend upon the agreement between the parties. (1) Recording a Bonus. Suppose that C. R. Daily and M. G.

Miles are partners with proprietary interests of $8,000 and $12,000 respectively. The firm needs additional capital and the services of W. R. Morris, an expert in its type of business. The two partners therefore offer Morris a one-third interest in the partnership if he will invest $7,000 in cash. If the capital account of Morris is credited for the amount of his investment, $7,000, the total proprietorship will be $27,000. The books should show, however, that his share is one third. Therefore, the partners allow him a bonus of $2,000, which is transferred from their capital accounts 1

[Ch. 17 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

in the profit-and-loss ratio. Morris then has a one-third, or $9,000, interest in the total proprietorship of $27,000. Since Daily and 

Miles formerly shared profits and losses equally, the following entries are made upon the admission of the new partner: Nov. Cash. 

7,000 00

1,000 00

2,000 00

To adjust the capital accounts so that the account of Morris will show his one-third interest in the partnership. 

This method decreases the proprietorship of the two original partners. It is assumed, however, that the investment of Morris and the service that this partner will render to the firm will, in the long run, more than offset the present disadvantage. (2) Recording Goodwill. The partners may not be willing, however, to have their capital reduced. Since the admission of 

Morris is expected to react favorably on the firm, they may prefer to set up goodwill and to credit the capital account of the new partner with an amount that will increase his proprietary interest to one third of the total capital. As the combined capital of the two former partners is $20,000, which represents two thirds of the proprietorship of the new partnership, the capital account of Morris must have a credit balance of $10,000. Since Morris invests $7,000, the goodwill account is debited for $3,000 and his capital account is credited for the same amount. PARTNERSHIPS 

1 The method of recording a bonus is usually preferred when a partner pays an amount that is smaller than the interest that he actually receives, because the total investment is then based on the net tangible assets. 

Significance of the Part- The partners may agree to distribute profits ners' Capital Accounts and losses in any way they wish, irrespective of their capital interest in the partnership, although, as already explained, one basis of distribution is the investments of the partners. Although the partners' investments may not affect the division of profits and losses, they will determine the amounts of the claims that the partners may have upon the assets when the partnership is dissolved for any reason. 

Consolidation of Businesses When a sole proprietorship or a partnership is consolidated with a partnership, the opening entries for the new partnership are similar to those that have just been discussed. For example, if a sole proprietor is to join a partnership and is to receive an interest in the firm equal to the net worth of his business, an entry debiting the various assets accounts, crediting the liability accounts, and crediting the capital account of the new partner must be made. 

If, upon the consolidation of his business with a partnership, a sole proprietor receives an interest in the partnership that is more or less than the net worth of his business, the entries to record the transaction are substantially the same as those required when a person invests cash in a partnership and receives an interest greater or less than the amount of the investment. 

Instead of only a debit to Cash, the entry will contain a debit to each asset account and a credit to each liability account. 

Revaluation of Assets

When two businesses are consolidated, an examination of the assets of one or both of the businesses may show that certain assets are recorded on the books at more or less than their real values. Before any entries are made in the books of the new organization, the records of each business that is being consolidated must be made to show the actual values of the assets and the true net worth of the business. 

After these entries have been made, the opening entries of the partnership will be made in the usual manner.

[Ch. 17 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES

Upon an examination of the assets of Hart, it is found that an additional allowance of $1,000 for bad debts should be established and that the present value of the merchandise inventory is $500 more than the book value. When the records of the partnership are examined, it is found that the reserve for depreciation of the building should be increased by $2,000. 

The entry to record the revaluation of the assets of Hart, and the balance sheet prepared by Hart after this entry has been posted, are shown below.

PARTNERSHIPS 

Dissolution of a Part- The dissolution of a partnership takes nership place when the agreement between the partners is terminated by an act of the parties, by operation of the law, or by a judicial decree. The ways in which a partnership may be dissolved under the Uniform Partnership Act are as follows:

The partnership may be dissolved by mutual agreement of the partners. This may result from the accomplishment of the purpose for which the partnership was formed, or it may be that the partners do not care to continue their association. A partner may withdraw or assign his interest with the permission of the other partners.

The court will decree a dissolution of a partnership upon application of one or more of its members, in case of the misconduct or incapacity of a member, or if the undertaking is impractical or incapable of being put into operation. A dissolution also results in case of the death or bankruptcy of a member, the bankruptcy of the firm, or a declaration of war against a country of which one or more partners are citizens.

Effect of Dissolution 

Dissolution terminates the agreement that upon a Partnership gave the partnership existence, but it does not necessarily mean the discontinuance of the business. The admission of a new partner or the consolidation of the partnership with another firm is an act of dissolution. The partnership agreement is void; but new articles of copartnership may be provided to define the altered relationship between the partners, and the business may continue without interruption. Frequently, however, a partnership may wind up its affairs after dissolution and go out of existence. Some of the problems that may arise upon the dissolution of a partnership will now be discussed. 

Retirement of a Partner When a member retires from a partnership, his interest may be purchased by one of the other partners. The entry required in such a case is a debit to the proprietorship account of the one who is retiring and a credit to the proprietorship account of the other partner. This entry is made regardless of the amount paid for the interest of the retiring partner, since the payment represents a transaction between two individuals and consequently does not affect the records of the partnership.

[Ch. 17 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

If, upon retirement, a partner withdraws assets of the business, the assets may be (1) equal in value to his proprietorship, (2) greater in value than his proprietorship, or (3) less in value than his proprietorship.

If the assets are equal to his proprietorship, it is necessary only to debit his proprietorship account and to credit the accounts with the assets. If, because of the unusually prosperous condition of the firm, or for any other reason, the retiring partner is given assets greater in value than the book value of his proprietorship, the total proprietorship of the other partners is decreased. For example, Clay, Scott, and Calhoun are equal partners in a business having a total proprietorship of $30,000. Scott wishes to withdraw from the partnership, but the business has been so unusually prosperous that he is not willing to give up his interest for $10,000, its book value. The partners agree to permit him to withdraw $12,000 as his share of the business. The entry on May 31 to record this withdrawal is:

May 31 

John Scott, Capital.. 

Frank Clay, Capital.. 

Henry Calhoun, Capital. 

Cash... 

To record the withdrawal of Scott, who was allowed $12,000 for his interest in the business. 10,000/00 1,000 00 1,000 00 

12,000 00

Under certain circumstances a partner may be willing to withdraw from a partnership and to accept assets of less worth than the book value of his interest. Perry, Cook, and Byrd are equal partners in a business having a net proprietorship of $30,000. 

Cook is anxious to withdraw; but he realizes that if he forces a sale, the assets will not bring their book value. He is therefore willing to accept $8,000 in cash, which is $2,000 less than the book value of his interest. The entry to record the withdrawal is: 

Aug. 31

N. W. Cook, Capital.. 

Cash...

N. P. Perry, Capital. 

S. P. Byrd, Capital 

To record the withdrawal of Cook, who was allowed $8,000 for his interest in the business.

Ch. 17] 411 

PARTNERSHIPS 

*Death of a Partner 

Since the death of a partner automatically dissolves the partnership, the books must be closed immediately, and the profit or the loss since the last closing date must be determined and distributed in accordance with the partnership agreement. The balance of the capital account of the deceased may then be transferred to a liability account with the estate. The surviving partners may draw up new articles of copartnership and either continue in the business by themselves or admit a new partner.

Often, however, the determination of the profit at the time of the death of a partner is very inconvenient. The partnership agreement may provide for such a contingency by allowing the business to continue to the end of the fiscal period. At that time the profit for the fiscal period can be estimated without difficulty. 

A part of this profit will represent the share of the deceased for the time during which he was an active member of the partnership. 

In the determination of the amount owed the estate of the deceased, the agreement of the partnership may also allow for goodwill or for increases in the value of the assets. Sometimes the agreement may provide that the estate or an heir shall continue in the place of the deceased partner. In that case the only entry required is one transferring the balances of the personal and the capital accounts of the deceased partner to the corresponding accounts of the new partner. In many cases the surviving partners may decide to wind up the affairs of the business. 

Liquidation of a Part- 

When a partnership is to cease operation nership and liquidation is to take place, it is necessary first to ascertain the net profit or the net loss to date and to distribute it to the partners' accounts so that the net proprietorship of each partner will be shown. It is necessary then to convert the assets into cash. When cash has been realized, it is applied in the following manner: 

1. To pay creditors other than partners. 

2. To repay loans or advances made to the business by partners.

These amounts do not represent capital or profits. 

3. To return the capital invested by the partners. 

The process of liquidation is illustrated by the following example. Head, Howard, and Stewart are partners who divide

[Ch. 171 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

profits in the ratio of 50 per cent, 30 per cent, and 20 per cent respectively. They decide to dissolve the partnership on June 30. av 

The books are closed preparatory to liquidation, and a balance sheet is prepared. The following is a condensed form of the report:

The assets are sold for $35,000; the profit on the sale of the assets is distributed in the profit-and-loss ratio; the liabilities are paid; and the remainder of the cash is then distributed according to the investments of the partners. The part of a loss charged to the capital account of a partner may sometimes be greater than the partner's investment. The debit balance of the capital account will therefore represent an amount owed the other partners. The remaining cash will not be sufficient, however, to pay the other partners until the partner whose account has a debit balance pays the amount that he owes. 

In this case cash should be distributed in such a manner that, if the partner is unable to pay, the others will bear this additional loss in the profit-and-loss ratio that exists between themselves. 

To illustrate, assume that the assets of Head, Howard, and Stewart realize only $10,000. The following journal entries are made:

O. A. Howard, Capital.

P. M. Stewart, Capital.. 

Loss or Gain on Sale of Assets..

2,000 00

To record the payment of the liabilities. 30 30 

O. A. Howard, Capital. 

P. M. Stewart, Capital.. 2,800 00 5,200 00 

8,000 00 Cash. 

To distribute the remaining cash to Howard and Stewart so that the balance in their accounts will be in the profit-and-loss ratio of 3/5 and 2/5. Before the last journal entry was made, the capital accounts of the partners had the following balances: R. S. Head, $1,000 (debit); O. A. Howard, $3,400 (credit); and P. M. Stewart, $5,600 (credit). Only $8,000 in cash was available for distribution to

Howard and Stewart, whose net capital totaled $9,000; but $1,000 was due from Head. Howard and Stewart considered Head's debt to the partnership as a possible loss and divided it among themselves on the basis of their profit-and-loss ratio of 30 per cent and 

20 per cent, or 3/5 and 2/5 respectively. Howard received $2,800 in cash ($3,400 capital -$600 possible loss), and Stewart received $5,200 ($5,600 capital -$400 possible loss). The capital account of 

Howard now has a credit balance of $600; that of Stewart, a credit balance of $400. By distributing cash in this manner, the partners will absorb the $1,000 loss in their profit-and-loss ratio if they cannot collect from Head. Head's debit balance should not be closed into the capital accounts of the other partners until it is definitely found that collection from Head is impossible. If collection is made on July 10, the following entries, which are shown in journal form, will close the rest of the accounts in the ledger of the partnership:

Howard and Stewart.
