P. M. Stewart, Capital. 

Cash... 

To distribute the cash received from

Head according to the balances of the other capital accounts.

Liquidation in Install ments 1,000,00 1,000/00 600 00 400 00 1,000 00 When liquidation takes place over a long period of time, the partners may desire payments to be made as proceeds are received from liquidation sales. In this case the partners may be paid in installments. When an installment is distributed to the partners, care must be taken not to overpay a partner. A partner has been overpaid when, as a result of a payment, the balance of his capital account is not great enough to bear the possible future losses in liquidation. To avoid this result, the following procedure may be used: 

1. On working papers set up "T" accounts showing the balances of the partners' capital accounts. 

2. Assume that all assets other than cash will result in a total loss. 

3. Distribute this loss to the partners' capital accounts on the working papers, using the profit-and-loss ratio. 

4. Distribute any debit balances in the partners' capital accounts, resulting from the completion of Item 3, to the capital accounts that have credit balances. Use the profit-and-loss ratio. 

5. The credit balances left in the partners' capital accounts will now equal the cash on hand and will indicate the interest of each partner in the cash. By means of a journal entry, record in the books of account the distribution of the cash. 

The procedure outlined above is followed at the time each installment payment is to be made. After all the assets have been turned into cash, and the cash has been distributed to the partners, the actual loss resulting from the entire liquidation process is determined and is charged to the partners' capital accounts by means of a journal entry.

This procedure is based on the principle presented in the early chapters of this book: namely, that the equities of the owners

[Ch. 17 

ACCOUNTING PRINCIPLES 

are represented in the capital accounts of the owners. If one wishes to determine the equities of partners in cash resulting from liquidation, it is necessary to eliminate their equities in the other assets. This is accomplished by assuming a total loss on all assets other than cash and distributing this possible loss to the partners' capital accounts in the profit-and-loss ratio. 

To illustrate liquidation in installments, assume that the partnership of A, B, and C, who share profits and losses equally, is to be dissolved. The partnership balance sheet in condensed form, at the time of dissolution, is shown below. 

A, B, AND C

BALANCE SHEET 

MARCH 1, 19--

10,000 00

Proprietorship: 

A, Capital. 

1,000 00 

B, Capital. 

3,000/00

C, Capital. 

6,000 00 

10,000 00

Total Proprietorship.... || 10,000 00 

The assets are liquidated by several sales at various dates, and the cash received from the liquidation sales is distributed in the following installments: (1) $2,500, (2) $1,500, and (3) $4,500. As a result of the first liquidation sale $2,500 is to be distributed. The maximum possible loss on the assets other than cash is therefore $7,500. On working papers the capital accounts of the partners are set up in "T" account form, and the loss is distributed to the capital accounts in the following manner: (a) The total possible loss of $7,500 is charged to the partners' capital accounts in the profit-and-loss ratio. (b) A's deficit of $1,500 is charged to the capital accounts of B and C in the profit-and-loss ratio. (c) B's deficit of $250 is charged to the capital account of C. 

After these entries have been made in the "T" capital accounts, those accounts appear as follows on the working papers: Loss 2,500 1,000 

A, Deficit 1,500 Loss 

A, Deficit 2,500 3,000 

750 B, Deficit 250

The procedure just outlined assumes that losses are not known while assets are being liquidated. Whenever specific assets are sold and the loss on these assets is determined, this loss may, at that time, be charged in the profit-and-loss ratio to the partners' capital accounts. For example, if a building that is recorded in the accounts at a net value of $30,000 is sold for $15,000, the loss can be determined and may be charged at once to the capital accounts by debiting these accounts in the profit-and-loss ratio and crediting 

Loss or Gain on Sale of Assets for $15,000.

1. Armstrong and Douglas have equal interests in a business with a total capital of $30,000. They especially need the services of 

Jasper, who has had much experience in their kind of business. 

They therefore offer to sell him a one-fifth interest for $5,000, each partner contributing an equal amount of his proprietorship. Sinclair also wishes to join the firm; but as he is entirely without experience in this kind of business, and will therefore be of no great value to the firm for some time, he is willing to pay the two original partners $8,000 for a one-fifth interest in the business. (a) Give the entry required if an interest is sold to Jasper. (b) Give the entry required if an interest is sold to Sinclair.

1. W. A. Stevens and R. A. Patten are partners with capital investments of $15,000 and $10,000 respectively. They share profits and losses in proportion to capital investments. H. T. Elwood is to be admitted as a partner. (b) Elwood invests $20,000 and receives a one-half interest in the partnership. (Do not use goodwill.) (c) Elwood invests $20,000 and receives a one-third interest in the partnership. (Show goodwill.) (d) Elwood buys one half of the interest of each partner, paying 

Stevens $9,000 and Patten $6,000 respectively. (e) Elwood buys one third of Stevens' interest for $6,000 and one half of Patten's interest for $6,000. (f) Elwood invests $25,000 and receives a one-third interest in the partnership. (Use goodwill.)
